### [1] [Does the Trust Boundary Hold over Time? A Long-Horizon Benchmark for Receiver-Directed Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03724)
*Ting Xu, Henry Leung*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In receiver-directed federated learning, each site draws a trust boundary. For every source and model component it judges, on its own data, whether the transfer helps (admit), harms (reject) or cannot yet be told (abstain). We ask whether this boundary holds over hundreds of rounds. Our benchmark has a federation of thirteen semi-synthetic sites, an exact oracle for every evaluated transfer, paired seeds, an attack protocol, fourteen methods over 600 rounds and Qwen3-1.7B LoRA sites over 500. The answer depends on which boundary is measured. Across 20 paired CPU seeds, a gate judging against a fixed reference better identified transfers useful relative to the site's starting model. Yet it admitted 29% of transfers harmful to the model the site now has. A gate judging against the current model admitted 2% of harmful transfers on that target in the CPU tier, but missed many transfers useful to the starting model. Two short propositions explain these distinct failures. We also evaluate a held out audit that leaves gate verdicts intact. After training, it decides whether each final model is deployed or the site falls back to local training, and reports admissions that have gone stale. With independent audit data its guarantees hold after any training history. At 600 rounds the audit deployed 65 to 72% of models, none with negative benefit on its target, and kept 98% of the gain. No stale report had the wrong sign in our runs. With 20,000 audit observations, a variance-adaptive bound found 99% of stale transfers that cost at least 0.01 in log loss. A common-target Qwen3 comparison on 60 selected transfers shows the same tension over 100 rounds. With 38% adversarial sites, FedFomo and CS-Gate were the only evaluated methods with positive mean gain in every honest group.


### [2] [LoopKV: Loop-Aware KV Cache Compression for Serving Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04137)
*Tianle Zhou, Yang Zhang, Tianyu Wu, Zhuohan Wang, William Nixon, Juncheng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped transformers apply a shared block several times per token to improve accuracy without adding parameters. For models such as Ouro that keep a separate key-value (KV) cache per loop, cache memory limits the batch size that amortizes repeated weight reads. Uniform-bit quantization and fixed rules per loop do not allocate precision according to each layer's sensitivity in each loop. Measurements on Ouro show that quantization sensitivity varies across (loop, layer) cells, and the sum of individual cell damages approximates the overall output damage. Sharing damage also varies by cell, with later loops generally tolerating sharing better. Skipping low-attention values to save reads produces errors that compound across loops. LoopKV uses a one-time offline profile to choose 4-bit storage, 2-bit storage or reuse of the final loop's KV for each cell. The planner minimizes reads under budgets for predicted damage and storage. During decoding, each loop reads the high 2 bits of each 4-bit value and fetches the low bits only where its own attention weights require 4-bit precision. A single fused kernel in vLLM executes the plan. Serving Ouro-1.4B on one RTX PRO 6000, the memory-optimized LoopKV-M plan stores 35% less KV than uniform 4-bit quantization (KIVI-4) on the same fused kernel and CUDA graph. It sustains 1.7-2.2x KIVI-4's goodput under a latency service-level objective with a 16K-token maximum length. None of its averages on six public benchmarks falls significantly below BF16's.


### [3] [FedCSGA: Client Selection Based on Genetic Algorithm for Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04667)
*Ghani Haider, Majid Kundroo, Leo Zhang, Jinchul Choi, Taehong Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative training of machine learning models across distributed clients while preserving data privacy. In practical deployments, FL relies on selecting a subset of clients in each communication round to reduce communication cost, mitigate stragglers, and improve training efficiency under heterogeneous data and system conditions. In heterogeneous edge computing environments, both statistical heterogeneity and system heterogeneity significantly affect convergence efficiency and model performance. However, existing client selection methods often address these factors independently, overlooking their coupled and dynamic impact across communication rounds. To address this limitation, we propose FedCSGA, a Client Selection method for Federated learning based on a Genetic Algorithm. FedCSGA encodes client subsets as chromosomes and evaluates these subsets through a non-linear fitness function that jointly integrates resource-aware metrics and history-aware participation weighting. This design captures subset-level interactions, mitigates persistent selection bias, and balances statistical utility with system efficiency. Extensive experiments on FMNIST, CIFAR-10, CIFAR- 100, and Tiny-ImageNet demonstrate that FedCSGA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across diverse heterogeneity settings, achieving accuracy improvements of up to 7.0% and faster convergence with fewer communication rounds. These results validate the effectiveness of jointly modeling statistical and system factors to achieve robust and efficient learning in heterogeneous federated environments.


### [4] [Latent Cluster Separation as a Regularizer for Lifelong Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03652)
*Sabarish Radhakrishnan, Nathalie Japkowicz*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Lifelong (continual) anomaly detection systems must learn a sequence of tasks without forgetting how to recognize anomaly types seen earlier -- a property known as backward transfer. A common way to try to improve backward transfer is to reshape a model's internal representation around learnable concept centroids, typically coupled with a new way of scoring anomalies based on distance to those centroids. Because these two design choices are usually introduced together, it is unclear which one is actually responsible for any performance gain. In this paper, we unbundle the two mechanisms within a VAE-based lifelong anomaly detector and study them separately. We find that used purely as a training-time regularizer -- while leaving anomaly scoring unchanged -- latent cluster separation can improve detection quality and backward transfer, though whether it outperforms the alternative scoring approach depends on the dataset's underlying representation. Switching to distance-based scoring is instead where most of the instability commonly associated with this class of method actually originates; we identify and explain the underlying mechanism. This benefit depends on the dataset's underlying representation, and we report this as practical guidance for choosing between the two design choices, rather than as a list of caveats.


### [5] [Four Extensions of the Universal Law of Robustness: Log-Concave Covariates, Heterogeneous Mixtures, Vector-Valued Predictors, and Markovian Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03653)
*Tobias J. Peter*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Bubeck and Sellke's universal law of robustness says that fitting $n$ noisy samples below the noise level with a class of $p$ real parameters forces a Lipschitz constant of order $\sqrt{nd/p}$, when the covariate distribution on $\mathbb{R}^d$ is isoperimetric: smooth interpolation costs a factor $d$ of overparametrization. Its hypotheses are Gaussian-type concentration, a homogeneous covariate law, scalar outputs, and independent samples; we relax each one. Exponential concentration suffices, at the unchanged rate and absolute constant and at the price of a capacity burn-in, some form of which we show is necessary; this gives the law for normalized log-concave covariates, with a $\log d$ loss from the current KLS bound. For heterogeneous mixtures the worst component constant is replaced by the weighted average of the component constants, under a typicality condition on the mixture composition. For vector-valued predictors the bound at the Frobenius Lipschitz scale is free of the output dimension $K$, and the Euclidean bound loses only $K^{-1/2}$, a loss that covariate-independent noise removes. Stationary Markov covariates retain the i.i.d. rate, the pseudo-spectral gap entering only the sample-size conditions. One master theorem carries the shared argument, and constants are explicit throughout.


### [6] [Towards Automatic Heart Murmur Characterization from Multi-spot Phonocardiograms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03654)
*Marcelo Nogueira, Jorge H. Oliveira, Carlos G. Ferreira, Miguel T. Coimbra, Alípio M. Jorge*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Cardiac auscultation traditionally detects the presence of a heart murmur, but clinicians further characterize it by timing, shape, pitch, quality, and grade. Most automatic phonocardiogram analysis systems stop at binary detection and do not estimate this richer description. In this work, we formulate automatic murmur characterization as five patient-level classification tasks and compare three multi-spot strategies on recordings from the aortic, pulmonary, mitral, and tricuspid auscultation locations. First, classical and deep-learning classifiers are trained independently at each location, and predictions are combined via a maximum-severity late-fusion rule. Second, a synchronous multi-input CNN jointly processes one representative segment per location for a single task. Third, a unified multi-input, multi-output CNN predicts all five attributes from one shared multi-spot representation. Using 735 patients from the CirCor DigiScope dataset with complete four-spot recordings, the best late-fusion configurations reach an unweighted mean sensitivity between 85.56% and 93.81% across the five tasks, with class-specific recalls between 80.00% and 100.00% for every relevant category. The multi-input CNN trails late fusion by 7-16 points depending on the task, while the multi-output model reaches values between 72.59% and 79.40%, estimating all five attributes from one shared representation. We further show that naively balancing training data by adding all minority-class segments degrades patient-level performance under the maximum-severity rule, since it introduces non-representative, noisy segments not filtered by the clustering-based segment selection used for evaluation. These results indicate multi-spot murmur characterization is feasible with both fusion-based and jointly learned architectures, outlining a path toward automatic, structured murmur reports complementing binary screening.


### [7] [Mixture of Wide Experts: Sparse Routing for Interpretable Native Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03655)
*Tavish Mankash, Vardhaman Kalloli, Keshava Prasad, Deepan Muthirayan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Language models often spread a concept across many internal dimensions, making their features difficult to interpret. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) recover clearer features after training. We ask whether models can instead learn interpretable output coordinates directly. We introduce Mixture of Wide Experts (MoWE), a sparse feed-forward layer in which selected experts read the full representation but write to fixed, disjoint output coordinates. On a controlled task with known hidden causes, this design preserves predictive performance while localizing causes more clearly than dense and standard mixture-of-experts (MoE) models. At approximately matched compute, we train two 19-layer runs of each architecture for 10 billion tokens. MoWE achieves evaluation cross-entropy 2.417 versus 2.452 for dense, while standard MoE reaches 2.22. We measure native-coordinate readability by asking a language-model judge to describe each coordinate from passages where it is strongly active and test that description on new passages. Correcting for descriptions that also fit other coordinates gives a specificity score of 0.523 for MoWE, 0.375 for dense, and 0.268 for standard MoE. TopK SAEs trained on the same FFN updates score 0.489 for dense and 0.556 for MoWE, making native MoWE comparable to the dense SAE. In the first case study, we identify a four-coordinate causal path supporting the model's prediction of degrees after the prompt A right angle is 90. We then copy seven angle-related coordinate values into the model as it processes A rectangle has four, changing its next-token prediction from sides to degrees. In the second, one fixed coordinate set detects hazardous requests and another steers the next token toward refusal. These examples show that MoWE combines sparse conditional computation with coordinates that can be identified and reused directly.


### [8] [Stress-Testing the Robustness of LLM-Based Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03656)
*Ankitkumar Patel, Jigarkumar Patel, Amit Kumar, Supreetha Sreeram, Venkatesh Kulkarni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Why do some of the world's most powerful Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems still struggle to reason about cause and effect? Causal discovery, the ability to discern the underlying drivers of a system, is fundamental to scientific inquiry and has traditionally relied on structured data and statistical methods. Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have been explored as an alternative paradigm for causal inference, leveraging their broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities. Yet, despite their linguistic fluency, LLMs often rely on surface-level pattern recognition rather than genuine causal logic. While it is known that LLMs stumble in this domain, we lack a precise map of where, why, and how significantly these failures occur. In this paper, we propose a systematic stress-test framework across six critical dimensions: model scale, causal graph complexity, prompting strategy, metadata quality, data quality, and sensitivity to uncertainty. Our findings reveal a significant "optimism bias" in LLMs; models consistently overestimate causal links, leading to low precision across the board. Furthermore, we demonstrate that traditional evaluation metrics can be deceptive: while reasoning-heavy models lead in performance, the highest scores often occur when uncertainty in inference is overlooked. Once uncertainty is integrated into the evaluation, performance drops, providing a more honest reflection of causal reliability. We also find that, while scaling model size improves outcomes, these gains are fragile; performance degrades sharply as the quality of metadata decreases. This work serves as a practical guide to the blind spots of LLMs, offering a clear-eyed assessment of when these tools can be integrated into a causal discovery pipeline and when they are likely to lead researchers astray.


### [9] [Learning Directional Randers Geometry for Latent-Space Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03675)
*Théau Blanchard, Stéphanie Allassonnière*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep neural networks extract powerful latent representations from images, enabling fast and efficient downstream analysis. However, these latent spaces lack explicit geometric structure, leading to possibly poor interpolation fidelity and misleading extrapolation. Latent interpolation techniques assume symmetric transition costs which are often not the case for real world data. Their symmetric nature cannot capture directional processes, such as disease progression, where certain transitions are impossible (e.g. tumor growth dynamics or cognitive improvement in Alzheimer's disease). Although Riemannian metrics extend Euclidean geometry by incorporating curvature, they remain symmetric. We show that understanding asymmetry and not curvature alone directly addresses these problems. To do so we propose a new Finsler-Randers metric learning framework. It incorporates direction-dependent distances, thus enabling the computation of trajectories respecting monotonic sequential constraints. We demonstrate its potential for interpolation and extrapolation on controlled cross-sectional datasets.


### [10] [Auditing a Model-Hub Benchmark: Data Quality, Training Budgets, and Side Information in HuggingBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03727)
*Michal Podstawski*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Benchmarks built from model hubs combine interaction data, knowledge graphs (KGs) and resource documentation, but valid files do not ensure that these artifacts describe the same objects. Treating the release as a data product, we audit HuggingBench (SIGIR 2025), a benchmark for recommending Hugging Face resources, through data validation, a replication of its eleven recommendation methods and controlled experiments with three training seeds. In three of its four external KGs, the files pass key and range checks but violate the item-entity identity constraint on which KG recommenders rely. Correcting this join improves every KG-based method in every seed, by up to 73% in mean Recall@10, and consistently reverses ten of 27 comparisons between KG configurations. The protocol matters as well: the 100-epoch budget ends before early stopping in seven to eight of 29 validation-selected settings, and 21 of 23 such setting-seed pairs improve with a longer budget. The released item text, which no reported method uses, is the strongest signal: closed-form linear models with text and the item graph exceed the best reported Recall@10 by 18% while fitting in minutes on a CPU. Checks of other recommendation releases show that individually valid releases of one dataset can use incompatible identifiers. We release corrected files, mappings, validators and logs, and derive practices for preserving identity and provenance across the artifacts of benchmarks and model lakes.


### [11] [Minimal Superior Penalties: Characterising and Calibrating Decision-Aware Proper Scoring Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03734)
*Rouhollah Ahmadian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training a classifier means letting a validation metric decide which model is kept and when training stops. A natural requirement on that metric is superiority: every correctly classified forecast should score better than every incorrectly classified one, however close the underlying probabilities are. Neither the Brier score nor the logarithmic score satisfies this on its own. We show that superiority is governed by the separation between two endpoints -- the supremum of the score on correct predictions and the infimum on incorrect predictions -- and derive both in closed form for each score. Their difference is the infimal penalty, the greatest lower bound on the penalty needed to restore superiority. Prior work restores superiority with a penalty calibrated from the first endpoint alone; for the Brier base, our infimal penalty is smaller than that published penalty by a factor ranging from 4.00 at three classes to 2.11 at twenty. We further embed the published and infimal penalties in a one-parameter family and show that, under a single-valued tie convention, every member of the family is strictly proper. In a pre-specified two-dataset case study, mean selected-model macro-F1 rises with the penalty in the reported ten-class regimes, though not distinguishably from noise once seed/selection-mode nesting is accounted for; a fifth regime at forty-seven classes does not reproduce even this descriptive pattern. The intended no-superiority baseline is algebraically a rescaling of the family under test, so the experiment cannot attribute the observed pattern specifically to superiority. When superiority itself is the objective, any penalty strictly above the infimal value suffices; choosing a larger penalty is a separate calibration trade-off rather than a requirement for the guarantee.


### [12] [SSPE: Robust Sequential Staypoint Extraction in Degraded Trajectory Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03737)
*Yueyang Liu, Lance Kennedy, Andreas Züfle*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Extracting semantically meaningful staypoints from raw trajectory streams is a foundational prerequisite for mobility analytics. However, real-world trajectory data is frequently degraded by severe spatial noise and protracted temporal dropouts. Classic threshold-based extraction methods, such as the widely adopted sliding-window algorithm, are highly sensitive to these anomalies, routinely fracturing continuous stationary episodes into disjointed fragments. To overcome this limitation, we propose the Sequential Staypoint Extraction (SSPE) algorithm. SSPE employs a resilient, state-driven architecture that replaces static spatial anchors with a dynamic centroid and introduces an $n$-step lookahead heuristic to structurally tolerate transient coordinate drift. Concurrently, it utilizes temporal bridging mechanisms to prevent artificial fragmentation during prolonged signal loss. Since optimal extraction parameters vary significantly across datasets, we further introduce a fully unsupervised, consensus-based parameter estimation framework. This framework derives baseline statistical scales directly from the raw trajectory data and identifies the optimal parameter configuration by maximizing structural agreement, thereby eliminating the reliance on annotated ground truth. We evaluate SSPE on the ExtraSensory dataset, a real-world mobility corpus with manually verified ground-truth staypoints, against the sliding-window implementation of Trackintel, one of the most widely adopted extraction baselines in the field. Empirical results demonstrate that SSPE achieves superior accuracy, yielding higher F1-scores while simultaneously improving computational efficiency over this standard.


### [13] [RATSIFT: Resolution-Adaptive Transformer for Solar Irradiance Forecasting and Temporal Downscaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03738)
*Jesto Peter, Timothy M. Hansen, Hossein Moradi Rekabdarkolaee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate forecasting and temporal downscaling of solar irradiance is critical for reliable renewable energy integration and grid management. Traditional methods often struggle to capture rapid irradiance variability. In this work, we develop a Transformer-based architecture for simultaneous forecasting and temporal downscaling. The model takes input data at arbitrary resolutions and generates outputs at any resolutions represented within the training data, leveraging both global and local contextual information. To improve training efficiency, a genetic algorithm-based optimization framework is implemented to identify suitable model hyperparameters, while reducing the computational cost of exhaustive grid search. The effectiveness of the proposed model is shown by analyzing real data in Dallas, Texas. The proposed model achieved root mean square error (RMSE) values of 28.785, 29.544, 30.157, 32.132~W/m\(^2\) for 15-, 30-, 60-minute, and irregularly spaced global input and producing forecast at 5-minutes resolution, respectively. Furthermore, this model produced reliable uncertainty estimates, exhibiting wider prediction intervals for high-resolution inputs during volatile solar conditions, whereas coarser-resolution inputs result in narrower intervals and significantly lower training time and computational cost. Our approach provides a scalable and efficient framework for multi-resolution solar irradiance forecasting and temporal downscaling, suitable for practical energy forecasting and grid management applications.


### [14] [CaRe-KT: Continuity-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning for Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03744)
*Mehrnoush Mohammadi, Kamal Berahmand, Mohammad Morsali, Shazia Sadiq, Wojtek Tomaszewski, Hassan Khosravi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Knowledge tracing (KT) estimates learners' evolving knowledge states from interaction histories to support adaptive learning. Retrieval-augmented KT improves these estimates using cross-learner behavioral evidence, but existing approaches typically separate evidence retrieval from downstream prediction. This creates a retrieval-inference alignment gap because evidence relevance varies with the learner's evolving state and prediction context. We propose CaRe-KT, a query-adaptive framework that maintains relevance continuity between evidence acquisition and inference without equating retrieval relevance with predictive utility. CaRe-KT introduces an Evidence-Graph Cache (EGC) that combines persistent question-level relational compatibility with interaction-level temporal validity to construct query-specific evidence subgraphs. Historical learning trajectories retrieved through these subgraphs are encoded alongside the target learner's history in a shared representation space using outcome-supervised contrastive learning. An attention mechanism combines acquisition relevance with learned query-evidence compatibility, allowing relevance to inform, rather than determine, each trajectory's predictive influence. Experiments across five benchmarks show that CaRe-KT outperforms strong KT baselines and remains robust under sparse-history and cold-start conditions. These findings support relevance continuity as a design principle for retrieval-augmented knowledge tracing.


### [15] [Honest Small Decision Models for Finance: Scale, Specialization Collapse, and Mixed-Corpus Remediation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03755)
*Ajitabh Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Financial workflows need fast, calibrated, auditable decisions, not just generated text. We train System-1 decision models, small language models fine-tuned to answer typed questions (choice/score/noul) with a probability in a single forward pass, across two model families (K2-Horizon and Qwen3.5/3.8) and nine rungs from 0.8B to 32B parameters, using one frozen LoRA recipe on a public decision benchmark and two new finance corpora: a programmatic-gold policy corpus (rule-reading with exact labels) and a reality-labeled outcome corpus (19,640 realized forward-return questions in walk-forward splits, plus a sealed 2026 test of 1,360 questions). Narrow fine-tuning costs general decision competence 9-45 percentage points, a data-mixture effect that shrinks with model scale and is associated with narrow-gradient volume. On reality-labeled questions, every trained rung from 0.8B to 32B lands at the horizon-conditioned prior baseline (accuracy 0.37-0.38, no detectable conditional edge under our controls); the models respond with flat, well-calibrated probabilities that track per-horizon base rates: naturally honest uncertainty, with a mid-scale calibration sweet spot (ECE 0.026 at 2B, degrading to 0.043-0.044 at 9-32B under prior drift). A mixed-corpus remediation carries all competencies in one adapter at modest cost: it matches or slightly exceeds the generalist specialist on the public benchmark while holding the policy ceiling and near-specialist outcome calibration, yielding a deployable artifact with per-domain temperature regimes and a band-dispatch escalation analysis: sharp, dispatchable bands where rules determine answers; flat, review-routing bands where reality does not.


### [16] [Language Models Can Benefit from Domain-Specific Inductive Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03756)
*Damien Teney, Liangze Jiang, Zachary Shinnick, Hemanth Saratchandran, Simon Lucey*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Language models are typically trained with a single choice of architecture on diverse domains and data sources. Modern transformers have been extensively refined for such general-purpose learning, but are they equally effective across different subsets of the pretraining data? We investigate this question by training small language models from scratch on representative subsets of pretraining data such as web text, code, and mathematics. We introduce variants of transformers in which standard activation functions are replaced with learned splines tuned on specific datasets, providing a simple way to modify their inductive biases. Using a mix-and-match of architectures and datasets, we evaluate cross-domain transfer, i.e. how well does an architecture tuned for one domain perform on data from another? We find that the tuned transformer variants improve training efficiency or generalization on all seven of our tested datasets, but the gains are often domain-specific. For example, architectures tuned on a natural language dataset such as FineWeb perform well on TinyStories, whereas those tuned on mathematics perform poorly on natural language. We identify several mechanisms underlying these improvements, including wider loss basins and more abundant "lottery tickets". Our results show that different subsets of pretraining data can benefit from different learning mechanisms, highlighting a limitation of the prevailing trend towards a small set of general-purpose architectures and optimizers. This points to an opportunity to improve data efficiency by developing domain-specific learning strategies that retain the ability to train general-purpose models.


### [17] [The role of injected noise in diffusion model generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03764)
*Vincent Emmerling, Felix Günther, David Kappel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models trained on distinct datasets can produce highly similar samples when prompted with the same noise input, suggesting that they learn a generalized data distribution. We investigate the role of noise injected during the sampling process, and show that it plays an important role in making or breaking this strong generalization effect. Based on this finding we investigate the robustness of sample similarity to changes in the injected noise, and find that its impact is largest in the sampling regime where broad structure already exists but the precise identity of the sample is not yet determined.


### [18] [Quantifying out-of-distribution data severity with Wasserstein distances: benchmarking large language models for alloy property prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03767)
*YuJie Liu, Dezhen Xue, Jun Sun, Xiangdong Ding, Zhibin Gao, Turab Lookman*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs), such as Gemini and DeepSeek, exhibit increasingly advanced reasoning capabilities, a critical question arises: how do their property prediction abilities compare to those of domain-specific pretrained materials models? In this study, we evaluate predictions for Ti, steels, high-entropy alloy (HEA) and Al, demonstrating that general-purpose LLMs surprisingly outperform SteelBERT, MatSciBERT, and SciBERT. To further enhance performance, we introduce a hybrid approach that utilizes numerical priors from a prior model, such as CatBoost or SteelBERT, to guide the general LLMs (Deepseek V3.1, Gemini-2.5-Flash). By inputting these initial estimates from a prior model as prompts, the LLM acts as a reasoning corrector, achieving accuracy superior to the prior model and "unguided mode". Furthermore, we investigate the impact of data strategy using a similarity-based metric, revealing that data quality dominates quantity; using merely 5% of high-similarity reference data of training set as input yields performance similar to the results of using 100% of training set. Additionally, the LLMs demonstrate excellent uncertainty calibration, where self-reported "high" confidence strongly correlates with minimal deviation from ground truth. Our findings suggest that synergizing general-purpose LLMs with prior model numerical baselines and high-quality data curation creates a cost-effective and accurate paradigm for materials discovery, reducing the need for expensive domain-specific pretraining.


### [19] [Multi-layer Indexer: Overlapping Selection and Attention in DeepSeek Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03768)
*Serdarcan Dilbaz, Arjun Krishna, Kiran Kamble, Daniel M. Bikel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Sparse attention cuts the cost of long-context inference by making the attention matrix sparse: each query attends only a subset of the sequence, chosen by a fixed pattern or, in recent production LLMs, learned per query. In DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA), the attention of DeepSeek-V3.2 and the GLM-5 family, every layer is preceded by a lightning indexer that scores the whole cache and keeps the k=2048 highest-scoring tokens for the layer to attend. At long context this indexer scan, not the sparse attention it feeds, is where the decode time goes. But adjacent layers select nearly the same tokens, so a layer can attend the selection of the previous indexer layer instead of its own. This removes the dependency: each indexer then runs concurrently with the attention of its layer. The resulting Multi-layer Indexer (MLI) needs no retraining and no new weights. Its default realization changes no kernel, running inside SGLang's production decode graph; an optional fused decode kernel accelerates it further on DeepSeek-V3.2. MLI composes with IndexCache (the cross-layer reuse GLM-5.2 ships as IndexShare): IndexCache drops the indexer from some layers, and MLI hides the scan on the rest. We serve DeepSeek-V3.2 and GLM-5 under IndexCache's protocol at retentions 1/2 to 1/32 (the fraction of layers that keep an indexer) and contexts 10K to 195K. At equal retention the composition decodes faster than IndexCache: up to 14.5% faster on DeepSeek-V3.2 and 9.8% on GLM-5 at 1/2, and 7.9% and 5.1% at 1/4, largest where IndexCache keeps the most indexers and growing with context. At retention 1/r it is faster still than IndexCache at 1/(2r) while keeping twice the fresh selections: at 128K it reaches 1.32x the unmodified model's decode throughput at 1/4, above IndexCache's 1.28x at 1/8. Its LongBench, RULER and LongBench v2 accuracy stays within a few percentage points of IndexCache's.


### [20] [Probabilistic Steering of Generative Priors for Lab-in-the-Loop Antibody Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03844)
*Anisha Parsan, Sebastian Ober, Calvin McCarter, Nick Bhattacharya, Hunter Elliott*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Therapeutic antibody development is often framed as comprising two discrete stages: initial discovery of novel binders followed by iterative optimization to refine these hits into therapeutic-grade molecules. Models and methods often reflect these stages, with generative structure models applied to discovery and probabilistic black-box optimization methods applied to refinement. Here we propose to unify these stages by smoothly transitioning a generative discovery model into iterative optimization, allowing the handoff between the structure-based prior and supervision from wet lab data to be guided by the posterior uncertainty of a probabilistic surrogate. We show that this yields improvements in optimization efficiency in the low-N regime, as well as improved diversity amongst the optimized molecules.


### [21] [Dr. No: Feature Steering for Abstention in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03862)
*Farid Hasanov, Riccardo Renzulli, Bahram Zonooz, Mykola Pechenizkiy, Decebal Constantin Mocanu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large foundation models often answer even when the available visual or textual evidence is insufficient to support a reliable conclusion. This failure to abstain makes them hard to trust in high-stakes or information-seeking settings, where a confident but unsupported answer can be worse than no answer at all. It also raises a natural question: can the internal mechanisms behind this behaviour be identified and controlled at inference time? We present Dr. No, a feature-steering approach that mines sparse autoencoder (SAE) features associated with abstention and commitment, then modulates a small set of them during the prefill pass, without retraining the base model. Dr. No is evaluated on LLaVA-1.6 Mistral 7B across multimodal abstention benchmarks, including UNK-VQA and MM-UPD, and additionally benchmarked on language reasoning tasks using Qwen3.5-2B on AbstentionBench subsets. Against multiple uncertainty-based abstention baselines, feature steering achieves a more favourable balance between correct answering and appropriate abstention than post-hoc rejection methods. The gains are strongest on UNK-VQA and on MM-UPD cases involving image--question or question--answer incompatibility, and transfer most clearly to mathematical reasoning subsets of AbstentionBench. However, performance is more limited on absent-answer detection and retrieval-heavy multi-hop QA, such as MuSiQue. These results suggest that sparse internal features provide lightweight control handles for shaping uncertainty-aware behaviour in foundation models, while also showing that abstention control is source-dependent rather than governed by a single universal signal.


### [22] [ExaTune: Exhaustive Hyperparameter Landscape Exploration at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03867)
*Kevin Dick, Katie Lonsway*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) returns a single optimal configuration, systematically discarding the explored performance landscape that encodes how results depend on configuration choices. Fitness landscape analysis comprises a robust collection of methods from evolutionary computation offering ruggedness indices, autocorrelation functions, and fitness-distance correlation that have never been applied to machine learning hyperparameter spaces at scale, given that fine-grained ground-truth enumeration data has not existed. We present ExaTune, an open-source HPC system to produce a new artifact class: the hyperparameter landscape map. ExaTune exhaustively evaluates every point on a discretized hyperparameter grid via $K$-fold cross-validation, aggregates results into reproducible parquet benchmarks, and applies a suite of five formalized landscape metrics: spatial autocorrelation (Moran's $I$), ruggedness, local optima density, fitness-distance correlation (FDC), and variance-based parameter importance. We release a benchmark collection of 8 complete landscape maps (totalling 285,080 exhaustively evaluated configurations) spanning five model families (SVMs, random forests, XGBoost, ridge regression, and $k$-nearest neighbors) across six classification and two regression benchmarks, hosted on HuggingFace. Empirical analysis demonstrates that Bayesian optimization, Optuna, Hyperband, and Random Search at standard trial budgets systematically miss instability regions, secondary optima, and parameter interaction effects that are visible in complete landscapes; information that is structurally unavailable to any optimizer by design.


### [23] [Embedding Semantic Entropy: NLI-Free Semantic Uncertainty Estimation with Geometry-Aware Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03876)
*Akshiya Raveendran, Praveenasarma Baskarakurukkal, Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan, Kamal Premaratne, Uthayasanker Thayasivam*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often generate fluent yet incorrect responses, making un- certainty estimation important for reliable deployment. Semantic Entropy (SE) estimates uncertainty by clustering responses based on semantic equivalence, but it relies on hard entailment-based clustering. We propose Embedding Semantic Entropy, which replaces entailment-based clustering with K-Medoids in semantic embedding space and uses dynamic cluster selection to account for intra-cluster compactness and inter-cluster separation. We evaluate the method on TriviaQA, SQuAD, NQ-Open, SVAMP, and BioASQ using Falcon- 7B-Instruct, LLaMA-2-7B, LLaMA-2-7B-Chat, and Gemma-7B. Results show competitive AUROC and AUARC performance, suggesting that NLI-based entailment clustering can be replaced with embedding-space clustering with limited degradation while improving the inspectability of semantic grouping. This matters because semantic uncertainty scores depend on underlying semantic groups; by making these groups explicit, compact, and separable in embedding space, Emb-SE turns uncertainty estimation from a black-box en- tailment decision into an inspectable clustering problem.


### [24] [The Information Limits of Neural Controlled Differential Equations Under Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03887)
*Rostislav Gusev, Evgenia Romanenkova, Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural controlled differential equations (Neural CDEs) evolve hidden states along continuous paths constructed by interpolating discrete observations. Sparse measurements leave the input between observation times undetermined, and admissible continuous paths consistent with the same data can produce different terminal states. We characterize this ambiguity for planar drivers with bounded speed. The set of compatible terminal states admits a leading-order zonotopic approximation determined by feasible signed areas and transported Lie brackets. Its projection onto a scalar prediction yields a computable, target-specific ambiguity certificate. We also study the number of measurements needed to reduce the remaining uncertainty. For nondegenerate planar CDEs, the optimal worst-case radius over deterministic adaptive sampling policies scales as $N^{-1}$ when speed is bounded and $N^{-2}$ when both speed and acceleration are bounded, where $N$ is the number of observation intervals. Synthetic experiments assess the certificate using admissible continuous completions and examine the role of path regularity.


### [25] [When Latent Dynamics Meet Data Assimilation: A Benchmark of Koopman-Type Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03916)
*Zhiwei Zhang, Wenxuan Yuan, Xiaoyuan Cheng, Pouria Behnoudfar, Chuanqi Chen, Charlotte Moser, Yuta Tarumi, Jin-Long Wu, Rossella Arcucci, Takemasa Miyoshi, Nan Chen, Marc Bocquet, Sibo Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Koopman representations describe nonlinear dynamics through linear evolution in a transformed space, offering an attractive framework for their linear simplicity and generality. Yet these advantages do not necessarily translate into improved performance in deep latent data assimilation (LDA). We systematically compare seven representative methods and structural baselines across five systems spanning approximately periodic to strongly chaotic dynamics and a broad range of state dimensions. We evaluate forecast and assimilation accuracy, physical state recovery, latent-trajectory behavior, and dynamical consistency, and assess Gaussian error assumptions, objective convexity, and uniqueness of analysis solutions. Our results reveal a trade-off between representational flexibility and optimization tractability. Linear evolution of the latent state can stabilize short-term forecasts but restrict expressiveness in strongly chaotic and high-dimensional systems, without consistently improving assimilation accuracy. Parameterized linear operators offer greater flexibility, yet their joint optimization with the encoder--decoder remains nonconvex. Methods with closed-form analysis updates generally perform better and exhibit greater robustness in our experiments, although matrix inversion remains a computational bottleneck. These findings show that the practical benefits of Koopman structure depend on both sufficient observational information and the choice of assimilation procedure.


### [26] [Sequential auditing of connected forecast errors under partial observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03928)
*Yuanbo Zhou, Junjie Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph forecasting systems often rely on prediction intervals to quantify uncertainty across connected locations, but the true outcomes needed for evaluation may be only partially observed. We study how an auditor can selectively retrieve missing outcomes to determine whether there exists a connected group whose outcomes violate the same side of their respective forecast intervals. The audit stops when the observed labels determine the event or the query budget is exhausted. On three public graph time series, MOMS reduces mean queries by 19.6% to 38.5% relative to screened random acquisition when 30% of labels are hidden. Under localized masking on METR-LA, shortest witness finds more positive cases within ten queries, although the difference depends on node ordering. Direct component traversal reproduces all 9,696 screened random traces without enumerating connected subsets. The experiments show that reducing queries to a decision does not necessarily improve early event discovery or reduce local computation.


### [27] [Accelerating FastDEC Clustering Algorithm with GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03934)
*Mi Li, Eibe Frank, Bernhard Pfahringer*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Fast Dominance Estimation Clustering (FastDEC) is an efficient variant of Density Peak Clustering (DPC) that achieves high clustering quality through dominance-based density estimation, but remains constrained by the computational cost of exact k-nearest-neighbor (k-NN) construction and dominance component (DC) processing. We present SymWarp-FastDEC, a GPU-accelerated implementation of FastDEC. At its core is SymTrikNN, an exact self-k-NN kernel that exploits search symmetry using upper-triangular traversal and warp-cooperative top-k selection. We also introduce WP-DC, a warp-parallel DC reduction algorithm designed for modern GPUs. Combined with memory-adaptive execution planning and stream scheduling, WP-DC eliminates DC reduction as a runtime bottleneck. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that SymWarp-FastDEC produces clustering outputs identical to the original FastDEC on all evaluated equivalence benchmarks. Compared with a FastDEC variant using FAISS GpuIndexFlatL2 for exact k-NN search, our approach achieves approximately 1.7x to 4.9x higher end-to-end performance, with the advantage generally increasing with dimensionality. Compared with the original single-threaded Python FastDEC implementation, speedups reach approximately 2.5 x 10^4 in a very high-dimensional case where its k-d-tree-based search degrades; this extreme result is not representative of typical acceleration.


### [28] [P$^2$-MOPD: Accelerating Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation via Parameter Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03953)
*Sinuo Wang, Jiacheng Lu, Zeyu Zhang, Maojia Song, Rui Sun, Beidi Luan, Yutong Xie, Jing Li, Daxin Jiang, Qi Wu, Zuo Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation consolidates expert capabilities by supervising student-generated trajectories, whereas task arithmetic offers a training-free alternative through linear parameter combinations. We view the linear combination of student and expert parameters as a single differentiable model and propose P$^2$-MOPD, which jointly optimizes the mixing coefficients and the student weights under the same on-policy distillation objective. Unlike task arithmetic, which stays within the span of the frozen student and expert parameters for any choice of merging coefficients, P$^2$-MOPD keeps the student trainable, so the experts act as parameter priors without confining it to this span. Across four domains at 1.7B and 4B scales, P$^2$-MOPD achieves faster and more balanced recovery than MOPD from the same initialization, surpassing its final average recovery in half the training steps. At the end of training, our 1.7B model reaches 94.7% average recovery, versus 80.9% for MOPD.


### [29] [Forget What Comes Next: LLM Unlearning via Statistical Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03955)
*Andrii Shkabrii, Claudia Plant, Martin Pawelczyk*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can memorize and reproduce fragments of their training data, raising privacy and legal concerns that motivate machine unlearning. Yet poisoned training examples can remain statistically detectable after unlearning, which raises the question of whether current unlearning objectives remove what the model has memorized at all. In this work, we introduce PINT, a novel algorithm for machine unlearning that uses the Hilbert-Schmidt Independence Criterion (HSIC) to reduce the statistical dependence between tokens to be forgotten and the internal representations used to predict them. We formally relate our HSIC-based objective to Exact Memorization through a computable upper bound, connecting the training objective directly to the model's ability to reproduce the forgotten tokens. In extensive experiments, we compare forgetting-utility trade-offs across eight settings with models up to 7B parameters and demonstrate scalability to 70B parameter models. Our evaluations cover unlearning Gaussian poisons, personal information, hazardous knowledge in biology and cybersecurity, and copyrighted books and news articles. PINT achieves the strongest forgetting-utility trade-off on average and is the only method to make Gaussian poisons undetectable while maintaining near-original model utility. Code is available at: this https URL


### [30] [Sampling Interventional Distributions with Orthogonal MMD Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03962)
*Bariscan Bozkurt, Hugh Dance, Zonghao Chen, Alexandre Galashov, Arthur Gretton, Houssam Zenati*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Many causal inference problems require sampling from interventional outcome distributions rather than estimating low-dimensional summaries. Existing approaches often rely on fitting generative models of the target distribution together with additional nuisance components, which can be computationally demanding for high-dimensional outcomes. We propose Interventional Maximum Mean Discrepancy Flow (IMMD-Flow), a particle-based method that instead represents the target interventional distribution through its kernel mean embedding. This kernel embedding admits a doubly robust, Neyman-orthogonal estimator with finite-sample guarantees and can be efficiently reused to sample from the interventional distribution via MMD gradient flow. IMMD-Flow accommodates both hard and soft interventions, admits theoretical guarantees for estimation and particle approximation, and extends to high-dimensional image outcomes through frozen encoders. Across image-based benchmarks, IMMD-Flow matches or improves upon state-of-the-art generative approaches while showing significant speed advantages.


### [31] [Ermakov--Based embeddings as a parameter-free layer for physics-informed neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03964)
*Roberto Bernal-Jaquez, Roberto Romero Huerta, Alexander Schaum*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Evolution equations with time-dependent coefficients force a neural solver to spend capacity on a scale that the equation already determines. We show that a single classical object, the Ermakov-Pinney scale function $\rho$, can be written into the input layer of a physics-informed neural network as a parameter-free embedding. One construction covers three problem families through the two signs of the Pinney constant $c$: linear diffusion ($c=-1$, $\omega\equiv0$), diffusion in a time-dependent confining trap ($c=-1$, $\omega\neq0$), and the quantum oscillator with time-dependent frequency ($c=+1$). An exact moment identity gives $\rho$ in closed form from the initial data alone, so no auxiliary network or knowledge of the solution is needed. Our central finding is comparative: each family requires a different component of the construction and has a different honest control. In family I, the diffusive-time reparametrisation alone reproduces most of the gain, so that family is a poor test of the idea and shows why an ablation is mandatory. In family II no time reparametrisation exists, the control degrades accuracy, and the amplitude prefactor becomes essential (40.8x). In family III the adiabatic approximation also fails, and the quadratic phase factor is what matters (5.7x). The method does not help when the reduced solution does not converge to a fixed point: its extrapolation advantage then disappears. The reduction itself is the classical scaling-variable change of the self-similar-asymptotics literature; what is new is the moment identity that makes it architectural for time-dependent coefficients, and the comparative anatomy above.


### [32] [Stacking-Based Weighting for Large Language Bayes in M-Open Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03966)
*Anant Bhide, Akarsh Gupta, Edmond Cunningham, Justin Domke*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large Language Bayes (LLB) transforms a natural language problem description into a collection of candidate probabilistic programs and performs inference within each model, combining their predictions using Bayesian model averaging. While this provides a flexible framework for probabilistic inference without manual model specification, the resulting candidate model set is often misspecified in practice, and likelihood-based aggregation can concentrate on a single model, under-representing uncertainty across the sampled models. We propose a stacking-based aggregation method for LLB that selects model weights by optimizing predictive performance rather than marginal likelihood, while preserving the original language-model-driven model generation and within-model inference pipeline. Because leave-one-out predictive quantities are intractable in latent-variable models, we derive a variational lower bound that yields a tractable ELBO-based stacking objective. Across real-world probabilistic inference tasks, our method achieves comparable predictive performance to Bayesian model averaging while yielding more dispersed model weights, reducing concentration on a single candidate model under misspecification.


### [33] [One Mistake, Three Layers: Persistence Rules Under Depth Correlation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03968)
*Maryam Rahimimovassagh, Michael Brown, Sanjeda Sara Jennifer, Niloofar Yousefi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Probing frozen representations layer by layer is now standard practice, but reporting when a signal first becomes decodable requires a rule for reading a depth curve, and the obvious rule is wrong. On synthetic curves with known ground truth, requiring a probe score to exceed a null-calibrated threshold for three consecutive stages reports an onset nineteen stages early under transformer-like depth correlation, with every resample returning an onset. The failure follows from correlation between neighboring layers, a hazard known in neuroimaging but not, to our knowledge, checked for depth curves. We give an estimator whose error rate is calibrated rather than assumed. Null trajectories are built by permuting labels within each evaluation unit and holding each permutation fixed across depth, preserving the correlation structure the rule must survive; thresholds are per-layer order statistics; and the persistence requirement is calibrated rather than chosen. Applied to pocket information in a frozen co-folding trunk, stratifying 1,820 post-cutoff targets by protein-family and pocket familiarity, the five-stage rule fires on 4 to 7 per cent of null trajectories against a nominal 5 per cent and the three-stage rule on 12 to 17: the synthetic failure reproduces on real data. Pocket membership is decodable above threshold at all 48 blocks in all four regimes, so the onset cannot discriminate them; the pre-declared accumulation depth does, and is latest for familiar families with structurally novel pockets in every feature set. That regime is also the weakest, by a margin surviving controls for length, pocket measure and alignment depth. Separately, a widely used public co-folding checkpoint loads against its own code with 128 trunk parameters missing; those would be randomly initialized, and every representation extracted would be partly noise while appearing normal.


### [34] [Rapid Mean-Field Formation Transport with a Shared-Representation Neural Fokker-Planck Controller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03981)
*Zejian Zhou, Hao Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This paper develops a mean-field formation transport method with low online computational cost for large-scale multi-agent systems. Classical mean-field planners often require repeated solutions of coupled forward and backward partial differential equations, making online replanning computationally demanding. Density and control are therefore approximated by a common parameterized nonlinear map with separate scalar density and vector control outputs. Parameters are optimized using Fokker--Planck and initial-condition residuals, formation-matching costs on numerically simulated closed-loop particle trajectories, and control effort. Numerical computations are restricted to a bounded region of the state space, with an additional penalty on probability flux across its boundary. For the periodic-domain model, we establish well-posedness of the induced density evolution and bound the density-approximation error in terms of the dynamics and initial-condition residuals. Parameter optimization precedes online operation, during which controller coefficients remain fixed. Experiments quantify the accuracy--runtime trade-off relative to a mean-field planner, show lower transport error than classical feedback baselines, and demonstrate the benefits of the common parameterization.


### [35] [Physics-Informed Identification of Tensegrity Prestress across Taut-Slack Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04007)
*Sandesh Lamsal, Sumit Kumar, Rubi Bhandari, Subhamoy Sen, Andrzej Rutkiewicz, Leszek Małyszko, Landolf Rhode-Barbarigos*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Prestress governs the stability, stiffness, and dynamic behavior of tensegrity structures, but it is not directly observable from sparse structural measurements, and it may decrease during service life. Under strong excitation, the cables may slacken and the dynamics become nonsmooth. Existing identification methods are limited to the taut regime, and physics-informed networks for tensegrities solve only the forward problem of predicting the response from known member parameters. This study formulates the inverse problem. Neural networks are trained on the equation-of-motion residual in constrained Cartesian coordinates, where the mass matrix is constant and the force densities enter linearly. The force densities, damping, and axial stiffness are trainable parameters, and the tension-only cable law is kept. A Fisher-information analysis shows, before inversion, which member parameters a record can determine. The method is tested on simulated planar tensegrity frames with up to five modules and a three-dimensional prism, and on measured records of a biomimetic leg and a full-scale tensegrity simplex. On the one-module frame, the identifiable force densities are recovered within 1.5% from noisy accelerations at half the free coordinates, on records with and without taut-slack transitions. Records with transitions determine members that taut records leave undetermined. On the five-module frame, a 16-cable two-span bridge, the median force-density error rises moderately, to 4.2%. On measured records, an identified spring stiffness is within 0.1% of its independently measured value on average, and the identified simplex prestress at 13 levels agrees with its cable force transducers to 2.9% root-mean-square.


### [36] [Decision Transformer with Task-Aware Representations for Offline Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04010)
*Xinbo Zhao, Yuhang Liu, Xin Zhang, Lei Le, Zhaohan Xi, Yingxue Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: To enable effective generalization in offline meta-RL, it is essential to accurately infer the underlying task through latent task representations, which provide a compact and transferable summary of task-specific characteristics for guiding policy generation. In this paper, we propose Contrastive Task-Aware Decision Transformer (CoTA-DT), a DT-based offline meta-RL framework that learns task representations by jointly integrating four complementary components: (i) contrastive learning from offline trajectories, (ii) alignment with natural-language task descriptions, (iii) one-step supervision via a task-conditioned world model, and (iv) long-term supervision via a task-conditioned Q function. The learned task representations are used as conditioning signals for a DT-based policy, enabling task-aware meta-policy learning. To further support generalization in both zero-shot and few-shot settings, we design a language-grounded, self-adaptive strategy that enables effective and reliable adaptation to unseen tasks. Notably, in CoTA-DT, the pretrained LLM serves as a shared backbone for language encoding, world modeling, Q-function estimation, and policy learning. This unified design facilitates parameter sharing, improving both efficiency and generalization. Extensive experiments across diverse offline meta-RL benchmarks demonstrate that CoTA-DT consistently outperforms prior methods, particularly in challenging zero-shot generalization scenarios.


### [37] [Denoising Is Stein: Moment Access and a Resource Hierarchy for Learning Hidden Structure in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04043)
*Rui Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study score learning from denoising pairs when the data contain a hidden non-Gaussian component. If covariance reveals the component, its subspace can be recovered directly. If covariance is isotropic, the first distinguishing moment has order $k^\ast$, and the learner must extract that moment from clean points and their noisy views. Correlations in the denoising loss are Stein discrepancies against a baseline; for polynomial fields they determine exactly the corresponding moment differences. Averaging fresh noisy views conditional on a clean point gives an exact moment query on the clean distribution. In the covariance-matched regime, orientation-independent dictionaries satisfying an $L^4/L^2$ condition need order $d^{k^\ast-1}$ features, and the stated dot-product kernel ridge models need the same power in samples. Small-step online SGD on one unit has this exponent up to logarithms. A two-stage learner uses the curvature of a smoothed denoising loss to recover the hidden direction, then sharpens it and fits a scalar readout. For every even $k^\ast$, it obtains a fixed score gain over the Gaussian baseline from $O(d^{k^\ast/2}\log d)$ clean points. In the covariance-visible regime, the frozen-feature threshold occurs at $d^J/J!$, while projection onto principal components makes the fitted-model, score-risk and generated-law distributions independent of ambient dimension. In the covariance-matched experiments, the measured partial-trace overlaps are consistent with the proposed spectral transition. A width-8 network trained with clean-point reuse shows a $d^2$-like training-set threshold over $d=64$--$192$, with fixed validation and calibration overhead.


### [38] [Structured Priors: Towards an algebra of component priors for prior-data fitted networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04056)
*Thomas Bartz-Beielstein*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks are typically trained using synthetic data generated by a single, manually constructed generator. In cases where such a generator combines multiple components, e.g., in TabPFN, Mitra, or TabICLv2, this is achieved through a mixture using manually specified weights, the impact of which on the network only becomes apparent after pre-training. Instead of such a hand-built generator, this work proposes a structured prior: a small set of named component priors, linked by operators whose algebraic properties are formally proven and whose influence on the trained network has been validated. Within a measure-theoretic framework, the operators of mixture, convolution, amplitude scaling, local transform, and projection are defined. Their governing laws are proven. These operators are implemented in the macl2l software package. Experiments demonstrate that the implemented generators reproduce the identities within the resolution of a permutation test. Furthermore, it is shown that trained networks obey the distributive law; that a mixed prior composed of small components performs as well as or better than any individual component in seven out of eight open regression benchmarks; and that on two of these tables a structured prior reaches, with a ninth of the data budget, an error that a generic random-function prior does not reach even with the full budget. A theorem on model averaging demonstrates that networks trained on individual components constitute the Bayes predictor for the mixture, provided that the component networks are themselves the Bayes predictors for their respective priors. However, the pre-registered test of this averaged predictor with trained component networks failed, because the networks identify the generating component too rarely. The paper proposes a research program focused on the algebra of structured priors for prior-data fitted networks.


### [39] [StreamLTT: Risk Certification for Policy Selection under Stream Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04062)
*Jinwen Xu, Qin Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learn-then-Test (LTT) provides a general framework for certifying deployment policies against a prescribed risk target, but its standard formulation targets a fixed population risk estimated from calibration losses. In sequential deployment, losses may instead be dependent and nonstationary, so the risk of a fixed policy can evolve with the observed history. We introduce StreamLTT, which replaces this fixed population risk with average predictable risk, defined by conditioning each decision's loss on the information available before its outcome is revealed. Conditional centering turns empirical deviations from this target into a martingale sum, enabling both a test that adapts to predictable variance and a finite-sample Hoeffding alternative. Combined with family-wise error rate control, StreamLTT yields a simultaneously certified policy region that supports subsequent deployment selection. Across seven forecasting datasets and seven deployment risks, StreamLTT places the certified frontier substantially closer to the prescribed risk target than IID, robust-variance, block-bootstrap, and concentration-based alternatives, while empirical family-wise error closely follows the prescribed level. A finite-sample ablation further shows that the predictable-risk formulation drives the tighter risk frontier, while adaptation to predictable variance makes more effective use of the available family-wise error budget. StreamLTT therefore extends risk-certified policy selection to dependent deployment streams by addressing both what risk should be certified and how it should be tested.


### [40] [TB-Transolver: Parallelized and Coherent Long-Horizon Rollouts via Temporal Bundling for Time-Dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04063)
*Sidharth S. Menon, Ameya D. Jagtap*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Transolver has recently gained widespread adoption for solving diverse physical problems on unstructured geometric meshes by leveraging its Physics-Attention mechanism, which seamlessly extends standard softmax attention to handle physical domains at scale. For time-dependent problems, existing literature relies on time-conditioned or autoregressive rollouts to preserve temporal causality while retaining Physics-Attention as the spatial backbone. However, a fundamental limitation of this formulation is its sequential execution model, wherein computational costs scale linearly with the temporal horizon, rendering standard Transolver inefficient for transient problems despite its exceptional spatial efficacy. This bottleneck has historically hindered its broader application in time-dependent physical modeling. To address this limitation, we introduce TB-Transolver (also referred to as Temporally Bundled Transolver), an efficient framework built on temporal bundling that scales Transolver for time-dependent PDEs. Furthermore, we propose \emph{spatiotemporal coherence} (${\rm C(K)}$) as a general metric to systematically quantify snapshot correlation within a temporal bundle, enabling the \emph{a priori} selection of optimal bundle widths using training data alone, thereby avoiding expensive grid searches. We demonstrate the computational efficacy of TB-Transolver across four physically distinct domains, namely, plasticity, fluid dynamics, reaction-diffusion, and global weather forecasting; achieving training speedups of up to $29\times$ over conventional autoregressive and time-conditioned baselines. Finally, these computational gains are further amplified through distributed multi-node and multi-GPU parallelism, yielding aggregated speedups of up to $45.6\times$ at optimal bundle widths for systems exhibiting high spatiotemporal coherence compared to single-GPU implementations.


### [41] [Graph-Localized Coverage for Conformal Prediction on Inductive Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04066)
*Jinwen Xu, Qin Lu, Georgios B. Giannakis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) provides marginal coverage guarantees, but marginal coverage does not determine where prediction errors occur on a graph. Queries from different graph neighborhoods can exhibit substantially different levels of uncertainty even when aggregate coverage remains near its target. We introduce GLocalCP, an online CP framework for inductive node classification and edge prediction that combines marginal and graph-local calibration with uncertainty-adaptive prediction sets. Specifically, GLocalCP puts forth a novel uncertainty-adaptive nonconformality score that relies on the mutual information obtained from Monte-Carlo (MC) dropout based prediction. Then, it augments a global online threshold with a kernel-based local correction that propagates coverage feedback among similar graph queries. On the theoretical horizon, we establish pathwise $O(N^{-1/2})$ marginal calibration error without exchangeability or stationarity and, for the exact-kernel formulation, an explicit $O(N^{-1/2})$ graph-localized calibration guarantee. Experiments on seven node-classification and seven edge-prediction benchmarks show that similar marginal coverage can conceal substantially different local reliability. Our proposed GLocalCP maintains coverage near the target while consistently reducing graph-local calibration error. Uncertainty scaling in GLocalCP further reduces prediction-set size across all evaluated node and edge datasets while leaving marginal coverage essentially unchanged.


### [42] [Finite-Sample Convergence in Networked Average Reward MARL: Decentralization Pitfalls and Entropy Remedies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04072)
*Yizhou Zhang, Yashaswini Murthy, Laixi Shi, Adam Vierman*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the problem of average reward Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) where agents interact within a network. Each agent in the network conducts local updates with information within its $k$-hop neighborhood and collaboratively maximizes the overall reward of the entire network. We first provide impossibility results in achieving convergence to the global optimal joint policies in our setting. These impossibility results highlight the challenges of decentralized learning with local information in multi-agent systems, namely the issues of multiagency, where each agent independently optimizes its policy, and partial observability, where agents have limited visibility into the global state. Given these challenges which indicate that decentralized policy optimization can generally be certified only up to stationarity, we provide finite-sample convergence guarantees for a decentralized actor-critic algorithm with linear function approximation that converges to an approximate stationary point with small gradient norm. To overcome such impossibility results, we further show that adding fixed-state entropy regularization reshapes the objective landscape which leads to even stronger global convergence guarantees for our proposed actor-critic algorithm.


### [43] [Computer-Use Agents Gain Little Benefit from a World Model When Conditioned on Tutorials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04073)
*Anh Nguyen, Tianyi Xu, Phat Tran, Harry Yu, Huazheng Wang, Stefan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work has improved computer-use agents (CUAs) by combining retrieved task-relevant tutorials and look-ahead planning using world models with MLLM judges. However, the contribution of each component -- tutorials, the judge, and the world model -- to overall improvement has not been evaluated in isolation. To fill this gap, we first construct OSWorld-Tutorial and ScienceBoard-Tutorial: two datasets for CUAs with curated multimodal tutorials, covering everyday and scientific domains. We then propose RAG-PolicyJudge (RAG-PJ), a policy-judge framework for CUAs conditioned on task-specific tutorials: the policy proposes candidate actions, and the judge selects among them. To isolate the effect of world models given tutorials, we compare RAG-PJ with and without common world models: using either zero-shot text-based descriptions of next states or an oracle that returns ground-truth visual observations. We find that RAG-PJ gains little benefit from these rollouts when tutorials are present, even with perfect visual futures. This suggests prior world-model gains in tutorial-conditioned methods come mainly from tutorial understanding and the judge's ability to select the proposed actions, rather than from predicted rollouts. Error analysis reveals that the bottleneck is not rollout fidelity, but the judge's ability to reason over future states and recognize task completion. This highlights the need to improve not just next-state prediction in world models, but also reasoning over their predicted rollouts. Datasets and code are available at this https URL .


### [44] [RAPID: Increasing Efficiency of Internal Distillation via Randomized Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04081)
*Ryan Swift, Konstantinos Psounis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Knowledge distillation is a technique used to transfer the expertise of large teacher models into small, efficient student models. Recent advances have integrated the distillation of internal activations, more directly training students to learn similar representations as the teacher. One of the core challenges of internal distillation is the dimensional incompatibility between the student and teacher internal representations. Existing methods add learnable matrices to project the larger teacher activations into the smaller representation space of the student. This is a notable point of inefficiency, as learning additional parameters for the projectors increases training costs, only for them to be discarded immediately after training. We introduce RAPID (RAndomized Projection for Internal Distillation) a novel distillation methodology which eliminates the wasteful projection learning by leveraging randomized methods. RAPID utilizes an efficient transform construction based on the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma to compress the teacher representations into the student embedding space with strong theoretical guarantees of structural preservation. RAPID also employs a combination of exponential moving average (EMA) and homoscedastic uncertainty weighting in a novel multi-objective function designed for internal distillation. Our experiments in post-training for reasoning demonstrate that RAPID increases training efficiency by as much as 21.1% while maintaining performance comparable to existing methods.


### [45] [Follow the Features: SAE Feature-Guided Data-Efficient Instruction-Tuning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04085)
*Ryan Swift, Konstantinos Psounis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Selecting small, effective instruction-tuning datasets requires identifying which aspects of the original data to retain. We introduce Follow the Features (FtF), a data-selection method that uses sparse autoencoder (SAE) features to characterize training data and guide subset construction. FtF ranks features using dataset-level activation statistics, scores samples by their expression of highly ranked features, and incorporates feature resampling to encourage diversity. The LLM-SAE pair used for selection is independent of the model being fine-tuned, enabling reuse across target models. We evaluate FtF on two model-dataset settings at a training budget of 100 samples with a fixed sequence length of 256 tokens. FtF achieves performance competitive with GRAPE and S2L, with relative rankings varying across settings. On Qwen 3 8B trained on Alpaca, FtF exceeds full-dataset training by 1.59 percentage points in the harmonic mean of an AlpacaEval-based win rate and Open LLM Leaderboard performance, although full-dataset training retains a higher Leaderboard score. These results support SAE feature statistics as a useful signal for data-efficient instruction tuning.


### [46] [ScaleTraj: Scale-Decomposed Human Trajectory Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04097)
*Yulin Zeng, Yiheng Zhao, Jun Yan, Yuhong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Individual-level trajectories are essential for applications that rely on user-level mobility behavior. Existing mobility-generation approaches face a fundamental tension between individual-level and population-level statistical fidelity: top-down models reproduce aggregate mobility statistics but struggle to guarantee realistic individual trajectories, while bottom-up generators synthesize individual trajectories yet often fail to preserve key population-level mobility regularities after aggregation. In particular, many bottom-up generated populations fail to reproduce the displacement distribution, whose heavy tail is well summarized by a truncated power law. We formalize this phenomenon as the \textbf{Population-Level Displacement Gap}. To investigate its prevalence, we conduct a systematic evaluation of representative model-driven and data-driven trajectory generators. Our results provide evidence that the Population-Level Displacement Gap is prevalent across diverse generation paradigms. To diagnose this gap, we introduce the \textbf{Scale Fidelity Profile (SFP)}, a scale-decomposed metric that quantifies discrepancies in trip allocations across spatial scales. SFP indicates that population-level displacement mismatches co-occur with systematic errors in how trips are allocated across spatial scales. Motivated by this observation, we develop \textbf{ScaleTraj}, an autoregressive Transformer that decomposes location prediction into a Scale Selection Head that decides the movement scale and a Scale-Aware Cell Selection Head that predicts the destination conditioned on the selected scale. Experiments on two datasets show that ScaleTraj reduces the Population-Level Displacement Gap versus model-driven and data-driven baselines, while preserving fidelity. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [47] [When Strong Reasoners Join the Pool: Small Union Premiums and Costly Runaway Generations in LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04098)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Routing benchmarks often judge a router against a hindsight oracle that counts a query as solved whenever any model's recorded answer is correct, but under stochastic decoding that oracle mixes two different objects: the clairvoyant ceiling of committing to one model per query and the premium of calling several models and keeping any correct answer. A companion study measured this premium at 3.5 percentage points (pp) for 11 small open models on MATH-500; how large it is once current reasoning models enter the pool was unknown.
