We measure it with a fixed-protocol, pre-committed generation on a single NVIDIA B300: all 500 MATH-500 problems, 30 seeded samples per problem, Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and NVIDIA's Nemotron Nano 9B v2 and Nemotron Super 49B v1.5 (a NAS derivative of the same 70B) under vendor sampling, a 65,536-token cap, a pinned Math-Verify scorer, and an append-only attempt ledger, for 45,000 scored responses. The two reasoning models both reach 96.1% accuracy against 76.2% for the 70B baseline. The empirical product-coupling premium is 0.215-0.588 pp across all two- and three-model pools; the empirical plug-in selection headroom over always calling Nano is 1.27 pp; and resampling Nano three times gives practically the same empirical any-correct rate as calling all three models once. Only 31 responses reached the cap, 25 of them with exactly periodic tails, yet the capped responses account for up to 60.2% of a model's output-length-squared proxy, and long generations made a fourth planned model, Llama-3.1-8B, too slow to finish. The premium is far below the companion's 3.5 pp, but models, pool size and protocol changed together; an oracle gap should therefore be reported with the pool, the protocol, the samples per cell and the decoding budget that produced it.


### [48] [ModelBlaster: Closing the Performance Gap through Learning-Augmented Agents Across Models, Kernels and Hardware Backends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04104)
*Shengjun Kris Dong, Dima Nikiforov, Sahil Modi, Sana Damani, Edward C Lin, Siva Kumar Sastry Hari, Christos Kozyrakis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Optimizing accelerator code across hardware architectures is difficult. ML compilers search predefined spaces of schedules and micro-kernels, while LLM-guided optimization can explore alternative algorithms, operator decompositions, and hardware-specific techniques. However, existing LLM agents typically target a single programming interface, toolchain, or accelerator family, focus on kernel-level optimization without model-level transforms, and offer limited reuse of experience across hardware targets. We present ModelBlaster, a Memory-Augmented In-Context Reinforcement Learning (MAIC-RL) framework for optimizing data-parallel kernels and whole models across heterogeneous SIMD and SIMT accelerators. Its mutable policy is a persistent knowledge base updated at inference time by Actor-Critic Textual Gradient Descent (AC-TGD). The knowledge base maps performance states to actions, outcomes, and constraints, enabling experience reuse across kernels, models, and hardware targets. A model-level optimizer uses kernel-level knowledge to inform whole-model decisions. The same optimization loop and knowledge schema support CUDA on NVIDIA GPUs, OpenCL on Qualcomm GPUs, and RVV intrinsics on RISC-V vector cores, with only lowering and measurement layers specific to each target. Compared with PyTorch, ModelBlaster achieves geometric-mean speedups of 1.85x, 5.58x, and 5.18x on KernelBench Levels 1, 2, and 3, and 1.69x on SOL-ExecBench Levels 1 and 2 on a B200 GPU. On Qualcomm Adreno GPUs, it achieves 5.8x over the vendor SDK and 1.7x over Apache TVM; on RISC-V vector cores, it achieves 2.7x over ExecuTorch with XNNPACK across 70 KernelBench problems.


### [49] [Structure, Dimensionality, and Distribution Shape - What Makes Pretraining Data Transferable, and Why the Input Pipeline Decides Which](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04106)
*Sangyoon Bae, Jiook Cha*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: What makes a pretraining corpus transferable? We describe a corpus by three properties: its structure, its dimensionality, and the distribution shape of each unit's values. We propose the Transfer-Invariance Principle: the same property can be irrelevant to transfer under one input transform and decisive under another, so the model's input transform, not the data modality, decides which properties can matter. To test it we build TransferForge, a generator that places a synthetic population at any prescribed structure, dimensionality, and distribution shape. KernelSynth, the synthetic prior of Chronos, cannot, because it draws independent series. When the model standardizes each input window (PatchTST on UCR), the distribution shape is inert and the structural setting governs. Synthetic pretraining then matches a real corpus of the same size and exceeds it once scaled. When the model encodes calcium fluorescence signals without normalization (POYO Plus), the shape becomes a governing constraint, and a shape-matched synthetic corpus replaces real pretraining with zero real neural activity. Flipping only the input normalization, with the model and data fixed, changes the effect of a shape manipulation by a factor of 5.3, so the pipeline, not the modality, sets the switch. The principle yields a recipe that chooses what to synthesize before any training. Pretraining data then becomes something to manufacture for the model, most valuably where real data is scarce, expensive, or private.


### [50] [Separating Common and Unique Directions for Data-Free Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04108)
*Shiting Wang, Yingjie Zou, Ke li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models without additional training, and arithmetic methods have emerged as a simple yet effective way to merge models without data. However, existing arithmetic methods treat task vectors in parameter space and apply a single element-wise rule to directions that can differ in mergeability. In a shared directional basis, we find a consistent two-regime structure: a low-dispersion common bulk shared across tasks and a high-dispersion task-dominated tail that carries disproportionate energy. These findings motivate Common--Unique Subspace Decomposition (CUSD), a data-free pre-merging method that separates task vectors into common and unique directional components, merges the common components with downstream arithmetic operator, and retains unique residuals separately. Experiments across $11$ base models, four model families, and four arithmetic-based merging operators show that CUSD improves 42 of 44 model--operator combinations and achieves up to 22.5% improvement over the corresponding baselines. Our code is publicly available at this https URL


### [51] [Decomposing Rank Reversals under Temporal Nonstationarity: Alignment, Aggressiveness, and Instability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04116)
*Deying Song, Xueying Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Under temporal nonstationarity, model performance rankings reverse across training horizons due to heterogeneous sensitivities to memory. While existing literature addresses adaptive lookbacks, effective model complexity, and empirical forecast evaluation in isolation, no unified framework explains the structural mechanics driving these rank reversals. We bridge this gap by deriving a model-agnostic decomposition of out-of-sample $R^2$ into target alignment, miscalibrated aggressiveness, and predictive instability. Within this framework, we mathematically characterize complexity phase boundaries, establish closed-form optimal-memory equations for ridge regression under local drift, and extend these guarantees to complex model architectures via surrogate bounds. We validate these theoretical dynamics by benchmarking 14 linear, tree-based, and neural architectures across large-scale financial panels. In doing so, our framework demonstrates that empirical model rank reversals are driven by predictable surges in miscalibrated aggressiveness, providing a rigorous mechanistic diagnosis of generalization under temporal shift.


### [52] [RGDM: Robust Graph Diffusion Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04146)
*Yancheng Wang, Ping Li, Dongfang Sun, Yingzhen Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models represent a powerful class of generative models known for their solid theoretical foundations and remarkable performance across diverse tasks and domains. Existing node level diffusion methods synthesize nodes and edges within a graph, but are not designed to learn a robust generator from adversarially corrupted graphs. In this study, we introduce the Robust Graph Diffusion Model (RGDM), designed to generate labeled synthetic graph structures consisting of nodes and edges that integrate seamlessly into a given graph. The RGDM consists of a Robust Graph Autoencoder (RGAE) and a Latent Diffusion Model (LDM). Leveraging an edge selection mechanism and an innovative low-rank regularization on the latent feature, the RGDM produces clean and high-quality synthetic graph structures, even when trained on graphs subject to adversarial attacks. Comprehensive experimental evaluations reveal that Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) trained on the augmented graph, which is formed by merging the original attacked graph with the synthetic graph structures, exhibit significantly improved robustness against various graph adversarial attacks in the context of semi-supervised node classification. The code of the RGDM is available at this https URL .


### [53] [On the Feature Evolution of Temporal Difference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04158)
*Shangzhe Li, Dechen Zhang, Difan Zou, Weitong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Temporal-difference (TD) learning trains neural representations using bootstrapped targets generated by evolving value estimates, yet how predictive features emerge and propagate under incomplete neural updates remains poorly understood. We investigate this problem using finite-width two-layer ReLU critics on a constructed family of smooth stochastic horizon-$H$ MDPs with unknown quadratic structure and $d$-dimensional observations. We first show that an informative TD update acquires the quadratic Bellman signal and yields a representation admitting a bounded predictive readout. The simultaneous hidden-weight and head update achieves a strict finite-sample prediction-risk improvement over matched head-only SGD, with a $2(d+1)$-fold larger population quadratic response. We then establish that an imperfect learned continuation can already provide informative supervision to an earlier zero-reward stage. By tracking coupled neural updates with one SGD step per target copy, without requiring intermediate Bellman regressions to be solved, we obtain a uniform prediction-error transient $\exp[-\Omega(K/(Hd))]$, up to action-independent shifts and explicit sampling and finite-width errors. On the constructed signed-action MDP family whose total reward is bounded by 1, we show that an exploration-then-commit algorithm can achieve $\widetilde{O}(d^3H^{1/3}T^{2/3})$ regret over $T$ episodes, using $K=\widetilde{O}(Hd)$ global updates. Our analysis connects bootstrapped feature acquisition, concurrent neural refinement, and finite-budget policy performance.


### [54] [Say the Same, Act Differently: Diagnosing and Steering Text-Action Divergence in Reasoning VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04166)
*Zihao Feng, Qingzhao Zhang, Chunyu Xia, Bo Yu, Z. Morley Mao, Ramesh Govindan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reasoning vision-language-action (VLA) models pair physical control with natural-language rationales that supervisors can read to monitor embodied safety. Prior attacks on VLA reasoning corrupt the rationale itself and leave visible evidence in the text. We show that visual perturbations can instead alter the physical action while keeping the generated rationale word-for-word identical. At the shared text/action interface of Alpamayo for driving and InstructVLA for manipulation, our text-neutral action-subspace (TNAS) diagnostic shows that the locally most action-sensitive direction is nearly orthogonal to the active text-loss gradients, with 1.87% and 5.28% norm overlap. At equal hidden-state perturbation norms across both models, interventions along this text-neutral residual shift actions 4.64x to 47.8x more than text-gradient perturbations, whereas text-gradient perturbations induce 21.2x to 277x more text drift. Steering visual perturbations along this direction reaches the same decoupling from the image. Across 154 obstacle-annotated driving scenes in Alpamayo, TNAS-guided attacks induce a new collision under a word-for-word identical rationale in 52.6% of scenes under full-image perturbations and 35.1% under a localized forward-camera patch, compared with 4.55% for unguided action attacks. Relative to a text-preserving baseline, TNAS guidance raises exact rationale preservation from 83.0% to 89.5% and cuts teacher-forced text drift by 68.4% at a comparable collision rate. In InstructVLA, TNAS guidance cuts text drift by 40.5% and preserves the exact response on 85.0% of examples alongside a 1.73-unit action-chunk shift. An unchanged rationale therefore cannot certify a safe action, so monitors for reasoning VLAs must verify the predicted action channel directly.


### [55] [What Does 4-Bit Microscaling Cost a Wearable Activity Recognizer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04185)
*Megan Healy, Hossein Valavi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Wearable sports sensors can recognize actions on the device itself, where lower-precision numbers reduce model storage and memory traffic. Two 4-bit block-scaled formats, MXFP4 from the Open Compute Project, introduced in 2023, and NVFP4 from NVIDIA, introduced in 2025, are drawing interest. Published evaluations cover general deep-learning benchmarks and large language models; to our knowledge, nobody has yet measured what they cost a small wearable-sensor model. We train a 159,492-parameter 1D CNN to recognize four basketball actions from wrist-accelerometer data in Hang-Time HAR and test it on players it has never seen, using five-fold cross-validation grouped by player. Every comparison between number formats is made fold by fold. In full precision the model reaches 69.26\% macro-F1. Measured TFLite FP16 and INT8 models lose at most 0.16 percentage points. In simulation, 4-bit weights cost 1.45 points with MXFP4 and 0.72 points with NVFP4. When activations are quantized to 4 bits as well, MXFP4 loses 5.14 points and NVFP4 only 0.75, and NVFP4 comes out ahead in all five held-out-player folds. The ranking survives two alternative simulator settings, with NVFP4 leading by 3.49 to 4.40 points. A $2 \times 3$ ablation with 4-bit weights and activations (two block sizes and three scale rules) points to scale selection as the main source of MXFP4's loss. The reference MXFP4 conversion we simulate rounds the scale down, which can pick scales that are too small, so many blocks clip their largest values. Rounding the scale up recovers 3.18 points with 32-value blocks and 3.63 points with 16-value blocks, and switching to an 8-bit floating-point scale, as NVFP4 does, adds another 0.84 and 1.20 points. Block size matters less. Under this round-down rule, 16-value blocks score 0.44 points below 32-value blocks in all five folds; under the other two rules there is no consistent effect.


### [56] [Where Did the Update Go? Certifying Declared Change Envelopes for Model Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04192)
*Muhammad Usama, Muhammad Saad Qadir*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deployed language models are updated continually, and regulators increasingly accept changes that were declared in advance. Showing that an update changed behavior only where it was declared to is a different statistical problem from bounding how much it changed: a useful update is meant to change some decisions, so global disagreement and regression bounds cannot certify it. We formalize the problem with declared change envelopes. Before training, the developer commits to a region of inputs, and every decision that changes outside it counts as leakage. Leakage compares two models, so it can be certified from unlabeled traffic with anytime-valid tests whose sample cost we bound from above and below, and per-update certificates compose into a bound on lifetime drift outside all declared scopes. We measure leakage for LoRA and full fine-tuning, preference optimization, reinforcement learning, and knowledge editing on models from 1.5B to 72B parameters. Narrow updates leak widely: domain and policy LoRA updates keep only about 7-36% of the decisions they change inside the declared scope. Across 256 trained variants, leakage tracks the update's KL divergence to the incumbent on out-of-scope inputs (Spearman 0.94). None of the customary remedies we tested makes a rule-like update certifiable without giving up much of its intended change. Distilling the incumbent's decisions on unlabeled prompts in the deployment format does: anchored LoRA, GRPO, and (at a small learning rate) full fine-tuning updates certify 5% leakage from a few hundred to a few thousand unlabeled samples, and the certificates replicate on held-out traffic and on written answers. The certificates cover decision-like outputs only; on open-ended chat, re-batching alone changes 11-21% of responses and no update certifies. Code: this https URL


### [57] [An Information Budget for Label-Noise Memorization, and What Statistical Complexity Tracks: A Study with Multilayer Perceptrons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04199)
*Nancy Baygorrea C., Otávio da Fonseca Martins Gomes*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: How much label noise has a network memorized, and when? We study a multilayer perceptron and what statistical complexity adds to a description of its predictions. Standard identities decompose predictive information into signal, memorization, and heterogeneity. The memorization term vanishes in the population under the stated noise-independence condition. A Fano-type bound relates it to training accuracies, but the memorized fraction M provides no upper bound. Across 89 MNIST runs with ten pre-specified hypotheses (five supported, four unsupported, one not testable), normalized memorization information rose with M on training samples, remained near zero on held-out samples with fresh noisy labels, and was close to its Fano bound. Unlike raw M, it has a zero population null under the independence condition and chance correction largely removed this difference. When every label was replaced by an incorrect class, test accuracy fell to 0.5%, yet predictions retained information about the true class. Mean statistical complexity peaked before memorization at corruption rates up to 40%. At higher rates, its peak fell within the memorization phase, near the epoch when mean training confidence reached 0.6 and the two could not be separated. As a stopping rule, it matched established rules only at low noise and detected corrupted samples less well than simple label-free scores. An exploratory extension of 280 runs covering Fashion-MNIST, pair-flip noise, and four training variants yielded similar results under uniform noise, but not pair-flip noise, where complexity peaked within two epochs. Conclusions are limited to this architecture, two small datasets, and synthetic noise.


### [58] [LENS: Reliability-Aware Stock Prediction via LLM-Extracted Priors and News-Driven Semantic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04217)
*Hao Zhou, Tiejun Ma, Luis Felipe Costa Sperb*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Predicting stock returns requires modeling persistent company relations and time-varying market signals. Graph Neural Network (GNN) approaches combine static priors with dynamic relational graphs, but treat every extracted edge as a trustworthy binary fact, ignoring heterogeneous reliability across relational sources. We propose LENS (LLM-Extracted priors and News-driven Semantics), a static--dynamic graph framework for stock return prediction and ranking that models relational reliability as a first-class signal across the pipeline. On the static side, we extract a multi-relational company graph from a Large Language Model and aggregate it over multiple runs with confidence weighting to downweight low-consistency edges. On the dynamic side, we build a news-driven semantic graph with exponential-decay temporal aggregation to capture changing market context under uneven coverage. A context-dependent gated fusion module rebalances the two graphs at the per-stock, per-day level. On S&P 500, LENS improves AR@5 by 16.4% and SR@5 by 22.6% over the strongest baseline, and on NASDAQ 100 it achieves comparable improvements (15.5% and 20.9% respectively). Ablations isolate each component's contribution, and a human-validation study of sampled relations supports that the aggregation is grounded, providing evidence that reliability-aware LLM relations and news-based semantic graphs carry complementary information for graph-based financial prediction.


### [59] [LIRA: A More Interpretable Dynamic Loss-Informed Rank Allocation for Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04239)
*Sihao Wang, Bozheng Li, Xuemiao Xu, Haoxin Yang, Yujie Xu, Jie Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has become a widely used parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large pretrained models. However, standard LoRA typically assigns a fixed rank profile despite layer- and stage-dependent adaptation needs, while recent adaptive-rank variants often rely on heuristic or first-order signals that provide only an indirect link between rank reallocation and local loss behavior. This leaves the rationale behind each rank reallocation insufficiently interpretable. Motivated by this gap, we propose LIRA (Loss-Informed Rank Allocation), a dynamic-rank LoRA method designed to make online rank reallocation more interpretable. LIRA scores each layer by the estimated magnitude of the local loss perturbation associated with its recent LoRA-factor movements under a second-order approximation, making the basis for each rank change interpretable term by term. By periodically applying DELTA (Displacement-Evaluated Loss-Trajectory Allocation), a displacement-based algorithm for LoRA rank reallocation, LIRA produces discrete effective ranks under matched budgets while remaining compatible with standard (A,B) adapters. On RoBERTa-base GLUE under the La-LoRA/LoRA protocol, LIRA outperforms the uniform-LoRA and adaptive-rank baselines reported under this protocol; Trajectory EMA attains performance competitive with Fixed-point Fisher at lower estimation cost.


### [60] [OmniST: Topology-Invariant Spatiotemporal Forecasting with Guaranteed-Coverage Uncertainty at Unseen Sensor Locations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04256)
*Praveg Vashishtha*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Standard spatiotemporal graph convolutional networks need a fixed-size adjacency matrix, coupling model weights to a specific sensor count N; they cannot produce any output at a sensor absent from training. We present OmniST, a topology-invariant forecaster whose computation has zero structural dependency on the number of nodes, and which, composed with inductive split-conformal calibration, gives distribution-free, coverage-guaranteed prediction intervals at arbitrary (including never-seen) sensor locations, preserving that guarantee as sensors are added or removed. Topology invariance follows from coordinate-based tokenization, k-nearest-neighbor attention from geographic coordinates at each forward pass, and per-node temporal processing via a bidirectional GRU. On real Delhi air-quality data (37 stations, 2019-2023), OmniST attains a 30-day PM2.5 MAE of 31.0 ug/m3 and degrades at most 1.1 percent when 40 percent of stations are removed. Its inductive forecasts at unseen stations beat nearest-neighbor interpolation (about 12 percent) and a learned kriging GNN (IGNNK, about 21 percent), and, unlike either, carry a finite-sample coverage guarantee. The guarantee holds at 90 percent or above under 30-50 percent node dropout across four real datasets over three domains (Delhi and Beijing air quality, NOAA GHCN weather, Caltrans PeMSD7-M traffic), and still holds at 325 real sensors (full PeMS-BAY). We also fix a temporal-drift failure: static conformal under-covers over time (about 0.50 late), but a rolling adaptive-conformal estimator restores target coverage (0.89 overall, 0.96 late) at the cost of wider intervals.


### [61] [Deep Learning Compilers: Architecture, Optimization, and Empirical Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04273)
*Jinghao Wen, Ruixuan Wang, Xun Jiao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As deep learning (DL) models become increasingly complex and hardware architectures grow more heterogeneous, DL compilers play a critical role in optimizing computational efficiency and enabling scalable deployment. This paper presents a systematic analysis of DL compilers, covering their architectural evolution, compilation workflows, optimization techniques, and emerging research directions. We organize DL compiler architectures into frontend, middle-end, and backend stages, emphasizing the role of the middle-end in connecting high-level model representations with hardware-specific optimizations. We further examine representative DL compilers and recent optimization techniques across different compilation stages. Beyond architectural and technical analysis, we conduct an empirical evaluation of compiler behaviors to investigate the effectiveness of graph optimization, compilation overhead, and dynamic-shape handling. Experiments using PyTorch/TorchInductor on an NVIDIA RTX A6000 demonstrate that compiler benefits depend strongly on workload characteristics, with speedups of up to 8.21\times for fusion-friendly computation graphs, while other workloads exhibit limited improvements. Our results further reveal that compilation overhead can require thousands of executions to amortize, and dynamic-shape compilation reduces cumulative shape-transition costs by approximately 70\% at the expense of modest steady-state performance degradation. By combining systematic architectural analysis with quantitative experimental evidence, this work provides insights into the design, optimization, and practical deployment of modern DL compilers.


### [62] [Reciprocity Can Erase Direction:tructural Boundaries and Controlled Utility of Magnetic Graph Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04295)
*Dawei Han, Zicheng Wang, Yaoxing Ren, Yi Lv, Rongqian Wu, Fumin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Magnetic graph networks encode direction through phase, but a learned graph can erase the intended directional mechanism before prediction. We characterize this failure for support-based magnetic Laplacians. Exact identities relate imaginary energy and distance from zero phase to amplitude-weighted reciprocity. Top-$k$ selection forces complete collapse when the observed-node count satisfies $m\leq k+1$, and dense support imposes a lower bound on reciprocal-arc fraction. We then construct paired propagation examples with identical node features and symmetric amplitudes but opposite labels, giving a provable chance-level limit for direction-erasing representations. In 72 fixed neural fits, support phase achieved test AUROC 1.0000 on a unilateral ring but 0.5000 on reciprocal ring and complete supports; continuous score phase and a real directed comparator achieved 1.0000 throughout. All models solved the direction-free control. These are oracle-direction mechanism tests, not evidence of learned clinical direction. In the preserved public DeepDRiD development study, continuous phase was active but its robustness difference from zero phase was only $+0.0036$ (descriptive 95\% interval $-0.0014$ to $+0.0095$) on validation patients used for checkpoint selection. The contribution is a structural and experimental framework for distinguishing erased direction from unestablished task utility. It provides a precise circumstance in which magnetic phase preserves useful information, without asserting universal or clinical superiority.


### [63] [What Is the Influence of Task Order in Continual Learning? An Empirical Study and Order-Aware Evaluation for Motion Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04309)
*Zirui Li, Yunlong Lin, Haohan Yang, Xiaocong Zhao, Chen Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Aggregate scores for continual learning algorithms rest on a distribution over task orders that is rarely stated. We formulate algorithm comparison as an expectation over a declared order distribution, which separates training variation from order variation, and apply it to motion forecasting with four road-layout tasks from the INTERACTION dataset, all 24 task orders, four algorithms, and three training replicates (288 runs). Task order accounts for 65\% to 91\% of the variation in current-task error and backward transfer, and 90\% to 99\% of this order effect comes from the identity of the starting task: the smallest task cannot be learned from a cold start. Beyond the first stage, the order effect is comparable to training variability. In most orders, the leading algorithm in current-task error and backward transfer is within one replicate standard deviation of the runner-up. Backward transfer depends on the order mainly through its reference term, which weights the first task most heavily, so relative improvement and final error identify different leading algorithms for displacement error. A transition-direction descriptor computed from task statistics tracks the position of the smallest task, and weighting orders by it leaves the algorithm ranking unchanged. These results call for benchmarks that declare their order distribution and report order-resolved, starting-task-resolved, warm-start, and final errors alongside aggregate scores. The code and data are available \href{ this https URL }{here}.


### [64] [Coarse Semantic Blocks for Efficient Training-Free Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04313)
*Sosuke Kobayashi, Shoichiro Yamaguchi, Seiya Tokui*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce a training-free sparse attention method that builds a prompt-specific key index to accelerate both prefill and decoding. The method clusters the keys into large semantic blocks that group tokens from noncontiguous positions. Each query position selects blocks by scoring their centroids and attends to the original keys and values within them. Queries that select the same block are processed together, so prefill stays fast even though each query chooses its own blocks. Large blocks make centroid scoring and this grouped processing cheap, so each query can attend to more tokens in the same time. Larger blocks with a larger token budget can thus be both faster and more accurate on long-context tasks than smaller blocks with a smaller budget. We evaluate two hybrid LLMs, Qwen3.5-4B and 35B-A3B, on one NVIDIA H200. Including the cost of building and using the index, whole-model wall-clock speedups over dense attention are 2.3-2.4x for prefill and 1.7-2.0x for decoding at 512K tokens, and 2.9-3.0x for prefill at 1M. The 35B-A3B model stays close to dense attention on five RULER tasks through 1M, on LongBench-v2 overall, and on MRCR through 256K. The 4B model loses accuracy mainly on multi-key retrieval, yet outperforms dense Qwen3.5-2B, whose latency is similar at 512K, on all three benchmarks.


### [65] [Anchor: Augmented Cross-Layer Recurrent State for Long-Context Retrieval in Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04327)
*Vimal William, Yuanzhe Li, Danella Zhao, Huanrui Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Parameter-efficient linearization turns a pretrained autoregressive Transformer into an efficient model by hybridizing sliding-window attention (SWA) with a constant-size recurrent memory. But does every layer's recurrent state contribute to long-context retrieval? Investigating an SWA and Test-Time Training (TTT) hybrid, we find that retrieval is sparse across depth, concentrated in a few anchor layers, and that the remaining states hold similar information expressed through layer-specific transformations. We exploit both observations in Anchor: TTT memory placed only at the anchor layers, and a cross-layer recurrent state written once and read by many layers, each through its own learned read map that realizes a layer-specific stretch and rotation, along with demonstrated generalization to other hybrid attentions. On Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B, Anchor delivers 2.3-3.1x higher recall and 6.7-8.9 points higher MMLU accuracy than LoLCATs with a 10x smaller recurrent state, retains 91-95% of the teacher's MMLU accuracy, and cuts end-to-end latency by up to 4.3x relative to softmax attention.


### [66] [Proper Agnostic Learning of Affine Halfspaces under Log-Concave Marginals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04344)
*Zhijie Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We give a proper constant-factor agnostic learner for affine halfspaces under full-dimensional log-concave marginals. For arbitrary binary labels, the algorithm uses independent labeled samples and returns a single affine halfspace with error at most $C\mathrm{OPT}+\varepsilon$. Its sample and real-arithmetic complexity are polynomial in dimension, inverse accuracy, and the logarithm of the reciprocal failure probability. The mean and covariance of the marginal need not be known, and the sample complexity can be made linear in this logarithmic confidence factor. The main geometric result compares global disagreement with disagreement on a balanced restriction: for a minority halfspace of mass $p$, global disagreement is within universal constant factors of $p$ times conditional disagreement, provided the latter is sufficiently small. To construct the restriction from noisy labels, we estimate the minority cap's mean and covariance robustly, approximately minimize the classical zonoid-depth dual objective, and expand a superlevel set to a marginal quantile. The disagreement comparison cancels the noise amplification caused by conditioning and allows the learned halfspace to be used on the entire space. The analysis combines degree-four sum-of-squares moment bounds for log-concave laws with a translation-based reduction from balanced affine learning to homogeneous halfspace learning.


### [67] [Aligning Training and Rollout Policies for End-to-End FP4 RL on Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04376)
*Hei Yi Mak, Hoang Le, Mehran Taghian Jazi, Yunke Peng, Huan Lin, Zhengzhi Tang, Zhexu Liu, Yao Wang, Yaoyuan Wang, Anandharaju Durai Raju, Tanzila Rahman*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this work, we study end-to-end FP4 reinforcement learning (RL) post-training for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models. Recent work on FP4 RL has introduced effective recovery recipes that restore accuracy under aggressive low-precision quantization for dense models, but we find that the same approach yields no improvement over the naive FP4 baseline in the MoE setting. We conduct an in-depth study to understand why. Our analysis identifies the root cause in the routing mechanism unique to MoE: because the router makes discrete expert-selection decisions, training-inference policy alignment becomes acutely sensitive to whether the same recovery technique is applied symmetrically on the training and inference sides. When an asymmetric recipe is used-including the dense-model approach, which corrects only the rollout-training-inference alignment deteriorates sharply, as measured by the Pearson correlation coefficient between training and inference probabilities, and router selection matching rates. Building on this finding, we propose MoeResQ, a quantization-recovery recipe for MoE RL post-training that enforces symmetry between the training- and inference-side corrections. MoeResQ restores training-inference alignment and substantially closes the accuracy gap to full precision, establishing symmetric recovery as the key principle in FP4 RL for MoE models.


### [68] [Sketchpad: certified exact arithmetic with a small vision-language model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04434)
*Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Language models are accurate on most long-arithmetic problems, but their accuracy is estimated on samples and carries no guarantee for an unseen input; tool use is exact but moves the computation outside the network. We show that exactness at all input lengths becomes a finite, checkable property of a network when each of its steps reads only a bounded view of an external working memory. In Sketchpad, Qwen3.5-0.8B with a low-rank adapter executes arithmetic procedures on a rendered sheet. Each forward pass reads a three-cell view and emits one step: a digit, a carry or borrow, and the next operation. A fixed runtime moves the view and writes no digit of a result. We prove in Lean that the model is then exact on all inputs of any length if and only if it is exact on the finite set of views the runtime presents, that no finite test suffices when steps also read earlier steps, and that the guarantee tolerates bounded numerical perturbation and extends to compositions of procedures. All 2,446 views pass on the deployment device, a laptop GPU running the model in a web browser. The certified model performs signed addition and subtraction, long multiplication and division, square roots and greatest common divisors at arbitrary length. It solved 200 of 200 pre-registered problems in the browser, and 220 of 220 sums of up to 1,000 digits and products of up to 20 digits, of which GPT-6.1 Sol, the strongest of ten other models, solved 217. A symbolic runtime built on these procedures solves, expands and factors polynomials with every number computed by the model, and a multiplicative gate on the adapter recovers the base model exactly when the sheet is not in use.


### [69] [Near-Zero Token Embeddings Silently Stall Training in Gated Sequence Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04449)
*Xin Liu, Boyuan Sun, Yuxin Wang, Haoran Song, Mingjian Zhao, Mengyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Among 291 surveyed open-weight language model repositories (280 distinct embedding tables), 52.6% of untied gated and hybrid checkpoints (20/38) contain reachable input embeddings with norms below $10^{-6}$, compared with 0% of weight-tied models. Such near-zero rows can arise during pretraining when unseen rare or template tokens receive zero input gradients while AdamW weight decay shrinks untied embeddings. In tested Transformer configurations, non-initial positions or a BOS token prevent this failure mode. In gated sequence models (Mamba, Gated DeltaNet, xLSTM), a near-zero token can amplify input-embedding gradients by more than 30 orders of magnitude (up to 35). At zero input, current-token readout factors (SiLU gates or linear queries) vanish, suppressing historical-state readout. Residual RMS values can then fall below the square root of the RMSNorm floor across consecutive layers, causing Pre-RMSNorm to amplify backward gradients by up to $10^3$ per layer. When a gradient entry exceeds $1.84 \times 10^{19}$, FP32 global-norm clipping in PyTorch, Hugging Face Trainer, and FSDP2 can overflow to infinity and zero the current batch's gradients. In Telugu continual pretraining of Nemotron-3-Nano-4B, this discards updates on approximately 20% of steps and leaves triggered rows frozen. We propose two remedies. For released checkpoints, GroupClip decouples input-embedding clipping using a 64-bit or rescaled norm; its rescaled implementation eliminates update loss and heals triggered rows in one step with negligible measured end-to-end overhead. For pretraining, GateFloor ($\mathrm{SiLU}(z) + \delta$, $\delta \in [0.01, 0.1]$) keeps the gate positive at zero input, reducing near-zero gradients by 28 orders of magnitude in experiments up to 370M parameters, with perplexity comparable to the baseline.


### [70] [SaMA: Morpho Adaptation via Asymmetric Expansion of Kronecker Product](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04462)
*An Nguyen, Anh Tong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has attracted significant attention in the parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) landscape. However, its design suffers from two fundamental limitations. First, its invariance manifold induces flat directions in the loss landscape, creating an optimization bottleneck for adaptive optimizers. Second, its effective rank scales only linearly with the parameter budget. In this paper, we propose Scalable Morpho Adaptation (SaMA), a PEFT method grounded in a principled generalization of the Kronecker product. By relaxing the shared-block constraint in the perfect-shuffle factorization, SaMA spans from low to full rank and achieves a practical rank that scales quadratically with the parameter budget, up to full-rank. Furthermore, we show that its invariance manifold collapses to a diagonal group, making it structurally more optimizer-friendly. Empirically, SaMA consistently outperforms strong PEFT baselines on commonsense and arithmetic reasoning benchmarks while being more parameter-efficient.


### [71] [Theoretical Analysis of Muon on Transformers for Next-Token Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04465)
*Shengwei Wu, Yixuan Yang, Gao Huang, Song Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Muon has recently shown strong empirical performance in training Transformer-based large language models (LLMs), yet its optimization dynamics remain poorly understood from a theoretical perspective. In this paper, we analyze the training dynamics of Muon for next-token prediction (NTP) in Transformers. The training procedure consists of two phases: the preprocessing phase learns the output-value component, while the main phase learns the key-query component. For the preprocessing phase, we characterize the optimization trajectory exactly and show that the normalized spectral margin is optimal after each update. For the main phase, we establish nonasymptotic convergence rates for the training loss and the normalized margin gap under softmax attention. These results suggest that Muon's update rule can help models efficiently learn which tokens matter for prediction, offering a possible explanation for its strong performance in large language model training. The learned weight structure also yields a generalization guarantee for unseen contexts. Furthermore, we validate our theoretical insights with experiments on next-token prediction tasks.


### [72] [SKILL2STEER: One-Pass Hypernetwork for Mapping Natural-Language Skills to Low-Rank Post-Block Activation Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04497)
*Ran Li, Lei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Compiling a natural-language agent skill into model parameters removes the skill text from the prompt and leaves behind a small artifact that can be distributed and reused. Existing methods differ in where they inject the compiled quantity, and all of them assume that the artifact carries the content of the skill. We test that assumption. Through three controls on a retrieval agent, we find that a compiled skill shift carries a domain-general behavioural prior, not the content of the skill. A wrong skill document performs on par with the correct one, a single averaged bundle matches per-skill routing under a two-sided equivalence test, and a same-norm random direction collapses to the no-injection baseline, which shows that the trained direction stays load-bearing. We then introduce SKILL2STEER, a one-pass hypernetwork that reads a skill document once and maps it to low-rank activation shifts added at the output of every decoder block. Each skill compiles to 2.36M parameters, 9.25 times fewer than a weight-space adapter, while the base weights stay frozen and the generator runs only at compile time. The shift can be scheduled across retrieval steps and deployed as one routing-free bundle. On SearchQA, a seven-dataset benchmark, SKILL2STEER reaches 37.9 exact match, 9.8 points above the frozen model and 2.3 points above a weight-space method that ships 9.25 times more parameters per skill.


### [73] [Sample-Grained Approximate Unlearning with Provable Per-Sample Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04501)
*Yaxin Xiao, Yulin Jin, Li Tang, Haoyang LI, Huadi Zheng, Qingqing Ye, Haibo Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine unlearning aims to erase the influence of a designated forget set from a trained model while preserving strong utility on the retain set. Approximate unlearning methods efficiently achieve this goal without full retraining, but their effectiveness is primarily assessed using dataset-level aggregate metrics. These aggregate metrics can mask deviations from retraining at the level of individual samples. In this work, we focus on per-sample unlearning effectiveness. We introduce SALA (Sample-grained Likelihoods ALignment), a plug-in optimization framework that enables approximate unlearning methods to operate at the level of individual samples by steering each example toward its correct target distribution. To quantitatively assess this behavior, we further propose the sample-grained distribution gap, a metric that captures per-sample discrepancies between approximate unlearning and exact retraining output distributions. We provide theoretical guarantees showing that SALA contracts this gap and yields a bounded per-sample discrepancy to retraining. To make this framework practical, we develop a low-cost estimation method for the member and non-member target distributions. Extensive experiments on classification and generation tasks demonstrate that SALA reduces the sample-grained distribution gap by up to $86.67\%$ when paired with existing approximate unlearning baselines, while also improving robustness to unlearning hyperparameters.


### [74] [Variational Learning with Lie Group Exponential Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04513)
*Eren Mehmet Kıral, Thomas Möllenhoff, Koichi Tojo, Mohammad Emtiyaz Khan, Kenichi Bannai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Variational Bayesian learning for deep neural networks offers many potential benefits. The difficulty in implementation arises from constraints on the parameters of the variational distributions, as well as the need for low-variance gradient estimators. Our recent work proposes Lie group methods for multiplicative or affine groups that naturally overcome such difficulties. Lie groups are essentially parameter spaces with a binary operator such as addition or multiplication, which allows for natural updates within the parameter space. In this article, we streamline and extend our Lie group framework to any general Lie group, and give an explicit relation to standard natural-gradient variational learning methods such as the Bayesian learning rule (BLR). In particular, we prove that linear approximations of the Lie group algorithm give generalizations of the BLR. The key ingredient is the harmonic exponential family, a general class of Lie group exponential families associated to representations of Lie groups. Our work provides a systematic method to construct from such representations efficiently implementable algorithms for variational learning.


### [75] [The Price of List Replicability: Tight Bounds for Bias Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04532)
*Michael Chen, Atieh Bastan, A. Pavan, N. V. Vinodchandran*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of list replicable algorithms for estimating the biases of $d$ coins. An algorithm is $(k,\varepsilon,\delta)$-list replicable if for every bias vector $\mathbf{p}\in[0,1]^d$, there is a set of outputs $L_{\mathbf{p}}\subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$ of at most $k$ elements such that every $\mathbf{y}\in L_{\mathbf{p}}$ satisfies $\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{p}\rVert_\infty\leq\varepsilon$, and the algorithm output is in $L_{\mathbf{p}}$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. Prior work gave a $(d+1,\varepsilon,\delta)$-list replicable algorithm with sample complexity $\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^3}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$, but no nontrivial sample complexity lower bound was known. We establish the first non-trivial sample complexity lower bound for this problem and give an improved algorithm that matches it up to polylogarithmic factors. Together, these results determine the optimal sample complexity for list size $d+1$ to be $\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{d^2}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$, showing that the quadratic dependence on dimension is inherent up to polylogarithmic factors. More generally, for every $k\in[d+1,2^d]$, we establish matching upper and lower bounds of $\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{d^2}{\varepsilon^2\log k}\right)$ on the total sample complexity.


### [76] [CANDO: Conservative Action-Noise Directional Optimization for Residual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04543)
*Zeyu Gao, Chengkai Hou, Chaoyue Li, Zhuangzhe Wu, Jiaming Liu, Yao Mu, Peng Jia, Li Zhang, Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Pretrained generative policies provide strong behavior priors for robot control, but adapting them efficiently with online reinforcement learning remains difficult. A central challenge is how to improve a frozen generative policy without destroying the structure learned from offline demonstrations. Noise-space residual methods address this by modifying the policy's input noise, but existing noise-space methods typically regulate adaptation through fixed latent feasible regions, which may exclude valuable noise edits. In contrast, action-space residual methods are more sample-efficient, but their efficiency can still be further improved. We propose Conservative Noise Directional Optimization (CNDO), a noise-space residual RL method that improves a frozen generative policy by learning state- and noise-conditioned directions around Gaussian latent samples. CNDO removes predefined latent feasible regions and instead regulates noise-space adaptation through the soft conservative constraint, which consists of conservative value estimation and gated execution. We further introduce Conservative Action-Noise Directional Optimization (CANDO), which adds an action-space residual, combining structured latent-space improvement with local action correction. Experiments show that, once CNDO narrows the gap between noise-space and action-space residual learning, CANDO further improves over single-space optimization, achieving 76.1 NAUC and a 91.4% final success rate, surpassing the best single-space results by 6.6 NAUC and 4.1% success rate. More demos and info are available on this https URL .


### [77] [Curriculum unlearning in humans and neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04565)
*Jirko Rubruck, Alice Zhang, Andrew Saxe, Christopher Summerfield*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: During learning, humans often benefit from curricula that teach simple rules before exceptions. However, we lack a computational explanation for this phenomenon. Here, using a canonical behavioural task and modelling with neural networks, we develop and test a theory for why simple-to-complex curricula succeed. We show that during learning, both humans and neural networks show simplicity biases, a tendency to learn simple rules that are underfit to the data. In neural networks this bias is accompanied by compression of neural representations. Simple-to-complex curricula help both types of learners overcome this compression, developing representations (and policy) with appropriate dimensionality for the task. We test and confirm a further prediction from this theory: learners that have been pre-exposed to simple tasks and with more compressed initial representations benefit more from curricula. These findings suggest that curricula help with ``unlearning'' of simple rules.


### [78] [Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571)
*Andrea Cenzato, Lukas Karch, Andre Luckow, Yoran Pichon, Francesco Pasqua, Abouzar Ghasemi, Rishikesh Ranade, Ira Shokar, Julia Kowalski*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets.


### [79] [SCANO: Restoring Boundary-Condition Completeness in Sampled Meshes with a Sliced-Cross-Attention Neural Operator for 3D Thermal Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04603)
*Tianyu Li, Zhiwei Cao, Qingang Zhang, Ruihang Wang, Binyang Song, Yonggang Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training neural operators on million-cell meshes can exceed GPU memory limits. Models are therefore trained on subsamples and deployed on whole meshes or selected regions. However, subsampling can distort boundary information, degrading accuracy. We ask when boundary conditioning under sampling stays consistent with whole-mesh training, and how to aggregate and inject boundary data. Representing the solution as an integral of boundary data against type-dependent measures, we show that subsampling distorts this conditioning in three ways: incomplete boundary coverage or a sample-dependent global state, face weighting by count rather than area, and compression of boundary inputs into a low-dimensional vector. This analysis yields four measurable consistency conditions checkable before training. We satisfy them with two backbone-agnostic modules. Boundary Quadrature Attention (BQA) aggregates all boundary faces, typed and area-weighted, into slice tokens that every query point reads at every layer. Anchored Global State (AGS) estimates the global state from anchors independent of the sampled points. Adding both to a scalable message-passing and slice-attention backbone yields SCANO. We evaluate the approach on controlled and public benchmarks and a data-centre thermal simulation dataset (DCTS) containing million-cell meshes. On both benchmarks, BQA improves every backbone, reducing whole-mesh error up to 15-fold. On all three datasets, AGS aligns regional with whole-mesh predictions, cutting regional error at least tenfold for most backbones on the public benchmark. Together, the modules nearly halve the best original backbone's whole-mesh error on both benchmarks. SCANO predicts a room-scale field in under one second, over 100 times faster than whole-room anchor self-attention.


### [80] [CAPSkip: Cancellation-Aware Prefix Skipping for Selective Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04611)
*Heejin Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Per-step selective back-propagation forwards every example of a mini-batch but back-propagates only some of them. Most rules rank examples by loss or gradient magnitude, but such scalar scores ignore cancellation between per-example gradients: the gradient removed by a set can be much smaller than its individual magnitudes suggest. We introduce CAPSkip (Cancellation-Aware Prefix Skipping). Among the examples the model already classifies correctly, CAPSkip skips the longest prefix, in increasing order of last-layer gradient norm, whose vector sum stays within a relative budget $b$ of the batch's last-layer gradient. In exact arithmetic this bounds the change of the raw final-layer gradient, using rank-one contributions already available from the forward pass; the bound does not extend to deeper layers, optimizer updates, or the training trajectory. Because the cumulative distortion is not monotone in the prefix length, CAPSkip scans all prefixes; a factorised Gram matrix makes the scan cheap and keeps it on the device. With activation reuse and selected-row backward, CAPSkip fine-tunes DeiT-S on CIFAR-100 for 103 epochs in 0.576x the time of 103-epoch dense training, and is +0.25 pp [+0.08, +0.42] above an approximately time-matched 59-epoch dense run (n=3).


### [81] [Policy Network Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04614)
*Jiate Liu, Deheng Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models have shown remarkable progress in AI-generated content, including images and videos. In this paper, we explore whether diffusion models can generate the parameters of policy networks in reinforcement learning (RL), and whether the generated policies exhibit generalization capabilities. To achieve this, we propose Diffusion-based Conditional Policy Network Parameter Generation (DPPG), which consists of a Transformer-based autoencoder and a task-conditioned diffusion model. The autoencoder achieves a high compression rate by patchifying network parameters, while the task-conditioned diffusion model generates corresponding parameters conditioned on different environments. Our experiments demonstrate that, with a single unified DPPG model, we can synthesize high-quality policies across a diverse set of tasks from different environment domains, including Classic Control, Atari games, and MuJoCo. Additionally, our method can generate networks with a larger number of parameters compared to previous approaches. Furthermore, we find that in previously unseen environments, DPPG can generate parameters that achieve faster convergence and reach competitive performance, showcasing the potential future applications of parameter generation via diffusion.


### [82] [Self-Privileged Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04632)
*Yangrong Hu, Xueyu Zhou, Yue Wu, Jian Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a key component of language model post-training, enabling effective task adaptation and serving as an important foundation for subsequent reinforcement learning. Recent methods seek to improve SFT by introducing token-level weights into the training objective, where these weights are typically derived from a model's output distribution at each token position. To better understand how such weighting schemes affect SFT, we analyze their optimization objectives and theoretically characterize the target distributions they induce. Our analysis shows that dynamic weighting methods, such as Dynamic Fine-Tuning (DFT), induce a heavily sharpened distribution, whereas static weighting methods fit a target distribution proportional to the product of the data distribution and a frozen prior. These findings suggest that static weighting provides a more stable training target, while its effectiveness critically depends on the quality of the prior distribution. Building on this observation, we propose Self-Privileged Fine-Tuning (SPFT), which uses a frozen copy of the initial model conditioned on privileged information (such as ground truth for mathematical questions) as a prior to provide guidance for token-level weighting during fine-tuning. Through a Bayesian decomposition, we further show that SPFT reweights the standard static prior according to how informative each target token is for predicting the ground-truth outcome, thereby assigning larger weights to more predictive tokens. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation show that SPFT improves over standard SFT and other baselines across the main settings.


### [83] [Does LinUCB Need Valid Confidence Sets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04641)
*Ahmed Ben Yahmed, Marc Abeille, David Janz, Clément Calauzènes*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We show that replacing LinUCB's usual confidence width $\beta$ by any fixed $\alpha>0$ preserves sublinear regret when the action set is $M$-smooth and $m$-strongly convex. Our high-probability regret bound for this generalisation, $\alpha$-LinUCB, establishes that valid confidence sets are not necessary for the success of LinUCB. We identify the mechanism behind this result: action-set curvature drives $\alpha$-LinUCB to explore every direction, even when optimism is not guaranteed. To analyse this mechanism, we introduce a logarithmically capped spectral potential that, unlike the standard log-determinant potential, caps contributions from well-explored directions. This yields lower bounds on the smallest eigenvalue of the design matrix. At the standard confidence width $\alpha=\beta\asymp\sqrt{d\log T}$, our $T$-round regret bound in $d$ dimensions is $O\bigl(\frac{M}{m}d\sqrt{T\log T}\bigr)$, up to iterated-logarithmic factors, improving the horizon dependence of the classical LinUCB guarantee by a factor of $\sqrt{\log T}$.


### [84] [Exploiting Biases in Attention for Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04657)
*Charles Herr, Gabriele Spadaro, Enzo Tartaglione*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Attention is known to be sparse in vision transformers. In particular, we analyze the unequal relevance of attention-matrix columns to the final computation and show that this phenomenon becomes increasingly pronounced with network depth. This can be interpreted as saying that tokens have an absolute importance for the attention process, independently of queries. Inspired by this, we propose a reformulation of the attention formula that makes this phenomenon explicit by adding an input-dependent bias to the query-key dot product. We show that this new term gives each token a scalar value indicating its anticipated influence on other tokens during the attention process, and can thus serve as a proxy for pruning attention maps. Across multiple transformer model sizes, this allows us to substantially reduce the computational cost of attention, in terms of both FLOPs and inference latency, while retaining most of the original classification accuracy. We publicly released the code and models at this https URL .


### [85] [Leakage-free evaluation of residual graph convolutional networks for network intrusion detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04660)
*Zahid Iqbal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph neural networks are increasingly proposed for network intrusion detection, yet many reported results come from protocols in which test flows shape the graph used for training, duplicated flows sit on both sides of the split, or a single split is reported. This study measures what a residual graph convolutional network gains over simpler models once these sources of optimism are removed. CICIDS2017 and NF-UNSW-NB15-v2 were cleaned with label-aware removal of exact duplicates, removing 69% of NF-UNSW-NB15-v2 flows. Flows were linked in a nearest-neighbour graph built from training flows only, and validation and test flows were attached to their nearest training flows at prediction time. A two-layer residual network was compared with eight models under the same settings, including a nearest-neighbour vote over the same graph, gradient-boosted trees and a random forest, over three seeds that each draw a new split, with ablations, leave-one-family-out and feature-noise tests. On CICIDS2017 the residual network reached a binary F1 of 98.11% (SD 0.18) and on NF-UNSW-NB15-v2 90.57% (0.38), indistinguishable from a multilayer perceptron without the graph and from other graph networks. Tree ensembles were 1.48 to 1.57 F1 points higher on CICIDS2017 and led on the area under the precision-recall curve on both datasets; their CICIDS2017 multiclass macro-F1 reached 93.08% against 64.51%, a gap mainly from benign flows the residual network assigned to small attack classes. No ablation, including transductive graph construction, changed F1 beyond seed variation. Under Gaussian feature noise (SD 0.5), the residual network kept an F1 of 96.16%, while XGBoost fell to 0.67%. Under a leakage-controlled protocol, message passing over flow graphs adds no measurable accuracy over a perceptron, and tree ensembles remain stronger on both; the advantage of graph models lies in robustness to feature noise.


### [86] [Best-of-Depth: Learning Exploratory Dynamics in Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04663)
*Shota Takashiro, Takeshi Kojima, Masanori Koyama, Kohei Hayashi, Yusuke Iwasawa*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped Transformers extend inference-time computation through repeated latent-state updates, but final-depth training does not directly encourage useful intermediate alternatives. We introduce Best-of-Depth (BoD), which trains a recurrent trajectory as a candidate set by minimizing prediction loss across depths. Different valid targets can be matched at different depths without architectural changes or depth-specific labels. An exact-match analysis connects this objective to solution coverage. On N-Queens completion and graph coloring, BoD improves coverage over final-depth and intermediate-supervision objectives. N-Queens coverage reaches 91.03%, versus 45.75% for final-depth training. Further experiments show discovery beyond the training depth and recovery of solutions withheld from teacher pools on familiar inputs. Fine-tuning a pretrained looped language model with BoD yields higher pooled code-generation success than final-depth training when programs are generated separately at evaluated fixed depths. These results support training recurrent computation for solution discovery along its trajectory.


### [87] [Feature-Based and Deep Learning Methods for Astronomical Light Curve Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04664)
*V. Baymakanov, Kh. Khamitov, A. Raimova, A. Isaeva, R. Taran, M. Zedan, M. Kornilov, M. Demianenko, K. Malanchev, M. Hushchyn*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We compare feature-based machine learning and end-to-end deep learning for variable star classification using the Zwicky Transient Facility (ZTF) StarEmbed dataset, which contains about 40,000 objects across seven imbalanced classes. Classical models trained on physically motivated features consistently outperform deep networks trained directly on raw light curves. We find that limited labeled data contributes to the performance gap, while the multivariate, irregularly sampled, and morphologically diverse nature of light curves poses additional challenges for deep learning models. While deep learning holds promise for learning representations directly from observations, our results indicate that current models fail to generalize with the available training set size. These findings motivate further investigation of the benefits of scaling labeled datasets and self-supervised pretraining on large unlabeled surveys. This work establishes a quantitative baseline for future research and highlights the need for further innovations to unlock the potential of deep learning in astronomical time series analysis.


### [88] [Sheaf-MPNN: A Library for Sheaf Neural Networks as Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04670)
*Alessio Borgi, Mario Severino, Luke Braithwaite, Steve Azzolin, Rémi Bourgerie, Olga Zaghen, Emanuele Mule, Riccardo Ali, Leo Minh Kustermann, Edoardo Coppola, Antonio Purificato, Stefano Fiorini, Christopher Irwin, Filippo Spiga, Fabrizio Silvestri, Michael Bronstein, Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Standard Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) implicitly assume that neighboring nodes share the same feature space, which contributes to oversmoothing and poor performance on heterophilic graphs. Sheaf Neural Networks (SNNs) remove this assumption by giving each node and edge its own vector space and learning restriction maps that transform features as they move between neighbors. These maps define a sheaf Laplacian that drives diffusion over the graph, but existing implementations often assemble it explicitly as a global sparse block matrix, which adds memory and computational overhead and can hinder scalability. In this paper, we introduce Sheaf-MPNN, a PyTorch Geometric library that reformulates sheaf diffusion as local incidence-wise transport and aggregation, eliminating the need to construct the global Laplacian. Sheaf-MPNN implements fifteen families of SNNs under a unified message-passing framework, each supporting both homogeneous and heterogeneous graphs, and directed and undirected settings, with diagonal, general, orthogonal, and low-rank restriction maps and attention-based predictors. We further provide a unified benchmarking suite covering node- and graph-level tasks, heterogeneous graph learning, and computational efficiency, enabling systematic and reproducible comparisons across architectures. We establish theoretical equivalence between these message-passing formulations and their original counterparts and identify the architectural mechanisms needed to preserve their propagation dynamics. This unified perspective provides a principled foundation for implementing, comparing, and extending sheaf-based architectures within standard graph learning frameworks. Source code is available at this https URL .


### [89] [Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04689)
*Jinwu Hu, Langyu Bian, Jian Sun, Dongjin Yang, Chao Shuai, Yufeng Wang, Kai Zhou, Bin Xiao, Mingkui Tan, Hongmin Cai, Wei Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Open-set test-time adaptation (TTA) aims to adapt source-trained models to unlabeled test streams where covariate-shifted in-distribution (csID) samples and covariate-shifted out-of-distribution (csOOD) samples may coexist. Existing TTA methods are typically designed under a closed-set assumption and therefore lack explicit mechanisms for reliably distinguishing csID from csOOD samples. Consequently, csOOD samples may be mistakenly included in confidence-enhancing adaptation, producing harmful gradients that degrade known-class performance. To address this, we propose Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation (UAPA), a plug-and-play method for reliable open-set adaptation. Specifically, we first introduce Uncertainty-Driven ID/OOD Separation, which combines a classifier-prototype score with a mini-batch-normalized evidence-derived uncertainty score to distinguish csID from csOOD samples. We then propose a Dynamic Prototype Refinement strategy that periodically refines class-specific target prototypes using buffered csID features to progressively follow the estimated class directions. Lastly, we introduce Contrastive Prototype Entropy Adaptation, which applies entropy minimization to csID samples and entropy maximization to csOOD samples, while prototype-level contrastive learning aligns csID features with their corresponding target prototypes and reduces the maximum similarity between csOOD features and known-class prototypes. Empirically, UAPA outperforms the strongest baselines on the CIFAR benchmarks and Tiny-ImageNet-C, improving average OSCR by 2.08 and 1.28 percentage points, respectively.


### [90] [Surrogate Models for Stochastic Al-Cu Phase-Field Solidification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04695)
*Simon Savukoski, Tatu Pinomaa*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Phase-field models resolve microstructural evolution during rapid alloy solidification, but their computational cost limits long trajectories, stochastic ensembles, and studies across process conditions. We present a variance-preserving denoising diffusion bridge model (VP-DDBM) for state-conditioned prediction of two-dimensional Al-Cu solidification. Given the current phase, concentration, and prescribed temperature fields, the model predicts the subsequent state and is applied autoregressively. Across two held-out thermal gradients, VP-DDBM produces lower long-horizon errors than deterministic U-Net and SI-ConvNeXt baselines in domain-mean phase, domain-mean Cu concentration, and leading-tip position. It also retains fine dendritic structure over long rollouts, whereas the deterministic U-Net progressively smooths the morphology. VP-DDBM predicts the phase and concentration evolution approximately $73\times$ faster than the phase-field solver, showing potential for faster process-condition screening and ensemble-based microstructure studies.


### [91] [TAPES: Task-Aligned Joint Predictive Pretraining for Transaction Event Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04747)
*Ruobing Zuo, Weiyu Han, Huilin He, Haoyu Jiang, Ze Zhao, Jie Sheng, Xiao Tan, Shuai Chen, Tianyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Transaction event sequences support diverse prediction tasks in online commerce and digital financial services. The same transaction records can yield prediction targets with different semantics, including values, changes, and relations to past events. These targets impose different constraints on history representations, but their predictability alone does not establish the downstream utility of a target set. Moreover, target-wise prediction losses do not explicitly model conditional co-occurrence among targets. To address these challenges, we propose TAPES, a task-aligned joint predictive pretraining framework for transaction sequences. TAPES constructs a finite collection of candidate target sets from admissible field-operator combinations. It selects a target set according to the downstream utility of representations learned through short joint pretraining trials under matched budgets. It then reinitializes the encoder and pretrains it on the selected targets through a joint likelihood parameterized by a shared discrete latent variable, before downstream supervised adaptation. Experiments in a real-world transaction-risk setting and on multiple public financial benchmarks spanning diverse downstream tasks demonstrate that TAPES outperforms representative baselines. Ablation studies further validate the contributions of predictive target selection and joint modeling.


### [92] [What Does It Mean to Remove an Edge? Intervention-Aware Fidelity for Graph Neural Network Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04751)
*Rocco Mattia Di Mauro, Marco Grassia, Giuseppe Mangioni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph neural network explanations are often evaluated via deletion fidelity, i.e., by deleting their top-ranked edges and measuring the resulting change in model output. Yet, in a message-passing GNN, "deleting an edge" does not specify a unique intervention. Structural deletion removes the edge from the graph and recomputes quantities such as degree normalization and attention, whereas message suppression leaves this structural context intact while blocking the message carried by the edge. The meaning of deletion fidelity therefore depends on what the intervention targets in the model computation. We formalize this distinction and derive sufficient conditions for exact equivalence. The two interventions coincide for additive message passing with edge-set-independent coefficients, but can diverge when aggregation depends on the presence of zero messages or when removing an edge changes the coefficients applied to surviving messages. We then construct intervention-specific edge attributions and evaluate each resulting ranking under both interventions, testing whether their relative fidelity changes with the evaluation intervention. Across nine datasets and a panel of GIN, GCN, and GATv2 models at depths 2--5, all nine GIN controls satisfy the equivalence predicted by the theory. On the target logit, the relative fidelity of the two rankings depends on the evaluation intervention in 10 of 11 reportable GCN and GATv2 cells, and in nine of them each ranking has the larger deletion area under the intervention it was built with. Deletion fidelity is thus not a property of an edge ranking alone. Evaluation protocols should explicitly report the intervention, output functional, target, and perturbation unit, and test alternative intervention semantics when the scientific question does not uniquely determine one.


### [93] [Non-Interfering Asymmetric AdamW Updates for Constrained Exploration of Equifinality in Hybrid Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04756)
*Ekaterina Gikalo, Zavud Baghirov, Christian Reimers, Markus Reichstein, Martin Jung, Marco Körner*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Hybrid models let neural networks supply the unresolved parameters of a process-based simulator, and are fitted to observations that constrain aggregate fluxes and storages but not the internal partitioning that produces them. Such a fit is equifinal: configurations indistinguishable in the observed aggregates can disagree substantially about the unobserved internals, and therefore about the mechanism they attribute to the same data. We study evapotranspiration, whose total flux is observed while its split into transpiration and evaporation is not. A model ensemble built from random seeds alone spans only a narrow band of the partitionings the data admit. We produce the spread deliberately, training members under auxiliary repulsion on transpiration, subject to the fit to observations not degrading. Non-interference is usually arranged by making the auxiliary gradient orthogonal to the main one, but under AdamW the applied step is a preconditioned momentum direction, so orthogonality between raw gradients no longer implies it. We introduce Non-interfering Asymmetric Network Optimization (NANO), an update whose first-order effect on the main loss is exactly that of the main objective alone, whose auxiliary step is bounded, and which concentrates repulsion in the coordinates the main gradient leaves idle.


### [94] [Koopman Generative Operators for Efficient Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04760)
*Raz Marshanski, Liran Nochumsohn, Mayank Jauhari, Boris N. Oreshkin, Omri Azencot*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Probabilistic time-series forecasting requires models that simultaneously capture structured temporal dynamics, expressive uncertainty, and efficient inference, yet existing approaches fall short of this goal: latent dynamical models impose structure but rely on restrictive generation mechanisms, while modern generative methods such as diffusion and flow matching achieve flexibility at the cost of iterative and computationally expensive sampling. We introduce the Koopman Generative Operator (KGO), a probabilistic forecasting method that conceptualizes prediction as the evolution of structured uncertainty. KGO integrates three core components: (1) Koopman Patch Embedding (KoPE) for temporally consistent latent trajectory extrapolation; (2) Koopman Flow Matching (KoFM), which enables fast, single-step generation through a closed-form matrix exponential in a Koopman latent space, bypassing iterative sampling; and (3) an Adaptive Uncertainty Gate (AUG), which provides probabilistic forecasts by adapting uncertainty per-variable and per-horizon, corresponding to a learned decomposition of aleatoric uncertainty without manual tuning. By unifying these components, KGO achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on 13/17 ProbTS benchmarks in CRPS and 16/17 in NMAE. Further, by eliminating iterative sampling, KGO delivers at least a 25$\times$ reduction in inference time compared to iterative generative models. These results position KGO as a practical and principled framework that advances the accuracy-efficiency trade-off in probabilistic time-series forecasting, combining leading accuracy on the evaluated benchmarks with fast, single-step generation.


### [95] [Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Submodular Trajectory Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04774)
*Yasaman Zolfimoselo, Nikolas Geroliminis, Maryam Kamgarpour*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning with decoupled dynamics and a monotone submodular reward function over joint trajectories.
