Due to the non-additive reward, an optimal policy may depend on the history of all agents. This dependence makes exact optimization exponential in the horizon and the number of agents. To overcome this challenge while retaining theoretical guarantees, we introduce pre-window curvature $c_m$, which quantifies how states visited more than $m$ steps earlier reduce current marginal gains. Using this curvature to control the error from restricting the policy of each agent to depend only on its last $m+1$ states, we propose a greedy algorithm that exploits the decoupled dynamics to optimize local policies sequentially. For fixed $m$, the algorithm achieves a $\frac{1-c_m}{2-c_m}$ approximation guarantee up to estimation error, and its computation time is polynomial in the number of agents and the horizon. We recover the known $1/2$-factor approximation for time-additive rewards and obtain an approximation factor no smaller than the known total-curvature bound for a single agent. We formulate multi-agent traffic monitoring as an example of this framework and evaluate our algorithm on it.


### [96] [Density-Aware Reward Scaling: A Reinforcement Learning Framework for Reward-Proportional Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04792)
*Ria Shekhawat, Abhijeet Sinha, Yingtao Zhu, Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning objectives that maximize expected return can concentrate probability mass on a small subset of high-reward outcomes, limiting diversity when multiple distinct solutions are desirable. We introduce Density-Aware Reward Scaling (DARS), a simple reward-processing mechanism that promotes reward-proportional sampling in continuous outcome spaces while retaining standard policy-gradient training. DARS estimates the local density of outcomes within each on-policy rollout group and inversely scales rewards by this density. The method requires no learned density model, auxiliary objective, critic, or modification to the underlying policy-gradient objective. In a controlled multimodal navigation task, DARS-GRPO avoids the single-mode collapse exhibited by GRPO and closely recovers the target mode allocation. Applied to text-to-image prompt adaptation, DARS-GRPO consistently improves image and prompt diversity across datasets, Policy LMs, and reward settings while maintaining good semantic alignment. These results show that density-aware reward scaling provides a lightweight way to improve coverage of diverse high-reward outcomes within existing reinforcement-learning pipelines.


### [97] [Training Specialist LLMs on Your Phone: Forward-Only Optimization via Block-Spectral Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04797)
*Liyan Tan, Yequan Zhao, Ziyue Liu, Yupeng Su, Ruijie Zhang, Zheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) on personal devices keep user data local, but limited memory restricts their general capability. Since most user-specific tasks do not require the breadth of frontier models, a practical solution is to adapt a shared on-device model into lightweight specialists. However, backpropagation is prohibitive in memory, and users rarely provide labeled data. We present MobileSpec, a system that trains specialists with forward passes alone and learns from automatically checked task outcomes, as in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Because forward-only updates are built from random perturbations, training is efficient only when these perturbations resemble the weight changes of RLVR, which largely preserve the singular values of pretrained weights and rotate their singular vectors. The core algorithm of MobileSpec, FuRA-S, produces such perturbations while training only the block-spectral coordinates of pretrained weights, namely the singular values of their column blocks. As we show theoretically and empirically, rescaling these blocks independently shifts their relative contributions to each weight matrix and thereby rotates its singular vectors. Since this update can be rewritten as a transformation of the layer input, FuRA-S also adapts INT4 models without modifying their weights. With specialists of only 1-5 MB that update 0.015-0.05% of the parameters, MobileSpec raises the success rate of a phone-use agent from 6-23% up to 89-100% and trains a personalized 8B model on an iPhone 16 Pro overnight. On RLVR benchmarks, FuRA-S is competitive with full-parameter evolution strategies (ES) and outperforms ES over LoRA adapters and quantized ES on INT4 models.


### [98] [On the Complexity of Finite-Sum Nonconvex-Strongly-Concave Minimax Optimization with Exact Proximal Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04806)
*Yuxing Peng, Zhiqing Tang, Weijia Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Finite-sum nonconvex-strongly-concave minimax problems arise in adversarial training and distributionally robust learning. Their known lower bounds cover only linear-span algorithms, zero-respecting algorithms, or a restricted proximal protocol. They leave open the power of exact component proximal steps, whose responses are global saddle points of regularized component problems and can expose directions that gradients do not reveal. We show that neither unrestricted adaptivity nor exact proximal access improves the polynomial complexity. We consider $n$ components with joint mean-squared smoothness $L$ and condition number $\kappa$, started from a point with primal gap at most $\Delta$ and joint gradient norm at most $\sqrt{2L\Delta}$. For $\varepsilon^2/(L\Delta)$ below a universal constant, every randomized adaptive algorithm with exact component values, gradients, and proximal responses needs $\Omega(n+\min\{\sqrt n\,\kappa,n^{3/4}\sqrt\kappa\}L\Delta/\varepsilon^2)$ component queries in the worst case to find an $\varepsilon$-stationary point of the value function. A method using only component gradients attains this rate up to a factor $\log^2(2+\kappa)$, including initialization. Zhou ( arXiv:2610.04944 ) concurrently proves the same rate for zero-respecting first-order methods. In our hard instances, every component admits proximal steps with the same step sizes, and a response for a component reveals hidden directions only up to the first one assigned to another component.


### [99] [Sleep-Disordered Breathing Detection with Quantum Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04818)
*Alejandro Mayorga-Redondo, José Daniel Viqueira, Diego Alvarez-Estevez, Eduardo Mosqueira-Rey, Andrés Gómez*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This work investigates the application of Quantum Recurrent Neural Networks to the binary classification of sleep-disordered breathing events in time-series data, aiming to distinguish between apneic and normal breathing episodes. A variational classical--quantum architecture is proposed and evaluated against classical Recurrent Neural Network models. Experiments were conducted on multiple real-world datasets with varying levels of complexity to assess the strengths and limitations of the quantum approach. In the comparative analysis, both classical and quantum models showed comparable performance. The quantum model achieved superior performance on the smallest dataset, while yielding slightly weaker results in the most complex setting. However, the quantum approach exhibited notable strengths, such as competitive performance despite a drastically reduced parameter count and low-qubit regime, as well as good generalization capabilities. These results suggest that, despite current hardware and algorithmic limitations, variational quantum recurrent models represent a promising alternative for time-series classification as Quantum Computing technologies continue to mature.


### [100] [Anomaly Detection on Resource-Constrained Devices with Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04835)
*Marco Angioli, Mathias Persson, Hiroto Tamura, Hadi Banaee, Amy Loutfi, Gouhei Tanaka, Alberto Giaretta, Denis Kleyko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Anomaly detection at the edge requires the deployment of models that achieve competitive performance, while minimizing computational cost and memory footprint. In this article, we investigate the suitability of the Mahalanobis Distance of Reservoir States (MDRS), an unsupervised time-series anomaly detection method, for resource-constrained devices and propose two optimizations to reduce its computational requirements. Specifically, we replace random reservoir connectivity (MDRS-Rand) with a circular shift (MDRS-Circ) and show that the anomaly score for an entire time series can be computed from the final reservoir state alone. Furthermore, we show that exploiting the reservoir's temporal memory enables more efficient input preprocessing, further reducing computational cost. We evaluate the MDRS variants on the MLPerf Tiny anomaly detection benchmark against the reference autoencoder and other anomaly detection methods, and deploy their inference kernels on a NUCLEO-F401RE microcontroller to assess latency, estimate energy consumption, and measure memory footprint. Among the evaluated methods, only MDRS and the autoencoder meet the benchmark's performance target, with MDRS requiring fewer operations and smaller models. With optimized preprocessing, MDRS achieves an ROC AUC of 0.869, a model size of 24 KB, an estimated energy consumption of 24 uJ per inference, and a latency of 0.32 ms, corresponding to a 75.9x speedup over the autoencoder with standard preprocessing.


### [101] [Found by Search, Broken by a Shortcut: Transfer and Tampering of Searched Intrinsic Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04838)
*Chase J. Metoyer*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Can a search over innate drives find intrinsic rewards that help a reinforcement-learning agent learn, transfer to a task it never saw, and resist an exploit that was absent during the search? Each candidate reward combines four generic events (a new view, a pickup, a drop and a toggle), each with a weight, a relief and a refill rate. We ran three pre-registered stages; the search protocol was locked in a public, timestamped commit before any search result existed. CMA-ES and an equal-budget random search used 1,560 PPO training runs, and the final evaluation and tamper test used 340. On the held-out task, KeyCorridorS3R2, the CMA-ES-selected reward (E) beat task-only PPO, episodic novelty and the random-search-selected reward (R) in all 3 registered comparisons, all of them meaningful by the registered size rule. Among these generic rewards E transferred best; the earlier paper's satiation reward, which on this task is given hand-wired key and locked-door events, did better (20 of 20 seeds solved against 4 of 20; descriptive). On UnlockPickup, where that reward uses generic events, E's mean area exceeded it by 0.276 (descriptive). On the training tasks there was no registered difference between E and R: both E-over-R tests missed. A free reset action, absent during the search, collapsed E's learning on DoorKey-8x8 (mean area 0.829 without it, 0.136 with it; registered) to below task-only PPO (descriptive). The searched drives helped on every task tested, but a reward given task-specific events did better on the held-out task, and selection gave E no protection against an exploit absent from the search.


### [102] [DLER: Dual-Logit Experience Replay for Online Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04885)
*Huiwei Lin, Shanshan Feng, Kong Aik Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Online continual learning aims to continuously train neural networks on a non-stationary data stream in a single pass. Rehearsal-based methods alleviate catastrophic forgetting by replaying stored samples, but the imbalance between current and previous data often leads to predictions biased toward current classes. Many approaches have been proposed to overcome the forgetting problem by correcting the bias; however, they still need to be improved in an online fashion. By decomposing the dot-product logits into an angle factor and a norm factor, our analysis reveals that the angle factor efficiently captures novel knowledge but is particularly susceptible to prediction bias, whereas the norm factor helps preserve historical knowledge. Based on these observations, we propose Dual-Logit Experience Replay (DLER), which exploits dual-logits for both model optimization and memory management. Specifically, DLER learns current samples using cosine logits and incorporates additional dot-product logits when replaying previous samples to mitigate prediction bias. To strengthen dual-logit learning under limited replay memory, an auxiliary supervised contrastive objective promotes angular compactness within each class, extending corrections learned from limited replay samples to other samples of the same class. Meanwhile, we introduce a memory update strategy that preferentially retains samples for which dot-product prediction assigns a higher probability to the correct class than cosine prediction, aligning sample selection with dual-logit training. Extensive experiments on three datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DLER, particularly under limited memory budgets.


### [103] [Fault-tolerant shallow recurrent decoders for sparse sensing with missing sensor measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04893)
*Matteo Romilio Pasqual, Matteo Tomasetto, J. Nathan Kutz, Andrea Manzoni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Real-world sensing systems are inherently imperfect: sensors may fail, intermittently disconnect, operate with asynchronous sampling rates, and be subject to noise, leading to measurement streams that are irregular, incomplete, and heterogeneous. Nevertheless, sensing strategies for real-time state reconstruction typically assume synchronous and continuously active sensors, a constraint that substantially limits their applicability in operational environments. In this work, we introduce fault-tolerant sparse sensing strategies in the context of shallow recurrent decoders (SHRED) to enable high-dimensional state estimation from sparse, asynchronous, and missing sensor measurements. Specifically, we consider naive imputation strategies, fault-tolerant recurrent temporal encoders, and we introduce four different transformer-based masking mechanisms to make SHRED explicitly aware of sensor availability through the binary data-availability mask. Moreover, we further introduce a multi-step decoding training objective for SHRED, resulting in smoother latent trajectories and more robust predictions. Throughout a synthetic benchmark and three real-world problems in hydrodynamic and ocean modeling, we show that (i) transformer-based masking mechanisms outperform standard imputation techniques and fault-tolerant recurrent architectures, (ii) the multi-step decoding training objective enhances reconstruction fidelity and robustness in the presence of heterogeneous missing-data patterns, while (iii) the computational efficiency of SHRED is preserved, with no additional inference-time overhead.


### [104] [Attending to Memory Writes Reduces Seed Variability in Recurrent Length Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04902)
*Sungwoo Goo, Hwi-yeol Yun, Sangkeun Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recurrent memory requires learning useful updates, but attention added to assist learning can also compromise length generalization. We study bounded delta attention: causal attention over uncorrected update proposals whose bounded correction enters the recurrent update. Its implementation in TraceRelay combines additive phase recurrence with attention computed before a parallel scan. On prefix-supervised modulo-5, trained at lengths 1--40, three findings characterize its behavior at length 80. First, upper delta attention attains $70.81\pm8.26$% accuracy across seven seeds, versus $69.21\pm22.42$% for the local-attention recurrent baseline (mean $\pm$ sample SD): a 63.2% reduction in SD with a similar mean. Upper state attention also reduces variation, reaching $74.75\pm6.24$%, so this benefit is not unique to update-level attention. Disabling the upper delta correction retains $70.62\pm8.60$%. Second, blocking gradients at the delta-attention inputs changes accuracy to $65.25\pm12.40$% in the matched seven-seed comparison, increasing SD by 50.2%. It also delays a 99% validation threshold on average among the same six seeds that reach it; the 95% threshold is reached earlier on average with input-gradient blocking across all seven seeds. Third, lower delta attention exhibits substantially less extrapolation degradation than lower state attention: 62.60% versus 19.40%, against a 71.06% baseline. Removing its correction reduces accuracy to 22.56%; its advantage over lower state attention also persists under input-gradient blocking. These observations motivate bounded update-level attention as a recurrent learning scaffold, combining reduced seed variation with less severe degradation in a placement where state attention fails.


### [105] [Cool Graphs: Active Property Search Towards Quantum Nano-Refrigerators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04907)
*Sofia Blyufer, Amir Kleiner, Uri Peskin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Nanodevices that absorb energy from their thermal environment can act as quantum refrigerators. Finding suitable configurations, however, is a needle-in-a-haystack problem: the many-body state space scales exponentially with the size of the device, where each candidate configuration requires an expensive quantum-chemical computation. We introduce a search framework that couples a Graph Variational Autoencoder (GVAE) with an uncertainty-driven Active Learning (AL) loop to efficiently identify energy-absorbing configurations in electronic junctions with a 2D "working material" to be optimized. The GVAE maps physical parameters - site energies, coupling matrices, temperature - into a smooth latent space that distinguishes cooling from dissipative parameter regimes, achieving a classification discriminability of $0.920 \pm 0.004$ on held-out configurations and $0.895 \pm 0.010$ on unseen extrapolation topologies. An AL pipeline based on Monte Carlo dropout then targets the decision boundary, iteratively selecting the candidates where the model is least certain for simulation. Over 32 AL cycles, the uncertainty-driven strategy discovers energy absorbing systems at rates consistently exceeding the random baseline across all tested topologies (where only 1.9-8.4% of configurations exhibit the desired property), providing a data-efficient pathway for navigating a combinatorically large design space. The learned latent space organizes configurations along physically interpretable axes, that correlate with underlying microscopic model parameters. This hints at the existence of "cooling coordinates" - low-dimensional, physically interpretable quantities separating cooling from non-cooling regimes. Our framework, thus, paves a new way towards effective cooling of nanoscale electronic information processing devices, to reduce the environmental footprint of high performance computing.


### [106] [Modulating Merging Strengths via Joint Loss Estimation for LoRA-based Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04931)
*Kun Gu, De Cheng, Zhipeng Xu, Lingfeng He, Di Xu, Nannan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) aims to adapt models to new tasks while preserving previously learned knowledge. Recently, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), a representative Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method, has gained increasing attention in CL due to its efficiency and scalability. Several LoRA-based merging methods maintain a single inference model by scaling the newly learned LoRA update with one coefficient and merging it into the previous weights after each task. However, they usually control the whole update with a shared scalar factor, although different update elements may affect previous and current tasks in different ways. To address this issue, we propose Merging Strength Modulation via Joint Loss Estimation (M2LE). It consists of Hessian Information-guided Adaptive Merging (HIM) and Task Simulation Perturbation for Merging (TSP). Instead of searching for a global merge coefficient, HIM directly solves for the target merged update under a joint objective over previous and current tasks. Solving this objective gives an analytic solution that naturally takes the form of element-wise modulation over the current task LoRA update. To improve the stability of the learned update under subsequent merging, we introduce TSP which simulates different tasks interference. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that M2LE consistently outperforms existing PEFT-based continual learning methods. Code is available at this https URL .


### [107] [Deep Neural Operator Learning for stochastic heat equations and applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04955)
*Hengrong Du, Qi Feng, Weinan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this paper, we establish a universal approximation theorem for solution operators of semilinear stochastic heat equations in spatial dimension \(d\ge2\), extending deep neural operator approximation results to SPDEs with distribution-valued Gaussian forcing. A smooth Fourier cutoff reduces the forcing to finitely many measurements, while Besov and Schauder estimates control the resulting solution error. The finite-dimensional solution map is then approximated by ReLU branch networks together with grid-based trunk functions, yielding explicit bounds on the numbers of forcing measurements and trunk functions and approximation in every finite stochastic moment. For linear equations, the reduction coincides with truncation of the first Wiener chaos. Lastly, as an application, we prove a high-probability local-in-time approximation theorem for stochastic Navier--Stokes equations, with finite measurements of both the initial condition and forcing and an explicit sensing rate.


### [108] [ZEMA: confidence-calibrated signed descent with only one state](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04987)
*Ali Dadsetan, Domenic Rosati, Frank Rudzicz*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work characterizes the Adam optimizer as sign descent modulated by gradient uncertainty: the update is the sign of the batch gradient, scaled by how strongly the batch supports that sign. We apply this view directly and introduce ZEMA (Z-score Evidence Momentum Adaptation), which measures that support with a per-coordinate $z$-score computed from the per-example gradients in the current batch, converts it into a sign probability, and smooths the resulting update with a single exponential moving average. Because the probability is formed before smoothing rather than after, ZEMA keeps one state per parameter instead of Adam's two, at a $4.6$ increase in per-step time; the per-example variance it requires can also be estimated from a small fraction of the batch. In fine-tuning and pretraining experiments, ZEMA is competitive with Adam, reduces the variability of the validation loss, and in some settings recovers the sign of the full-dataset gradient more consistently. We close with two directions this opens: extending confidence adaptation beyond the sign geometry, to methods such as Muon, and to differentially private training.


### [109] [Exact Selective Inference for Spectral Clustering via Piecewise-Constant Graph Perturbation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04995)
*Naotoshi Nakamura*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Cluster labels are often used twice: first to discover groups, and then to test whether the discovered groups differ. This double use of the data makes ordinary two-sample $p$-values anti-conservative. Selective inference fixes this by conditioning on the clustering event, but for spectral clustering the event appears difficult because the algorithm combines an affinity map, an eigendecomposition, and a rounding step. We show that, for spectral clustering based on unweighted $\varepsilon$- or $k$-nearest-neighbor graphs, the obstruction is the affinity construction rather than the eigendecomposition. Along the one-dimensional perturbation used by the truncated-$\chi$ selective test, graph changes occur only at finitely many real roots of data-dependent quadratic distance comparisons. Between these breakpoints, the graph, Laplacian spectrum, embedding, rounding, and reported cluster pair are deterministic functions of a finite graph state. Consequently, in real arithmetic and under a known-scale spherical Gaussian model, the selection set is an exact finite union of intervals, yielding finite-sample selective $p$-values for selected spectral-cluster pairs. The same argument gives feature-wise truncated-normal tests. We also record direct extensions to known feature covariance, eigengap-selected $K$, and auxiliary-coordinate graphs. We validate null and partial-null calibration, operational power, numerical sensitivity, and model-scope diagnostics, and give a working-model PBMC algorithmic illustration. Smooth kernels such as RBF affinities fall outside the finite-state guarantee.


### [110] [Auditing Multi-Proxy Explanations of Web User Preferences: Identification Boundaries and Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04997)
*Yu Zhang, Zhihan Wang, Yuhang Lu, Shuai Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Web systems infer user preferences from behavior, ratings, text, and LLM-generated profiles, yet explaining a proxy may reveal its measurement process rather than the preference of interest. We develop an audit framework distinguishing point identification, partial identification, and abstention relative to an explicit reference distribution. Under feature-dependent, arbitrarily signed proxy bias, a strict majority of uncontaminated proxy attributions lets the coordinatewise median recover latent-attribution support and direction and, with externally justified positive scales, magnitude. We prove this threshold sharp using response-valid indistinguishable worlds, characterize sharp minimax error and exact compatible-target sets under approximate cleanliness and scale uncertainty, and propagate clean-proxy attribution error. A frozen known-truth benchmark spans two response families, four proxy counts, all contamination counts, three bias strengths, and eleven methods, with a focal comparison confirmed on twenty unseen seeds. Under correct budgets, a direct attribution-tensor audit achieved 100% coverage, 95.40%-97.05% active-driver sign decisions, and no false signs. In a temporally separated Amazon study, median aggregation reduced comparator-relative conditional sign error for all four targets; six of eight prespecified target-metric contrasts had Bonferroni intervals excluding zero. A controlled real-covariate perturbation favored the median on the prespecified focal metric for all four targets. Compatible sets showed that the current proxy configuration rarely licensed strict-sign conclusions. Weak comparator calibration limits the Amazon evidence to fitted-surface alignment rather than latent-preference recovery.


### [111] [Unifying and Beyond the Theory of Pervasive Capability Degradation in LLM Post-training SFT from Feature Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05003)
*Hang Chen, Jiaying Zhu, Wenya Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Pervasive Capability Degradation (PCD) refers to the phenomenon in which supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while adapting a large language model (LLM) to a target task, often erodes the general capabilities acquired during pre-training. Prevailing explanations attribute PCD to macroscopic distribution shift and therefore lack microscopic manipulability, leaving existing mitigation methods without a complete theoretical foundation. In this paper, we first develop a bottom-up geometric theory of PCD grounded in feature interference under superposition: narrow-domain SFT data disrupt the almost-orthogonal feature structure formed during pre-training, and the resulting interference drives activations across decision hyperplanes in the output Laguerre-Voronoi geometry, flipping predictions on general tasks. This theory unifies existing methods into six paradigms and reveals both the source of their efficacy and their theoretical limits. We then prove that cross-entropy under one-hot supervision cannot resolve PCD on its own, and accordingly propose the AEGIS loss, which directly constrains the drift of activation margins along all competing token directions, thereby decoupling interference suppression from task adaptation. Across 3 model families and 14 SFT tasks, AEGIS outperforms representative methods of every paradigm on target tasks and pervasive capabilities simultaneously, at only about 1.3 times the computational cost of standard SFT. Its generality, scalability, and mechanistic interpretability offer a new perspective on fully resolving PCD.


### [112] [Rethinking GA2Ms: performance and out-of-distribution generalisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05012)
*George Dimitriadis, Damian Machlanski, Paul Clarke, Spyridon Samothrakis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Generalised Additive Models (GAMs) are a cornerstone of interpretable machine learning, offering transparent predictions through structured additive decompositions. Despite advances from classical GAMs to modern neural and spline-based variants, two notable gaps remain. First, comprehensive benchmarking of spline-based GAMs on large-scale tabular learning tasks, the dominant modality in applied settings, is lacking. Second, their robustness under distribution shift, a setting where structured inductive biases should confer advantages, remains unexplored. We propose a multi-resolution, higher-order Composite Generalised Additive Model (CoGA2M) and evaluate it on the TALENT benchmark suite. CoGA2M ranks in the top third across all baselines (29 for regression, 32 for classification), establishing competitive performance with state-of-the-art tabular methods. We further extend TALENT with out-of-distribution (OOD) splits exhibiting train-test distributional shift and benchmark CoGA2M against LightGBM, a consistently top-performing model on TALENT. Under distribution shift, CoGA2M outperforms LightGBM, demonstrating a clear structural advantage. Our results position spline-based GAMs as strong contenders for tabular prediction and reveal a previously unrecognised benefit of additive structure in the OOD regime.


### [113] [Finite Spectral Depth Controls Gaussian Entrance Rank and Avoidance of the First Gradient-Flow Saddle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05022)
*Yanlin Long*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Matrix factorization provides a simple model for studying the training dynamics of neural networks. Recent studies of gradient flow under small initialization have analyzed how trajectories escape from a neighborhood of the origin and approach their first nonzero stationary point. In this paper, we study continuous-time dynamics that applies a finite number of Newton-Schulz iterations to the normalized matrix gradient (Muon). We show that the number of Newton-Schulz steps can change the rank selected during the early stages of training and allow the resulting trajectory to bypass the first nonzero stationary point approached by gradient flow. Under an additional residual-inertia condition, we prove that bypass occurs for all sufficiently large finite Newton-Schulz depths and provide an explicit sufficient threshold. We also construct an example in which the bypass threshold is four.


### [114] [DRAP: Dynamics-Risk Adaptive Plasticity for Continual Learning in Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05029)
*Oswaldo Ludwig*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual learning in physical AI differs from conventional continual learning because small per-step policy errors can accumulate over a trajectory. Each deviation may move the agent outside the training distribution, increase subsequent prediction errors, and ultimately cause closed-loop task failure. This work introduces Dynamics-Risk Adaptive Plasticity (DRAP), a replay-free method that estimates the historical importance of adaptable model tensors from sample-level action gradients weighted by local trajectory-amplification risk. A ridge-regularized local linear model is fitted to expert state-action transitions, and the largest singular value of its state-transition matrix provides a policy-independent, Lyapunov-inspired estimate of one-step perturbation amplification. After each task, risk-weighted squared gradients are accumulated in a tensor-level importance memory. During subsequent tasks, DRAP uses normalized cumulative importance to scale the effective AdamW learning rate of each tensor, immediately reducing the plasticity of components that supported previous behavior at dynamically sensitive trajectory points. We instantiate DRAP on a fixed KappaTune-selected subnetwork of OpenVLA and evaluate replay-free sequential adaptation across the LIBERO Spatial, Object, and Goal suites. Compared with standard AdamW fine-tuning and EWC, the results show substantially improved retention of previously learned behaviors while preserving strong new-suite acquisition.


### [115] [Federated Transfer Learning for Medical Image Classification under Mixed Skew](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05035)
*Alexandre G. Leclercq, Sébastien Bougleux, Aurélien Corroyer-Dulmont, Romain Hérault*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep learning in medical imaging is constrained by data scarcity and by privacy regulations that make centralizing patient data across institutions impractical. Federated Learning (FL) addresses these constraints by keeping data local, but its distributed nature exposes training to strong statistical heterogeneity. Multicentric medical data is particularly affected, exhibiting a mixed skew that combines feature, label, concept, and quantity shifts across centers. Personalized FL mitigates heterogeneity by tailoring a model to each client, yet most methods assume training from scratch, which is unrealistic given the high dimensionality of medical features relative to the limited data available. We propose the Personalized Aligner Federated Pretrained (PAFP) framework, a personalized FL approach built on a pre-trained backbone. PAFP inserts client-specific Feature Alignment modules before the fine-tuned blocks to realign hidden feature distributions toward a server-aggregated target. On the FedISIC benchmark, PAFP attains the highest unweighted balanced accuracy while reducing inter-client performance variance roughly six-fold relative to the strongest baseline, yielding accuracy and cross-client equity jointly. Our code is publicly available at this https URL .


### [116] [Positional Encoding is All You Need for Scalable Equivariance Constraint Relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05041)
*Hagay Michaeli, Haggai Maron, Daniel Soudry*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Equivariant neural networks incorporate known symmetries of the data, such as permutations, rotations, or translations, directly into their architecture, often improving sample efficiency and generalization. However, strict equivariance can make optimization difficult, for example, when the data only approximately satisfy the assumed symmetry, or when the equivariance constraints induce a challenging loss landscape. Recent studies have shown that relaxing equivariance during training by introducing non-equivariant dense linear layers can ease optimization in such cases and improve the performance of the resulting strictly equivariant model, after removing the relaxation layers at test time. While effective, this approach incurs a significant computational overhead, limiting its applicability in scalable architectures. In this work, we propose a more efficient constraint-relaxation method based on positional encoding (PE). Our framework injects a positional encoding with learnable scale that breaks the model's original symmetry. This positional signal is gradually annealed to zero, recovering the original equivariant model by the end of training. Empirically, we demonstrate significant improvements for translational and rotational equivariant models with only marginal compute and memory overhead.


### [117] [Effects of Parton Pair Correlations on Direct-Photon Yields and Elliptic Flow in Heavy-Ion Collisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05042)
*Xingjian Lu, Shuzhe Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Direct photons escape heavy-ion collisions with little rescattering, carrying information from the full emission history. Yet describing the measured direct-photon yield and the sizable elliptic flow $v_2$ together remains a long-standing challenge. Here we explore how correlations between emitting partons may affect both observables. The local $2\to2$ photon rate depends on the joint distribution of incoming partons, so a connected correlation between them can change photon emission without changing the one-particle distribution of medium particles. Once folded over the evolving medium in Au+Au and Pb+Pb collisions, the correlation raises the photon yield toward the fitted PHENIX and ALICE data and increases $v_2$ mainly at intermediate $p_T$. The fitted temperature dependence accounts for most of the net flow increase in this region, suggesting that the correlation changes the relative contributions between hotter and cooler regions.


### [118] [Dual-VaDE: Efficient and Robust Prototype Learning via Variational Dual-Domain Mixture Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05099)
*Wugeng Zheng, Song Wang, Jian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this paper, we propose Dual-VaDE, an unsupervised generative framework for learning interpretable anatomical prototypes from unlabeled medical images. In contrast to previous variational methods that rely on a single spatial latent code, which often entangles global geometry with local textures and fails to capture their cross-domain dependencies, Dual-VaDE introduces a dual-domain Gaussian mixture prior that explicitly couples spatial anatomy with phase-preserving Fourier spectral representations. We derive an exact, closed-form variational lower bound that fuses this joint prior into the decoder and optimizes the entire network end-to-end via an alternating EM formulation, avoiding heuristic two-stage clustering while ensuring superior training stability and robustness. Evaluated on OASIS-3 brain MRI, Dual-VaDE delivers substantially sharper and more plausible prototypes, achieves superior cluster separation with improvements of up to 94% over spatial-only baselines, exhibits markedly reduced variance across random seeds, and incurs negligible inference overhead (0.28 ms per image).


### [119] [Molecular Prior Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05103)
*Pawel Gomoluch, Anthony Bradley*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Predicting affinity between small molecules and their protein targets remains one of the central problems in drug discovery. Due to the substantial cost of wet-lab experiments, machine learning models of drug-target affinity are usually trained on relatively small datasets, ranging from tens to hundreds of molecules. Given the inherent difficulty of generalization between different targets and chemical series, existing datasets of protein-ligand affinities are typically not used for new projects. At the same time, tabular foundation models, including Prior-data Fitted Networks, have recently shown how transformers can be used to solve machine learning problems in a single forward pass of the network, after being trained over a large corpus of, typically, synthetic datasets. Inspired by that line of work, we propose a novel affinity modelling approach, in which the labels of the query molecules are predicted from the labelled support molecules in a single forward pass of a transformer model. The model is trained on thousands of existing affinity datasets, enabling it to capture the general patterns of molecular activity across different targets, chemical series and dataset sizes. For the smallest support sets, our model matches the state of the art on the regression variant of the FS-Mol benchmark, while being faster than existing methods, thanks to the lack of task-specific training. A comparison against a reduced variant of our architecture, which amounts to kernel regression in a meta-learned feature space, shows that the predictive performance of the transformer model is only partially explained by simple molecular similarity.


### [120] [Beyond Multi-Index Models: Learning Intersections of Degree-2 PTFs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05114)
*Ilias Diakonikolas, Daniel M. Kane, Mingchen Ma, Lisheng Ren, Trung Tran*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We give an algorithm for PAC learning intersections of $k$ degree-2 polynomial threshold functions over $R^d$ under a Gaussian distribution. Our algorithm achieves classification error $\epsilon$ using $d^{O(k)}2^{O_k(\log^3(1/\epsilon))}$ time and samples, improving over the $d^{O(k^2/\epsilon^2)}$ running time of the polynomial regression approach. To our knowledge, our result is the first to obtain a dimension exponent independent of the accuracy parameter, even for $k=2$. We complement our algorithmic result with an SQ lower bound showing that the complexity dependence of $d^{\Omega(k)}$ is unavoidable. Lifting the input to polynomial features turns the target into an intersection of $k$ halfspaces. This suggests using the first two labeled moments for subspace recovery. In the lifted space, however, these moments can reveal nothing. Our starting point is a Gaussian comparison theorem showing that, for sufficiently regular defining quadratics, the labeled moments needed for subspace recovery are approximately preserved. To handle arbitrary defining quadratics, we establish a new structural result showing that every direction with significant resampling influence can be detected through degree-$O(k)$ moments. These results yield an initial $d^{O(k)}2^{\mathrm{poly}_k(1/\epsilon)}$-time learner using adaptive restrictions. To improve the exponential dependence on $1/\epsilon$, we develop a two-stage dimension-reduction approach that avoids the costly adaptive partitioning. After identifying a subspace containing all directions with large influence, we use generalized regularity and Gaussian comparison to recover the remaining relevant quadratic features through a new derivative-based moment operator. The recovered features support an intersection close to the target. Combining them with robust hinge-loss minimization gives our quasipolynomial-time learner.


### [121] [Towards an Optimal Control Foundation of Early Exits - Turnpikes and ResNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05120)
*Jens Püttschneider, Simon Heilig, Asja Fischer, Timm Faulwasser*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The feed-forward propagation through neural networks is crucial for training and inference. To trade off efficiency and accuracy, it has been proposed to consider intermediate outputs of hidden states as data-dependent regularization during training and, ultimately, as early-exits during inference. In this work, we analyze the joint optimization of loss functions computed for the output layer and intermediate layers using optimal control theory. We prove that ResNets trained in this joint fashion exhibit the so-called turnpike phenomenon, this implies that the number of layers required to reach a prescribed loss level is bounded from above independently of the overall network depth. This induces a similarity property across the internal representations of early-exiting networks of varying depths. We connect the turnpike to the overthinking phenomenon, and provide theoretical insights into the success of early-exit criteria derived from softmax probabilities, specifically entropy and max-probability. In a range of experiments, we demonstrate the existence and effect of turnpikes and the resulting similarity in the early-exit performance for ResNets of varying depths.


### [122] [Do Ray-Traced Cities Make a Better Channel Prior for an Unseen Site? A Pre-Registered Study in Simulation, and a Test on Measured Channels that Reverses It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05128)
*Krish Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Channel estimators at a newly deployed base station usually start from 3GPP stochastic channel models, which describe no particular street. We ask whether a channel prior pooled from many ray-traced cities serves an unseen site better than the 3GPP CDL models, and whether learned estimators make the difference. With Sionna RT on 14 city scenes, LMMSE estimation from covariance priors, leave-one-city-out evaluation, and pass/fail bars written down before each main experiment, we find in simulation that the pooled prior sits about 0.6 dB from a site-trained estimator with no site measurements, about what CDL reaches after 50 measurements. Learned models added little: at most 0.65 dB in estimation, and in channel prediction a simple classical method beat CNN predictors by 1.8-12 dB from 2 ms ahead. On measured channels from the DICHASUS outdoor dataset (1.272 GHz, three 2x8 arrays on one building) the result reversed: the pooled ray-traced prior was 3.2-4.1 dB worse than CDL. The simulated channels were too clean; with users at the real distances, even a scattering level tuned from 50 site measurements left the prior about 2 dB behind CDL. A pre-registered follow-on on two more measured environments found the generic CDL prior 2.1-4.7 dB from a site-trained one: no further in a factory hall than on the street, furthest in a small lab room. Finally, a hallway model calibrated to measurements by differentiable ray tracing reproduced delay spread, but as a prior in an unmeasured part of the hallway it was 4.5 dB worse than CDL on the full array. All measured results come from one sounder.


### [123] [PAC Learning with Multiple Correct Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05133)
*Steve Hanneke, Farnam Mansouri, Alireza F. Pour, Amirreza Shaeiri, Kun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study PAC learning when each example comes with a set of at most $\ell$ correct labels and a prediction is correct if it belongs to this set. Training may reveal the full set, only whether a proposed label is correct (bandit feedback), or one correct label chosen by a demonstrator (emonstrations). Our main finding is that demonstrations can be much harder than full sets, and even than bandit feedback, both in which classes can be learned and in how many examples are needed. For finite label spaces, a generalized Natarajan dimension characterizes learnability from full sets and from bandit feedback, in both the realizable and the agnostic setting. From demonstrations, however, a class is realizably learnable if and only if the intersections of at most $\ell+1$ of its hypotheses are learnable from full sets. Already with three labels, this condition can fail for a class learnable from a single full-set example or even a single bandit answer. When the condition holds, we show that $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-\ell})$ demonstrations suffice, and the exponent $\ell$ cannot be improved in general, even for classes learnable from $\widetilde O(1/\varepsilon)$ full sets or bandit answers. For countable label spaces and $\ell \geq 2$, our packed dimension, which asks for many disjoint groups of at most $\ell+1$ hypotheses with no common label, characterizes full-set learnability, and demonstrations still reduce to intersections. Agnostic learning from demonstrations, already for finite label spaces, is not determined by the dimensions of the intersection class that characterize realizable learning. Finally, we show that the uniform bound $\ell$ on the number of correct labels is essential. Without this bound, it is impossible to characterize learnability by any dimension determined by finite subclasses.


### [124] [Beyond Foundation Models: PFN Priors Improve Tabular Hyperparameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05141)
*Artem Chistyakov, Renat Sergazinov, Artem Babenko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Synthetic priors have so far served one purpose: pretraining tabular foundation models. We ask whether the same priors can improve conventional tabular models, which are trained from scratch on each dataset. We propose PriorTune, a method that meta-tunes hyperparameter configurations on synthetic tasks alone. Offline, for a given model, we fit a pool of configurations on datasets sampled from a published PFN prior and greedily select the subset with the highest winrate over the random baseline. Online, a new dataset fits only the selected configurations, so the gain comes at no extra cost. Empirically, we observe that scaling both the number of configurations and synthetic datasets has an equal scaling effect on the performance of the final subset of configurations, which we also explain theoretically. On the full BeyondArena benchmark, LightGBM with TabPrep features with our configurations ranks above TabPFN-3 and RealMLP, while being much more efficient at inference. Overall, our results establish existing PFN priors as a scalable source of meta-training data for hyperparameter selection for non-foundational tabular models.


### [125] [Macrocanonical Generator Networks: Data-Efficient Neural Surrogates for Amortized Physics Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05157)
*Niall Jeffrey, Benjamin D. Wandelt*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Many scientific simulations are computationally expensive, limiting their use in simulation-based inference, uncertainty quantification, and decision making. We introduce macrocanonical generator networks, a framework for learning fast, data-efficient neural surrogates for amortized simulation of stationary physical processes by matching multiscale statistics of a target process. Inspired by microcanonical maximum-entropy synthesis, which matches feature statistics per sample by inner-loop optimization, our approach instead trains a neural generator to satisfy the same constraints in expectation, preserving physically realistic sample-to-sample fluctuations. We prove that gradient-descent training induces preconditioned gradient-descent dynamics in sample space that inherit the symmetry-preservation properties of the microcanonical gradient descent of Bruna and Mallat, amortizing its per-sample inner-loop optimization into a single training run after which every sample is generated in one forward pass. We further show that residual generator parameterizations initialized near the identity, trained with a small learning rate, preserve entropy locally during early optimization and mitigate mode collapse. Experiments on cosmology and fluid simulations show that macrocanonical generators recover realistic samples from extremely small training sets, in some cases a single training example, while generating new realizations more than $10^6$ times faster per sample than the reference simulator and more than $10^3$ times faster than microcanonical gradient descent. The trained generators can be reused for downstream pretraining, including simulation-based inference, providing a data-efficient route to multi-fidelity scientific machine learning workflows.


### [126] [Basin2Vec: Learning Basin-Centric Embeddings for Scalable Hydrological Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05164)
*Muhammad Talha, Xiaofeng Liu, Yuan Yang, Pang-Ning Tan, A. Pouyan Nejadhashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reliable streamflow prediction across time and in ungauged basins requires representations that capture hydrologically relevant basin characteristics. Existing methods rely on either fixed handcrafted attributes or general-purpose Earth observation embeddings not tailored to basin structure. We introduce \textbf{Basin2Vec}, a self-supervised framework that learns compact basin representations from hydroclimatic and physiographic data without observed streamflow. Basin2Vec organizes representation learning around basin scale and relationships across space and time. Across multiple forecasting architectures, it consistently outperforms predefined attributes and general-purpose geospatial embeddings in temporal generalization and zero-shot spatial transfer. Basin2Vec provides average relative gains of up to 8.3% temporally and achieves 3.5x the median predictive performance of the strongest baselines on unseen basins. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of basin-centric pretraining for transferable hydrologic prediction. Code is available at this https URL


### [127] [Provably Learning Mixtures of Linear Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05184)
*Adrian Candocia, Aamod Varma, Mark Davenport*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are widely deployed in modern machine learning. However, few convergence guarantees exist for gradient-based training of MoEs: the objective is nonconvex, and even simple mixtures trap first-order methods in spurious local optima. We analyze a canonical gated mixture featuring linear experts and a gate induced by Gaussian clusters in the input space, and give, to our knowledge, the first global convergence guarantee for jointly training the gate and the experts of such a model by gradient EM on its likelihood. Given a logarithmic overparameterization relative to the true number of experts $m$, gradient EM converges from randomly initialized gates and least-squares-initialized experts to any prescribed accuracy in polynomially many iterations, under a separation condition on the gate centers. Our analysis establishes a gradient-dominance inequality for the coupled dynamics by decoupling the loss into per-cluster subproblems. Empirical results validate the predicted $m\log m$ phase transition and suggest that the least-squares warm start required by our analysis is not needed in practice: randomly initialized experts perform nearly as well. We also find that leaving the mixing weights unconstrained concentrates routing onto the true number of experts, whereas load-balancing penalties spread it, so that the fitted model can be pruned to a fraction of its width without loss of accuracy.


### [128] [Stepsize modulation for cross entropy from local quadratic optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05191)
*Bruno Bauwens, Viacheslav Krivodub, Bingyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Two methods are presented that perturb a given learning rate schedule in stochastic gradient descent with cross entropy loss. Both methods use an extra forward pass to estimate various derivatives of the minibatch loss. The first method selects a value $\eta$ by linearising the softmax values and minimizing the squared error $\tfrac 1 2 ||y - q - \eta q'||_F^2$, where $q$ is the matrix representing the softmax values of the minibatch and $y$ is the matrix with one-hot encodings of the labels. The second method considers a quadratic Taylor approximation of the minibatch loss under the outer approximation of the Hessian. Thus, it minimizes $L(0) + \eta L'(0) + \tfrac 1 2 \eta^2 L''(0)$. Here, the derivatives $L'$ and $L''$ are computed from the derivatives of the logits (using the outer approximation of the Hessian of the loss). We observe that both methods give very similar values of $\eta$. Finally, these estimated $\eta$ are used to slightly increase or decrease the learning rate of an existing schedule. The method is evaluated on Cifar10 and Cifar100 datasets for training Resnets, where they decrease the loss faster than the schedule that is perturbed and increase monotonicity. For Cifar10 it also leads to a small improvement of test accuracy, but in Cifar100 test accuracy remains the same.


### [129] [Ensembles of Random Partition-Based Models under Covariate Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05195)
*Mary Versa Clemens-Sewall, Eliza O'Reilly*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Distribution shifts, including covariate shifts, are ubiquitous in real-world applications of machine learning. Empirical evidence suggests that, across a variety of models with and without downstream adaptation, ensembles tend to be more robust to covariate shift than single models. We extend the theory of covariate shift severity from single models to ensembles, providing theoretical justification for the empirical trends. We introduce new discrepancy measures of covariate shift severity to control the risk of partition-based kernel estimators. We then show that with sufficient ensemble size, these models can achieve minimax-optimal risk convergence rates under covariate shift. Our results suggest that modelers can expect to need larger ensemble sizes for more severe covariate shifts.


### [130] [Empirical Bayes Flow Matching for Continuous Cryo-EM Heterogeneity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05202)
*Daniel Aibinder, Azmi Haider, Dan Rosenbaum*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We address the problem of learning generative priors over high-dimensional latent variables from indirect, noisy observations, with a focus on continuous heterogeneity in cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM). In this setting, 3D atomic structures $x \in \mathbb{R}^{3N_a}$ are never directly observed and must be inferred from 2D projection images $y$ generated by a complex, non-linear forward operator. We propose Empirical Bayes Flow Matching (EB-FM), a method that learns a flow matching prior $p_\theta(x)$ directly from observations using a Monte Carlo stochastic approximation Expectation-Maximization framework. EB-FM alternates between an E-step that performs approximate posterior sampling from $p_\theta(x \mid y)$ via guided flow-based generation while maintaining persistent latent estimates through stochastic approximation, and an M-step that updates the flow model using these stabilized latent variables as training targets. This approach eliminates the need for pretraining on clean data and naturally accommodates non-linear forward models. We validate our method on controlled inverse problems using two-moons and MNIST data, and demonstrate its effectiveness on simulated cryo-EM data with known poses, showing that EB-FM can recover continuous distributions of protein conformations directly in atomic-coordinate space.


### [131] [Multi-Objective Generative Optimization via Expected Hypervolume Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05207)
*Juan Guevara, Riccardo De Santi, Andreas Krause, Raul Astudillo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scientific discovery requires plausible designs spanning useful trade-offs among competing properties: a molecule with strong binding affinity, for example, may be difficult to synthesize. Pretrained generative models, e.g., flow and diffusion generators, provide a foundation for proposing such designs, but steering them toward high-quality, complementary trade-offs remains challenging. Inference-time search incurs high per-sample costs, while fine-tuning typically relies on fixed per-candidate rewards that do not capture how designs complement one another. We introduce Hypervolume Fine-Tuning (HVFT), which adapts a single pretrained generator to maximize the expected hypervolume of its samples while controlling departure from the pretrained distribution. Expected hypervolume jointly rewards candidate quality and collective trade-off coverage. Nonetheless, we show that it cannot be expressed as the expectation of a fixed per-candidate reward and therefore cannot be optimized directly with standard techniques. A key observation of this work is that its first variation corresponds to the expected marginal hypervolume contribution of a candidate, which yields a tractable adaptive reward via an unbiased estimator based on model samples. HVFT uses this reward to decompose the objective into a sequence of KL-proximal scalar-reward subproblems. We establish concavity, relative smoothness, and convergence guarantees for the resulting distributional procedure. Across therapeutic-peptide and molecular design tasks spanning discrete diffusion and continuous flow-matching models, HVFT achieves state-of-the-art expected hypervolume and trade-off coverage.


### [132] [Privacy for Free in Efficient Agnostic Halfspace Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05208)
*Giorgio Racca, Amartya Sanyal, Vikrant Singhal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We provide the first computationally efficient, differentially private algorithms for agnostically learning halfspaces. In particular, we study three different settings of agnostic learning: improper learning under Gaussian and log-concave marginals; proper learning under Gaussian marginals; and semi-agnostic learning under Gaussian marginals. In all three aforementioned settings, the added cost in the sample complexity due to privacy matches the respective non-private sample complexity, implying that we get privacy for free.


### [133] [Fusing Earth observation embeddings and spaceborne terrain with meteorological forcing for flood peak depth prediction from high-water marks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05220)
*Wen Zhou, Shaowen Wang, Upmanu Lall, Venkatesh Merwade, Dingqi Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Flood peak depth on normally dry land governs damage and risk to life, but is observed only sparsely, by post-event high-water mark (HWM) surveys, and depth derived from satellite flood extent is produced after the event and inherits the vertical error of the digital elevation model (DEM) it is computed from. Deep-learning flood models have in turn been trained on hydrodynamic simulations rather than on observed depths. We ask whether flood peak depth can be learned from field observations and predicted before the peak from spaceborne terrain, geospatial foundation-model embeddings and antecedent meteorological forcing. We assembled 5689 USGS HWM surveys from 39 events (2011-2025) in 24 U.S. states and Puerto Rico, assigned each a peak time from nearby sensors, paired each with a rain-free window at the same site, and linked them to Copernicus GLO-30 terrain, AlphaEarth embeddings and 144 h of hourly forcing masked to the lead time. A cross-modal fusion network trained on these observations predicts depth at the surveyed marks with a mean absolute error of 0.39 m 6 h ahead of the peak. Supervision by surveyed depth corrects the DEM error implicitly: differencing the measured peak water level with the same DEM gives 0.93 m at the same marks, 2.4 times larger, although it uses the measured water level and the model does not. Forcing accounts for 77% of the attributed reduction in depth error; terrain and the embeddings are statistically equivalent to each other and contribute the remaining 23%. On eleven held-out events the model still separates flooded from rain-free conditions but no longer resolves depth at the marks (R2 = 0.03), so the skill reported here is confined to the events and regions sampled. Field-surveyed depths can thus supervise flood depth prediction and correct terrain error that extent-based estimation cannot, while transfer to unseen storms remains the open problem.


### [134] [Fairness Metrics based on the AUC in Clinical Predictive AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05222)
*João Matos, Ben Van Calster, Richard D. Riley, Paula Dhiman, Gary S. Collins*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Fairness evaluation of clinical prediction models for binary outcomes often involves fairness metrics that compare the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC, or c-statistic) across subgroups. However, the AUC has been shown to be a non-collapsible metric, meaning that its subgroup specific and overall values are not directly comparable as often implicitly assumed. This raises important questions about how existing AUC-based fairness metrics should be used and interpreted. We examine eight AUC-based fairness metrics proposed in the literature, including: within-group AUC parity; cross-group AUC (xAUC) parity; gap-to-overall AUC; equity-scaled AUC; minimum-based metrics; $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$; $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$; and $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ or $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ parity. We characterise their estimands, relate them to the decomposition of overall AUC (into within- and cross-group components), and critically appraise them. We then illustrate their behaviour using simulations based on the EuroSCORE II, a clinical prediction model for in-hospital mortality. We recommend reporting an AUC matrix, containing the overall AUC and all within- and cross-group AUC components, alongside population characteristics. When fairness metrics are needed, we recommend reporting the within-group AUC parity, with cross-group AUC parity providing supplementary information but requiring caution due to its potential conflict with calibration. Metrics involving $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ and $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ have merit, summarising within- and cross-group AUCs into single values, but their interpretation is non-trivial, and they should be used with caution. Gap-to-overall AUC, equity-scaled AUC, and minimum-based metrics compare quantities that should not be compared and it is unclear how to operationalise them, making them not fit for purpose and generally not recommended.


### [135] [Post-Inference Model Chaining: A Problem Statement and a Model-based Bootstrap for Training End-to-End Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05225)
*Lukas Madenach, Timo Bailer, Tobias Eilert*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: End-to-end digital twins can be constructed by fitting separate stage models and connecting them after inference. We investigate the Frequentist prediction properties of a commonly used Monte Carlo simulation scheme that forwards complete predictive draws through such a chain when measured stage outputs contain independent normal errors, but the latent stage means are deterministic. Analytical two-stage examples show persistent asymptotic interval and coverage inflation and, with quadratic propagation, mean bias dependent on the parameters to be estimated. Simulations of four- and eight-stage linear-quadratic univariate chains with 7, 15 or 30 training runs demonstrate pronounced input-dependent distortions. A diagnostic using error-free latent training predictors shows that the effect is mainly not attributable to errors in variables, but to the MCSim approach even showing underestimation of coverage. We propose a model-based bootstrap: an initial chain forwards fitted means, bootstrap chains are refitted on fixed-center bootstrap data while forwarding their mean prediction, and a target-stage future error enters a studentized prediction root. Across the investigated scenarios, relative mean bias is substantially reduced, and coverage is closer to the nominal 90%. At sample size 30, the eight-stage bootstrap has mean width ratios of 1.16-1.42 relative to the known-parameter normal reference, compared with 4.11-4.97 for observed-to-observed MCSim. These results support improved endpoint prediction in the tested setting. Further investigations of the bootstrap scheme in the supplement for a bivariate 6-stage process show similar satisfactory results.


### [136] [Shrinkage prior for implicit generative models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05241)
*Shreya Sinha-Roy, Christian P. Robert, Ritabrata Dutta*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Implicit generative models based on deep neural networks have revolutionized many scientific fields, from weather research to language generation. They successfully model complex random objects arising in real-world problems. In this work, we propose a sample-efficient training mechanism for these models. Our approach leverages regularized inference with shrinkage priors and generalized Bayes. In the large $p$ (parameter dimension) and small $n$ (sample size) setting, sparsity-inducing priors have been successfully used for linear regression when $p \gg n$. However, their application to implicit generative models remains challenging due to the intractability of the likelihood function. To address this, we adapt a scoring rule posterior based on a strictly proper scoring rule, following the generalized Bayes framework. We then propose a block SGMCMC within Gibbs sampling mechanism. This enables efficient exploration of high-dimensional parameter spaces by identifying a sparse representation of neural implicit generative models. We demonstrate the performance of our proposed method through experiments on $p \gg n$ linear regression and an application of neural generative models.


### [137] [Logarithmic Regret for Alternating Mirror Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05243)
*Tianlong Nan, Christian Kroer*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Alternating online mirror descent (AltOMD) is a simple learning rule for zero-sum games, which improves upon simultaneous OMD numerically and theoretically. Previous work obtained $O(T^{1/3})$ and $O(T^{1/5})$ regret bounds for AltOMD, by leveraging modified-energy-function-based analyses. However, settling whether AltOMD achieves near-constant regret has remained an open question. In this paper we resolve the question of whether AltOMD achieves near-constant regret in the affirmative. In particular, we show an $O(log(T))$ regret bound on the sum of the players' external regrets for any two-player zero-sum bilinear game with compact convex strategy sets, under mild regularity assumptions on the regularizers. We verify that our assumptions hold for the negative entropy regularizer, thereby obtaining logarithmic regret for the alternating multiplicative weights updates algorithm. The regret bound also yields an $O(log(T)/T)$ duality gap bound for the averaged strategies.


### [138] [Going Deeper With Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05255)
*Afshin Khadangi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Byte level language models avoid tokenizer dependence and provide universal coverage, but processing every byte at full resolution is expensive. We introduce DeepMem, a causal multiscale architecture that separates semantic compression from exact byte preservation. A learned invertible subband transform constructs a hierarchy of shorter sequences, while a lossless carrier and explicit detail sidebands preserve byte identity. Fast weight memory blocks operate along the encoder, with iterative shared state computation at the compressed bottleneck, while an attention based super resolution decoder restores byte resolution representations. DeepMem achieves stronger language modeling performance than a matched byte level Transformer on FineWeb-Edu and PG19, and obtains the best macro average performance across the evaluated FLORES languages. It also improves PIQA accuracy while remaining competitive on code modeling, multiple choice reasoning, and byte level corruption robustness. Bottleneck refinement requires approximately $7{,}600\times$ fewer counted FLOPs than equivalent full resolution reasoning, while maintaining competitive evaluation throughput.


### [139] [NovoCRF: Globally Normalized De Novo Peptide Sequencing on a Prefix-Mass Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05260)
*Rui Qiao, Lei Xin, Baozhen Shan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: De novo peptide sequencing has moved from dynamic programming over hand-designed or generative scores to large transformers that learn the task end to end. We present NovoCRF, a conditional random field (CRF) whose states are prefix masses, so that its paths are exactly the peptides within +/-0.06 Da of the precursor mass. A small network scores each edge from physics-derived ion-match features and is trained with its scores globally normalized over all mass-feasible peptides. To our knowledge, NovoCRF is the first de novo method trained with exact normalization over all mass-feasible peptides. Our software has three modules, each with exactly one job: a precursor mass finder recovers the accurate precursor mass from MS1, the CRF finds the peptides that fit the precursor mass by exact k-best decoding, and two BiGRU rerankers add sequence context and internal-fragment evidence. The model has 310k parameters and trains in about 17 GPU-hours. On seven held-out data-dependent acquisition datasets published in 2026 (126,262 labeled spectra), NovoCRF's mean peptide recall is 42.4%, against 39.1% for Casanovo v5 with five beams, and its amino-acid recall 64.0% against 53.1%, at 1,746 spectra/s on 18 CPU threads, 55x Casanovo's throughput on a GPU. Split by fragment evidence, NovoCRF leads on labels with up to three peptide bonds without a b or y ion, and Casanovo only beyond. NovoCRF can also search modifications it was never trained on at their exact mass: it finds 32.1% of the hydroxyproline peptides of a lion bone sample.


### [140] [Stabilizing On-Policy Targeted Guidance in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05266)
*Kevin Frans, Alexander M Rush, Sergey Levine, Pieter Abbeel, Ashvin Nair*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Naive on-policy RL is often bottlenecked by the capabilities of the base model --- insufficient coverage can result in an inability to learn from certain rewards. At the same time, rewards in modern post-training systems often come from rich textual sources, e.g. judge models with a rubric of criteria. Can we utilize such structures to provide a rich directional signal that enables improving on otherwise difficult to optimize rewards? In this work, we examine a framework we refer to as targeted guidance that learns from per-turn textual feedback via an on-policy self-distillation objective. Our focus is not only on directly improving hard-to-optimize behaviors, but on how to do so throughout the training process without degrading baseline RL performance. Over a range of settings across an agentic multi-turn software engineering environment, we find success by combining targeted self-distillation with a per-criteria RL reward, and by limiting feedback to turns where the policy specifically fails. We further examine the design choices that lead to stable entropy and reduce oscillatory behavior throughout training.


### [141] [iGC: Antibody Optimization via Reinforcement Learning over a Learned Somatic Hypermutation Prior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05270)
*Thao Nguyen, Robert G. Alberstein, Ewa Nowara, Homa MohammadiPeyhani, Holly R. Steach, Sarah A. Robinson, Yuval Shechter, Donald W. Lee, Jason A. Vander Heiden, Franziska Seeger, Andrew Leaver-Fay, Richard Bonneau, Joshua Yao-Yu Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Antibody affinity maturation is a natural optimization process in which B cells acquire mutations through somatic hypermutation (SHM), undergo selection for improved antigen binding, and iteratively evolve toward higher affinity. Computationally modeling this process requires capturing both biologically plausible mutations and antigen-specific selection. However, existing mutation models do not directly optimize binding, while affinity optimization often neglects realistic mutation trajectories. We introduce iGC (in silico Germinal Centers), a two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) framework for antigen-specific antibody optimization. Stage 1 learns an SHM mutation prior from parent-child pairs in B-cell lineage trees and adapts it to a target affinity-maturation dataset. Stage 2 initializes an RL policy from this prior and optimizes antigen binding with Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularization toward the frozen prior. To prevent reward exploitation, including repetitive high-reward substitutions and structurally tolerated framework mutations, we introduce a Reward-Tax Meta-Gradient objective. RewardNet smooths the affinity landscape, while TaxNet penalizes reward overexploitation, jointly defining a learned meta-reward for policy optimization. We evaluate iGC on mutation prediction, SHM consistency, affinity optimization, and mutation diversity. The mutation prior more than doubles the strongest baseline in mutation-site prediction. Following RL optimization, iGC maintains SHM sequence structure. It also achieves a strong exploration-exploitation balance, yielding competitive reward-diversity products across candidate-selection cutoffs. These results demonstrate that iGC integrates biologically plausible mutation generation with antigen-specific affinity selection, providing an in silico framework for iterative antibody affinity maturation.


### [142] [De Rham cohomological architectures for PINNs and PDEs with topological holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05276)
*Andrew Gracyk*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We attempt to alleviate failure modes of classical physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) in scenarios of topological pathologies corresponding to punctures, holes, poles, and voids by introducing de Rham cohomological augmentations, or Hodge-de Rham PINNs. Vanilla PINNs often encounter pitfalls in hole-based settings due to discontinuous interfaces, nonzero closed path integration, path dependence, correspondence to exact forms via the exterior derivative, etc., therefore we absolve traditional failures via parameterizing a 1-form by the Hodge-Morrey-Friedrichs (HMF) decomposition for manifolds with boundary, drawing a connection to de Rham cohomology such as through the HMF isomorphism theorem. 1-forms can act as representations of scalar-valued PDE solutions through integral operator mappings. We propose performing a PINN-based loss on the parameterized 1-form through an associated PDE, which subsequently solves the potential-based PINN problem and moreover remedies the failures of vanilla PINNs via the Sobolev-regular operator under a Fréchet differentiation interconnection. The operator relationship often has a nontrivial closed-form and often requires integration or a case-by-case analysis, which we address thoroughly in our experiments. When an integrability condition is not met on our losses, we can equip exhaustion functions to train the networks. We demonstrate our methods on the Aharonov-Bohm effect, which, to our understanding, is largely nonexistent in PINN literature. We develop theoretical guarantees of our methods and demonstrate our pathological safeguards on a broad selection of topologically-adverse problems, including scenarios of inexact forms, and those of nontrivial harmonics and coexact terms.


### [143] [Universal Hardness From Masking Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05296)
*Parikshit Gopalan, Manoj Prabhakaran, Pranay Tankala*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We prove a broad learning-theoretic generalization of the celebrated hardcore lemma concerning the average-case complexity of computing a Boolean function (Impagliazzo, 1995). Our starting point is the observation that the classic notion of a hardcore measure, which reweights a function's input distribution, is equivalent to an input-dependent masking channel that injects randomness into the function's output distribution until it appears fully pseudorandom. The analogue of density of the measure is the fidelity of the channel. From this viewpoint, we develop a general framework for certifying hardness that allows for non-Boolean functions $f$ and a wide range of notions of hardness besides the classic 0-1 loss, by choosing the right notion of fidelity via convex duality. We show the difficulty of learning $f$ using a class $\mathcal{C}$ to minimize a convex loss is tightly characterized by the maximum fidelity possible for a masking channel, which measures the least amount of noise the channel must add to hide the structure of $f$ and make it $\mathcal{C}$-indistinguishable from random noise. Using our framework, we derive a number of multiclass generalizations of the hardcore lemma, and reveal some unexpected subtleties in going to larger alphabets. We construct a universal masking channel that applies to many natural notions of hardness simultaneously. Such channels are dual to omnipredictors, which optimize multiple losses at once (Gopalan, Kalai, Reingold, Sharan, and Wieder, 2022).
