We observe a symmetry between masking channels and generalizations of the hardcore lemma using pseudoentropy. We present a tight characterization of pseudoentropy using the MaxEnt algorithm from machine learning. A long line of research has shown that learning and hardness are two sides of the same coin; our framework gives a crisp and general formulation of this principle via convex duality.


### [144] [LoopVQ: Sub 1-bit KV Cache Quantization for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05304)
*Minsoo Cheong, Sungjoo Yoo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped transformers are a parameter-efficient alternative to standard transformers for language modeling. By reapplying the same layers across multiple loop steps, recent looped language models match the performance of non-looped models several times their size. While this architecture effectively reduces parameter memory, each loop step still stores its own keys and values, so the key-value (KV) cache grows linearly with the number of loop steps and becomes the main memory bottleneck. We observe that the KV cache of looped models is highly redundant along the loop axis and that the loop steps differ substantially in their sensitivity to quantization. Based on these observations, we propose $\textbf{LoopVQ}$, a KV cache vector quantization method for looped transformers that codes channels of multiple loop steps jointly, and fine-tunes the codebooks to match the output of the full-precision model. At 0.5 bits per element, LoopVQ outperforms existing KV cache vector quantization methods for standard LLMs on general, long chain-of-thought reasoning, and long-context retrieval tasks with Ouro and Huginn models. Its design is orthogonal to these methods, and combined with TaSQ, it retains much of the BF16 accuracy even at 0.25 bits per element. We also implement LoopVQ in SGLang, where it nearly halves the decode latency at a 32K-token context and raises the peak serving throughput by up to $4.3\times$ and $6.1\times$ on the 1.4B and 2.6B Ouro models compared to the BF16 baseline.


### [145] [$Φ$asmaNet: A Staged Spectral Token-Refinement Architecture for Fast Gravitational-Wave Signal-to-Noise Ratio Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05309)
*Ioannis Tsioulis, Anastasios Tefas, Nikolaos Stergioulas*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Quantifying selection effects in gravitational-wave population studies requires millions of costly signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) calculations. We present $\Phi$asmaNet, a compact neural architecture for structured tabular regression, with staged spectral refinement as an explicit architectural prior. Learned input tokens are refined through ordered attention-based $\Phi$-Stages, each augmented by a $\Phi$-Refractor subspace-mixing module, then revisited through gated top-down $\Phi$-Feedback. Applied to the 11-dimensional parameters of precessing, higher-mode binary black hole signals (IMRPhenomXPHM), it predicts phase-averaged optimal SNR for a single detector at fixed Advanced LIGO design sensitivity. With approximately 732k parameters, it outperforms MLP, 1D-ResNet and Transformer-encoder baselines under matched training across $10^5$--$10^6$ samples. At $10^6$ samples, it achieves $100-\mathrm{MAPE}=98.73\%$ on the held-out test set, $99.29\%$ after rank-preserving monotonic (PCHIP) calibration, and $99.39\%$ ($R^2=0.9998$) in the detection-relevant regime $\rho>8$, with stable accuracy across independent dataset realizations. On a single GPU, it sustains approximately $1.2\times10^5$ SNR evaluations per second and processes a $10^6$-injection catalogue end-to-end in approximately 8.3 s, about two orders of magnitude faster than conventional waveform-and-inner-product calculations on a 16-core CPU. Exact distance rescaling through $\rho\propto1/d_L$ supports repeated catalogue-wide detectability evaluations. The fully documented pipeline is intended as a reproducible benchmark, while results on public tabular datasets indicate competitiveness beyond gravitational-wave applications. Although demonstrated for a fixed single-detector configuration, the construction supports extensions to detector networks and epoch-specific sensitivities. (Abridged)


### [146] [CATF: A Manager-Worker Framework for Context-Aware Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05325)
*Yueqiao Chen, Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study context-aware time-series forecasting, in which a single target variable is predicted from its own history and a set of contextual variables, such as weather and calendar information. As the context becomes more diverse, a single model struggles to capture all of its regimes, while ensemble learning and Mixture-of-Experts approaches rely on manual data partitioning or soft routing, which increases the inference cost and hinders interpretability. We propose CATF, a Context-Aware Time-Series Forecasting framework built on a Manager-Worker architecture: a Transformer-based Manager routes each input to a single Worker based on the context, using hard routing during both training and inference. The Workers thus specialize without manual data partitioning, and any single-model forecaster can serve as a Worker. On five context-rich datasets, four horizons, and two normalization schemes, CATF improves over its standalone counterpart in 162 of 200 comparisons, the best CATF-enhanced model outperforms the best standalone baseline in 19 of 20 settings, and it outperforms the zero-shot Time-MoE foundation model in 15 of 20 settings. A case study on bike-sharing data shows that the Manager routes inputs by the calendar and that each Worker specializes in a different weekly pattern.


### [147] [Agnostic Learning by Imitating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05346)
*Sílvia Casacuberta, Parikshit Gopalan, Varun Kanade, Omer Reingold, Konstantinos Stavropoulos, Pranay Tankala*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Given a class of machine learning models and sample access to a data distribution with binary labels, which computational task is harder: training a discriminative model that distinguishes positive and negative labels as well as the best member of the class, or training a generative model whose outputs are indistinguishable from real data by all members of the class? We prove that these two fundamental tasks in computational learning theory are not just closely related, but actually equivalent to each other in complexity. One direction of this equivalence was previously known. Indeed, the powerful regularity lemma in complexity theory gives an efficient oracle reduction from the indistinguishability task (a.k.a. multiaccuracy) to the distinguishing task (a.k.a. agnostic learning) in the style of boosting algorithms. Our main contribution is an efficient oracle reduction in the other direction. Taking this idea further, we show that a multiaccuracy oracle is a remarkably powerful primitive, achieving not just agnostic learning but also a wide range of seemingly more demanding learning objectives via similarly efficient and direct constructions. Our results crucially use the power of making adaptive queries to the oracle, and stand in stark contrast to prior work, which showed that post-processing a single multiaccurate predictor is insufficient for even weak forms of learning. On a technical level, we draw a connection between our (offline) problem and a version of no-regret learning in the online setting, which allows us to use follow-the-leader type algorithms to modify the label distribution and aggregate the predictions of the multiaccuracy oracle.


### [148] [Aligning Language Models with General Preferences: An Empirical Study of Direct Nash Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05373)
*Austin Tsai, Saurav Nanda, Ganapathy Parthasarathy, Bhuvnesh Kumar, Parivesh Choudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Direct Nash Optimization (DNO) aligns a language model to a general preference function by approximating the Nash equilibrium of a two-player preference game. Its practical variant, DNO-Prct, iterates on-policy sampling, LLM-based rating of the samples together with a teacher's gold response, large-margin preference-pair selection and a DPO-style contrastive update. We re-implement DNO-Prct independently and study it on two open-weight instruct models, Llama 3.1 8B Instruct and Mistral 7B Instruct v0.1, with GPT-4o or a larger open-weight model of the same family as the annotator. On AlpacaEval 2.0 with GPT-4o as judge, three iterations raise the length-controlled (LC) win rate against GPT-4o from 31.7% to 45.3% for Llama and from 6.8% to 18.4% for Mistral, and the tuned 8B model scores above Llama 3.1 70B Instruct on every reported win rate. With same-family open-weight annotators, the runs retain 62% (Llama) and 82% (Mistral) of the LC gain achieved with GPT-4o as annotator, so the rating step does not require a proprietary model. In a separate ablation configuration, variants that remove the gold responses, remove the data look-back, or accept only teacher-preferred pairs score 6 to 10 LC points below the full procedure, whereas varying the number of on-policy samples per prompt (1 to 7) moves the LC win rate by at most 2.1 points, and a margin sweep (1 to 5) run in its own pipeline by at most 2.4 points. We also report the cost of the pipeline and where the practical algorithm departs from the theory it approximates. All results are single runs, and GPT-4o is both the teacher and the evaluation judge; we discuss what this implies for the reported gains.


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [149] [INSIGHT-HPC: An Interpretable System Intelligence for Global HPC Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03875)
*Rupak Kadel, Jie Li, Yong Chen*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Modern high-performance computing (HPC) clusters integrate multi-generation CPUs and GPUs, which generate rich, high-dimensional telemetry streams from out-of-band baseboard management controllers (BMC), job schedulers, and facility power distribution units. Detecting anomalies within this telemetry is critical to prevent energy waste and costly node failures. Existing approaches often rely primarily on statistical deviations and require stronger labels than actual production environments can provide. In addition, these detectors have a limited operational context for interpreting alerts. We present INSIGHT-HPC, a multi-plane anomaly-detection architecture that combines statistical, workload-aware, and physics-guided evidence into operator-interpretable episodes. INSIGHT-HPC integrates heterogeneous, large-scale telemetry streams and transforms noisy minute-resolution observations into de-duplicated fault episodes with operator-facing explanations. This design allows the system to distinguish workload-explained behavior from genuine infrastructure anomalies while producing interpretable alert summaries for operators. On production HPC telemetry with weak operational labels and controlled HPC-relevant fault injections, INSIGHT-HPC achieves a stronger precision-recall balance than all evaluated baselines. The system achieves a precision of 0.913, a recall of 0.817, an F1 score of 0.862, and an AUROC of 0.896. These results demonstrate the value of combining workload context, physical consistency, and explainable episode-level fusion.


### [150] [EpiLoRA: Fusing Many LoRA Adapters into Low-Precision GEMMs for Serving and RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03899)
*Hieu Tran, Hong Yu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Low-rank adapters (LoRA) let one base model serve many fine-tuned variants: multi-tenant engines keep a single copy of the base and batch the requests of many tenants, each with its own adapter. As base models move to 8-bit (FP8) and 4-bit formats, their general matrix multiplications (GEMMs) become cheaper, but the adapters do not: engines run them as separate small GPU kernels beside the GEMM, whose latency takes a growing share of every decoding step. An adapter projects its input down to a low rank and back up, and our key observation is that the GEMM needs the down-projection only in its epilogue, the final stage in which it writes its output. EpiLoRA, a plugin for the vLLM engine, therefore launches the down-projection first and the low-precision GEMM as a dependent kernel that streams its weights meanwhile and adds every tenant's up-projection in its epilogue. On NVIDIA Hopper GPUs, EpiLoRA decodes for a single adapter with up to 24% lower time per token than vLLM's fastest configuration, and faster than SGLang and TensorRT-LLM; with many tenants, its adapter kernels deliver more throughput than vLLM's on most GPU-model pairs we tested. The same low-precision base also trains: a LoRA reinforcement-learning trainer that computes on the engine's FP8 weights needs no 16-bit copy, so a 27B model trains on one 48 GB GPU, and an engine that then keeps activations in 16 bits removes most of the remaining mismatch between the model generating training data and the one trained.


### [151] [PhotonZip: Lifecycle-Aware Compression for HDF5-Based Synchrotron Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04138)
*Haoxu Li, Wenjing Huang, Chengyu Liu, Shiyuan Fu, Hao Hu, Guangming Tan, Dingwen Tao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Synchrotron detectors generate data at rates that challenge storage and I/O systems. Compression can reduce data movement, but codec-only measurements do not capture the end-to-end behavior of HDF5 workflows. We present PhotonZip, a lifecycle-aware compression framework that tests candidate modes through a common HDF5 path and selects among eligible measured configurations according to either compression ratio or round-trip throughput. Its modular filters preserve standard HDF5 access when the required plugin is available. Across four beamline-derived datasets, PhotonZip reaches lossless compression ratios up to 1.629$\times$ and HDF5 write throughput up to 3.40GB/s write and 7.75GB/s read. The throughput-oriented configuration achieves 1.529$\times$ lossless data reduction while improving HDF5 write and read throughput by 21.2\% and 15.0\%, respectively. Lossy preview modes provide up to 16$\times$ data reduction and reduce preview latency by up to 8.35~s in the evaluated file-per-rank HDF5 configuration.


### [152] [When the Consumer Is an Agent: Execution-Grounded Service-Level Agreements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04182)
*Michal Orzechowski, Renata G. Slota, Jacek Kitowski*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly act as service consumers. They select operations and report whether a task is complete. In an asynchronous distributed service, an accepted call or immediate state read does not establish that the required state became observable by its deadline. We introduce execution-grounded completion contracts, which treat completion as a service-level outcome. An independent control plane observes a deadline-bound service-state predicate, admits completion, and directs recovery while the agent performs the task. We evaluate placement and deletion in a live multi-provider data service with heterogeneous models and an injected transfer delay. Across policies, some tasks that missed the deadline were accepted and some that met it were rejected. Under enforcement, accepted completions remained consistent with later observations; all included healthy tasks completed, and unresolved faulted tasks terminated as unverified. The results show that completion of these tasks can be governed by independent observation of service state rather than by the agent's self-assessment.


### [153] [GC-MoE: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with FP8/FP4 Grouped GEMM via CUDA Green Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04222)
*Yong Li, Kan Liu, Tao Lan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Under expert parallelism, mixture-of-experts (MoE) layers pay all-to-all token dispatch, grouped GEMMs, and a combine step in every layer. In decode each rank holds only 1-256 tokens: dispatch latency and weight traffic dominate while tensor-core utilization is inherently low. In fused operators such as DeepGEMM's Mega MoE, dispatch and computation overlap only by sharing every SM: dispatch warps compete with TMA/MMA and epilogue warps for registers, shared memory, and issue slots; every phase shares one CTA resource budget, synchronization spans the whole SM array, and every replay ends with a workspace-clean pass and a cross-rank barrier. We present GC-MoE, an open-source FP8/FP4 MoE inference operator for NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs that uses CUDA Green Contexts to split the device into a small communication partition and a large compute partition. Each role gets its own kernel, resource budget, and synchronization domain, so dispatch and GEMM tune independently. A three-node CUDA Graph (four on Hopper with fused shared experts) launches the dispatch and grouped-GEMM kernels as concurrent roots; a token-ring handshake starts computation on the first filled ring blocks, and double-buffered receive counters remove the trailing cross-rank barrier between replays. The compute partition runs operand-swapped tcgen05 block-scaled MMA on Blackwell and register-A WGMMA with in-register FP4 decoding on Hopper, with fused SwiGLU, requantization, and optional shared experts. On 8 H20 GPUs GC-MoE accelerates DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash decode by 1.188x on average on both the routed and shared-expert suites (peaks 1.296x and 1.353x); on 8 B300 GPUs it accelerates GLM-5.2 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash by 1.106x (1.123x with fused shared experts, peak 1.209x), beating Mega MoE at all 54 measured points at matched accuracy. GC-MoE reserves only 20 of 148 SMs on B300 for dispatch.


### [154] [Beyond Compression Ratio: High-Throughput KV Compression for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04382)
*Junhong Wu, Zedong Liu, Xinyang Ma, Yuhan Chen, Guangming Tan, Dingwen Tao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: KV cache compression reduces the communication volume of disaggregated large language model serving, but its practical benefit depends not only on compression ratio and model quality, but also on how efficiently the compression pipeline executes. This requirement becomes increasingly important as modern serving systems adopt high-bandwidth interconnects, where inefficient compression can offset a substantial portion of the saved communication time. This paper presents \system, a high-throughput KV cache compression data path built on top of \kvserve. \system improves compression execution in two complementary ways. First, it fuses the distribution transformation and low-bit quantization stages into a KV-aware \tilelang kernel, eliminating intermediate tensor materialization and redundant memory accesses. Second, it replaces the original lossless compression backend with an open, extensible, and searchable backend based on \lc, enabling high-performance compression operator pipelines. The fused kernel achieves up to $5.9\times$ speedup, while the new lossless backend increases compression throughput from below 90~GB/s to over 500~GB/s. Across the evaluated workloads, \system reduces job completion time by up to $2.04\times$. End-to-end evaluation shows that these optimizations allow KV compression to retain meaningful communication benefits under modern high-bandwidth network.


### [155] [LiquidCP: Decoupling Attention Tasks from Communication and Kernel Boundaries in Context Parallelism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04401)
*Hongyu Chen, Yikang Ruan, Yuhai Long, Ziyu Huang, Yiyu Luo, Liukun Yu, Yangjie Zhou, Zihan Liu, Weihao Cui, Zijun Li, Anbang Wu, Yu Feng, Jing Yang, Jingwen Leng, Bingsheng He, Minyi Guo, Shixuan Sun*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Context parallelism (CP) partitions long sequences across GPUs for LLM training and inference. For variable-length training and full prefill, attention-balanced placement can leave token loads uneven, while ring execution repeats overhead at each computation and communication step. In stage-serialized decode CP (DCP), long-running tasks delay dependent work and leave substantial GPU hardware idle. We propose LiquidCP, which unifies training and inference through a common model of Transfer, Compute, and Reduction tasks with tile-level dependencies, decoupling attention tasks from communication and kernel boundaries. For training and full prefill, MegaRing incorporates BuddyRing placement to balance attention and token loads, and coalesces ready KV regions during forward attention to amortize overhead. For decoding and mixed batches, MegaDCP selects history-KV splits by jointly modeling the completion of attention and its dependent reductions. A shared persistent GPU-resident engine executes both designs. Compared with the fastest baseline per configuration on eight H100 GPUs, MegaRing achieves geometric-mean core-attention speedups of $1.23\times$ forward and $1.18\times$ backward, and accelerates a Transformer layer by $1.08$--$1.16\times$. MegaDCP achieves geometric-mean core-attention speedups of $1.20\times$ for decode-only and $1.35\times$ for mixed batches. In vLLM serving with KV prefix cache, it achieves an average time-to-first-token speedup of $1.22\times$.


### [156] [Minimizing Bottleneck Overload Risk in Online Edge Task Placement under Workload Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04425)
*Lei Wang, Winston K. G. Seah, Gang Xu, Xiaodong Xu, Baoqi Huang*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: In edge computing, tasks must be placed on servers of finite capacity before workloads are realized, often with only workload means and variances known. An assignment balanced on expected load can still leave a server with more fluctuations than its residual capacity can absorb. Per-server risk constraints can enforce a prescribed overload tolerance but do not distinguish the assignments that meet it by how close the riskiest server comes to the limit. We show that, for independent tasks, placement preserves a period's total variance and total residual capacity, and decides only how fluctuation, measured by standard deviation, is paired with residual capacity across servers. The risk ratio, the ratio of fluctuation to residual capacity, parameterizes a server's worst-case overload probability over all aggregate workload distributions with given mean and variance. We minimize the largest risk ratio, attained at the risk bottleneck. Risk-Bottleneck Placement (RBP) places tasks in descending order of standard deviation, each on the server with the smallest post-assignment risk ratio, and refines the assignment with relocations and swaps within a finite per-period online budget. On small instances solved exactly by enumerating every assignment, minimizing the maximum expected utilization leaves the largest risk ratio 25.66-29.19% above its exact minimum on average, even with risk-minimizing tie-breaking, and the corresponding gap for RBP is 1.77-2.64%. In trace-derived multi-period simulations at two workload pressures, RBP reduces the worst-server overload probability by 26.0% and 18.0% relative to the best baseline and remains the lowest at every tested pressure and scale.


### [157] [EvoCG: Self-Evolving Precision Control for Faster Conjugate Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04469)
*Jiaqi Zhai, Haozhi Han, Kaiyi Huang, Wenju Zhao, Yifeng Chen, Yunquan Zhang, Ting Cao, Yunxin Liu, Hai Jin, Xuanhua Shi, Kun Li*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Modern GPUs offer far higher throughput in low precision than in FP64, but exploiting this in conjugate gradient (CG) solvers is difficult because tolerance to reduced precision evolves with the Krylov state. We present EvoCG, a mixed-precision CG solver that treats tile-wise precision as an online control action rather than a fixed setting: a reinforcement-learning policy in the iteration loop selects per-tile precision from the evolving solver state, made profitable by co-design with a Tile-CSC unified mixed-precision SpMV kernel and a low-overhead in-loop runtime. Across 47 SPD matrices from eight scientific domains, EvoCG is the fastest solver overall, with geometric-mean speedups of 2.52x, 3.06x, 2.48x, 1.93x, and 6.99x over cuSPARSE, PETSc, Ginkgo, Mille-FP64, and Mille-Mixed. On the same substrate, it improves time-to-solution over EvoCG-FP64 by 1.28x at only 11.4% more iterations and 1.09% overhead, with FP64-recomputed final true-residual norms nearly identical to those of FP64, and extends to Jacobi-preconditioned CG without retraining, yielding a further 1.15x geometric-mean speedup over EvoCG-FP64.


### [158] [Distributed LTL Synthesis under Arbitrary Message Adversaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04561)
*Benedikt Bollig, Patricia Bouyer-Decitre, Matthias Függer, Dhrubajyoti Ghosh, Thomas Nowak, Nathalie Sznajder*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Reactive synthesis is the problem of generating a program that produces outputs satisfying a logical specification for every input stream. We study distributed synthesis for two processes communicating in rounds over unreliable links, with at least one message delivered in every round. Despite this delivery guarantee, we show that synthesis from linear temporal logic specifications is undecidable, even when messages carry the processes' complete causal histories. More generally, we give a complete characterization of the communication restrictions that make synthesis decidable. Restrictions are described by a message adversary $A$, a set of allowed infinite graph histories: sequences of communication graphs, each specifying which messages are delivered in a round. Iterated chromatic subdivision associates these histories with points in the unit interval, yielding an image $\pi(A) \subseteq [0,1]$. For every $\omega$-regular message adversary $A$, synthesis is decidable if and only if $\pi(A)$ is nowhere dense. In the decidable cases, our procedure takes a deterministic parity automaton for $A$ and a specification, decides realizability, and constructs a realizing distributed algorithm whenever one exists. Further, our topological arguments shed new light on the fundamental requirements of synthesis. They show that, for $\omega$-regular message adversaries, synthesis is decidable if and only if consensus is repeatedly solvable in bounded time. This links two previously unrelated problems from formal verification and distributed computing through the processes' uncertainty about communication.


### [159] [EvoCache: Accelerating Browser Large Language Model Delivery with Public Cache Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04638)
*Zhiyu Wang, Rajkumar Buyya*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can run directly in a browser, but first use typically requires downloading model files that are hundreds of MiB or even several GiB in size. The same LLM is often repackaged by different publishers; these files share substantial content, yet byte differences prevent whole-file caches from reusing them. We propose EvoCache, a browser-managed LLM delivery system. EvoCache uses public cache anchors that are stored in advance by the browser and independent of browsing history to reconstruct losslessly the publisher version requested by a site. A site specifies only the target, while the browser selects the anchor and performs reconstruction, keeping both hidden from the site. An audit of 1,000 public repositories finds that 61 of the 100 most popular LLMs are offered by multiple publishers and that 98.1% of overlapping versions cannot be reused by whole-file caching. Across 23 cross-publisher model-package pairs, EvoCache achieves a median traffic reduction of 77.3% and outperforms every comparator. Across 240 real end-to-end browser experiments, EvoCache reduces network traffic by 78.9% and page-ready time by 64.2% relative to ordinary full downloads. For cached data left by other sites, EvoCache also protects users' cross-site cache privacy by using the same return time for cache hits and misses.


### [160] [LeanSparse: Memory-Efficient Serving for Native Sparse-Attention LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04671)
*Zaifeng Pan, Chris Wu, Xinwei Qiang, Zhengding Hu, Zhongkai Yu, Yufei Ding*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Sparse attention limits the history read by each query, but serving long contexts still requires substantial GPU memory. Existing KV offloading systems retain the full history of indexer keys on GPU, limiting decode concurrency. Prefill's temporary memory demand can further delay admission even when enough memory remains for the same request's subsequent decode. We present LeanSparse, a serving system that reduces both decode's persistent memory use and prefill's temporary memory demand. Our design exploits two observations: prefill consumes historical data one layer at a time, and decode can identify promising candidates before comparing their original indexer scores. During prefill, LeanSparse streams layer histories through two reusable GPU buffers, overlapping next-layer loading with ongoing model computation. During decode, coarse-to-fine indexing searches compact representations and retrieves original indexer keys only for candidate refinement. Separate indexer key and KV caches reuse data across generation steps. We implement LeanSparse in SGLang and evaluate it on DeepSeek-V4-Flash. At 950K context length, LeanSparse supports 2.95$\times$ as many concurrent requests and achieves 2.14$\times$ decode throughput over HiSparse. On multi-turn Open-SWE traces, it reduces median time to first token from 21.6 to 0.6s and median session completion time by 17.7% relative to ECHO.


### [161] [FenixWalk: Adaptive Sampling for Large-Scale Dynamic Graphs on Heterogeneous Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04683)
*Pinhuan Wang, Jianian Fu, Jie Zhang, Shixuan Sun, Qing Wang, Xiaoliang Wang, Chen Tian, Chengying Huan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Random walks are fundamental to graph analytics, yet executing them efficiently on large, evolving graphs remains challenging. Existing approaches must balance sampling efficiency, update overhead, and memory consumption. Sampling structures optimized for throughput often incur substantial memory overhead and costly maintenance, whereas lightweight alternatives can suffer from high sampling latency on high-degree vertices or skewed weight distributions. These challenges become more pronounced on heterogeneous CPU--GPU platforms, where host memory provides capacity but GPU memory limits the amount of graph data that can benefit from parallel acceleration. We present FenixWalk, a memory-efficient random-walk system that combines unified in-place sampling with selective GPU acceleration for large-scale dynamic graphs. At its core, FenixWalk employs an in-place Fenwick representation that supports both rejection sampling and inverse-transform sampling without duplicating edge-level weight storage. This representation enables expected O(1) weight lookup for rejection sampling, together with O(log d) prefix sampling and updates. To exploit heterogeneous hardware efficiently, FenixWalk maintains the complete graph and its sampling structures in host memory while selectively placing frequently reused vertex-local samplers on the GPU. By transferring compact walker states instead of repeatedly migrating graph partitions, the system reduces cross-device communication and makes effective use of limited GPU memory. Experiments on large real-world graphs and representative random-walk workloads demonstrate that FenixWalk achieves average speedups ranging from 2.06x to 400.57x over existing random-walk systems.


### [162] [Self-stabilising approximate clock synchronisation requires linear time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04725)
*Joel Rybicki*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Synchronising the clocks of processes is a fundamental problem in distributed computing. We consider message-passing systems in which processes start in an arbitrary initial state (self-stabilisation) and up to $f$ processes may be non-transiently faulty. It is well known that synchronising clocks \emph{exactly} is at least as hard as consensus and requires $\Theta(f)$ time. In contrast, without self-stabilisation, clocks can be synchronised \emph{approximately} much faster, in time independent of the number of non-transient faults [Srikanth and Toueg, JACM 1987; Welch and Lynch, Inf. Comput. 1988]. In this work, we show that deterministic self-stabilising approximate clock synchronisation tasks require $\Omega(f)$ time to stabilise. This gives the first non-trivial time lower bound for these tasks, whose time complexity has remained open for over two decades [Dolev and Welch, JACM 2004; Dolev, Függer, Schmid and Lenzen, JACM 2014]. In particular, this answers the open question whether self-stabilising approximate clock synchronisation tasks are as hard as consensus or as easy as approximate agreement [Lenzen and Rybicki, JACM 2019]. The results show that guaranteeing \emph{both} transient and non-transient fault-tolerance simultaneously is expensive even for approximate synchronisation tasks. The lower bounds hold in a very strong model: the faulty processes can only crash, the local clocks of processes have no clock drift, and the message delay is bounded and known to all processes. The proof is based on a combinatorial topological argument showing that no fast finite-state protocol can ensure that the clocks behave consistently across all executions. To our knowledge, this is the first use of topological arguments to prove time lower bounds in the self-stabilising setting.


### [163] [A RISC-V Simulator for the Tenstorrent Tensix Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04740)
*Nick Brown, Daniyal Arshad*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: RISC-V based AI accelerators have become popular and not only for AI and ML, but also as vehicles for exploring HPC workloads more generally. Tenstorrent are one example, and whilst they document their Tensix architecture in detail and develop their software stack openly, running code has historically required a physical card, and their recently released simulator targets hardware equivalence rather than introspection. In this paper we present Wolfpine, a community developed RISC-V simulator for the Tensix architecture, supporting both the Wormhole and Blackhole generations and integrating seamlessly with the TT-Metal software stack. Constructed entirely from publicly available documentation, open source software and measurements on physical hardware, Wolfpine is written in pure Python for readability and ease of modification. An opt-in, provenance-tracked cost model adds timing fidelity incrementally, and typed event streams, per-component tracing and timeline export enable programmers to identify and diagnose performance bottlenecks that the hardware itself hides.


### [164] [Replica-Aware Split Inference for Edge IoT Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04779)
*Zied Jenhani, Nasrin Razmi, Admela Jukan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Split Inference (SI) addresses the hardware resource limitations of Internet of Things (IoT) devices by distributing tiny Machine Learning (TinyML) model segments across multiple IoT devices. However, a device failure can interrupt the inference pipeline. This paper proposes a replica-aware recovery approach that deploys replicas of TinyML model segments originally hosted on failed devices onto surviving devices. We formulate the joint selection of replica hosts and communication protocols as a mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) problem that minimizes post-reconfiguration inference latency, accounting for computation and transmission latencies, under flash memory and runtime memory constraints. Simulations using MobileNetV2 on heterogeneous IoT networks compare the proposed approach with four baselines. We implement the model in a network of 15 IoT devices with a maximum link-rate reduction of 75%, and show that MILP approach achieves an average modeled end-to-end inference latency that is 6.8% lower than that of Computation Greedy and 18.3% lower than that of Random. Index Terms TinyML, split inference, Internet of Things, fault tolerance, replica placement.


### [165] [Accelerating Offloading-Based MoE Inference by Interleaving Concurrent Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04794)
*Hongseung Yu, In Gim, Kyunghan Lee, Lin Zhong*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Expert offloading enables large mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models to run locally, but conventional batching stalls the GPU while missing experts are fetched. A popular remedy, known as prefetching, is to predict what experts will be needed soon and fetch them using idle link bandwidth. Imprecise prediction, however, wastes bandwidth and GPU memory on fetching unnecessary experts. Our key finding is that prefetching can be harmful when there is an expert cache in GPU memory. This is because as the cache includes more necessary experts, expert prediction becomes less precise. Our key insight is that proper request scheduling beats prefetching. Building on this insight, we present Bevy, an MoE inference system based on runahead inference for concurrent requests. A request whose experts are available can run ahead to later layers while other requests wait for fetches, revealing the experts it needs next. Bevy can then fetch the missing experts so identified while computing ready requests, overlapping computation and fetching without predicting expert selections. Running requests separately, however, repeats reads of shared weights in GPU memory and increases compute time. To limit this cost, Bevy's self-balancing scheduler batches ready requests at each layer and prioritizes lagging requests, adapting their grouping as expert availability changes. Across two local platforms, Bevy delivers up to 3.46$\times$ the decode throughput of this http URL and up to 1.55$\times$ that of the fastest prefetching baseline.


### [166] [TapeSAC: Automated, Pareto-Optimal Fine-Grained Memory Management for Large Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04897)
*Kaihui Ren, Yifan Zhao, Zejian Wang, Yingtong Xiong, Xiaoling Zhou, Daohong Xu, Chenhao Guan, Zhikang Mo, Jihu Guo, Zhuoru Ma, Yufeng Li, Lin Zhang, Yiqiao Liao, Chengru Song*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Selective activation checkpointing (SAC) decides which activations to save for the backward pass and which to recompute: each choice is a point in the plane of peak memory and recomputation time, and the goal is that plane's Pareto frontier. Current practice falls short of this goal. Hand-tuned policies fix a single point that does not adapt when the model, batch or kernels change; PyTorch's SAC reruns each block's forward pass in program order, recomputing values the backward pass never reads; and the memory budget of this http URL treats recomputation as free or forbidden and selects saved tensors by bytes alone, an assumption that fails for the custom kernels that produce many of a large model's activations. We present TapeSAC, which automates SAC in eager PyTorch without editing the model's forward code. TapeSAC records each block's forward pass at the dispatcher and, during backward, replays only the producers of the values autograd consumes, so saving a tensor eliminates every producer needed only for it. A planner selects the saved tensors from measured recomputation costs under a single limit on step-peak memory. On Qwen3.8-27B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, replay alone reduces step time by 3.0% and 1.7% versus full checkpointing at no greater memory cost; memory-aware planning raises these gains to 6.3% and 13.9% under a 90%-of-device-memory limit. The evaluated hand-tuned policies fall short of this memory-recomputation frontier and, on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, run out of memory at larger batches. Over 500 steps on real data, TapeSAC reduces training time by 8-11%; on Qwen3.5-397B, it is 2.7% faster than a model-specific hand-tuned policy while using 14% less memory.


### [167] [Local Distributed Computation under Crash Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04898)
*Diana Ghinea, Fabian Kuhn, Chen-Da Liu-Zhang, Zahra Parsaeian*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: We study deterministic algorithms for locally checkable graph problems in the synchronous LOCAL model with crash failures, where a crashing node may send messages to only some neighbors. We introduce consistent neighborhoods: partial radius-$R$ views that agree locally across surviving edges and are complete without crashes. For $R=T+\ell+1$, these views allow local simulation of $T$-round fault-free algorithms that remain correct on subgraphs, satisfying constraints wherever the radius-$\ell$ checking neighborhood contains no crash. On graphs with at most $n$ nodes and maximum degree at most $\Delta\geq3$, we construct these views in $O(\min\{n,(\Delta-1)^R,R\Delta f\})$ rounds when a crash bound $f\geq1$ is globally known; the first two bounds require no knowledge of $f$. For $\Delta=2$ or girth greater than $2R$, including forests, $O(R)$ rounds suffice. Our upper bounds are essentially tight in suitable parameter regimes. One crash can force $\Omega(n)$ rounds even for $R=3$ on diameter-two graphs. For each fixed $\Delta\geq3$ and sufficiently large $R$, we prove a $(\Delta-1)^{R-O(\sqrt R)}$ lower bound even with crashes restricted to round beginnings. We also prove an $\Omega(\Delta Rf)$ lower bound when $R\geq4\lceil\log_{\Delta-1}f\rceil+4$ and $n\geq\Delta Rf$. For locally checkable labeling (LCL) problems, we preserve $O(\log^* n)$ complexity under any number of crashes whenever this bound holds without faults on all graphs with a fixed degree bound. In contrast, we construct an LCL on graphs of maximum degree at most five with fault-free complexity $\Theta(\log n)$ and crash-tolerant complexity $\Theta(n)$. Finally, any fault-free sinkless-orientation algorithm can be transformed to tolerate any number of crashes with only one additional round, guaranteeing correctness even at surviving nodes adjacent to crashes.


### [168] [ForgeKernel: Self-Evolving Kernels Catching and Surpassing Experts and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04998)
*Yaojian Chen, Dazhi Peng, Yiyun Zheng, Zhui Zhu, Shengye Xiang, Leshan Li, Zhen Li, Xianglong Deng, Haojun Sun, Qingfeng He, Shangzhan Li, Yifei Shen, Lin Gan, Yuxuan Li, Xu Han, Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Purpose-built kernel agents can write high-performance GPU kernels, but existing systems generally lack cross-run knowledge accumulation and architectural evolution. Each run starts from the same initial knowledge, and later iterations largely optimize within an architecture fixed early in the search. We present ForgeKernel, a kernel-generation system that accumulates and reuses knowledge across runs while enabling continued evolution of kernel architectures. It analyzes the hardware behavior of a high-performance reference and distills validated architectural decisions into reusable knowledge of hardware mechanisms rather than imitating source code. It then seals a kernel that passes correctness and performance validation and uses it as the reference for the next optimization run, enabling architectural decisions to be revisited. Across 27 operators and 29 input configurations on NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, ForgeKernel matches or beats its expert reference on 25 configurations, with a geometric-mean performance gain of 10%. On Ascend 910C, it outperforms the per-operator public best on the public case set of all 8 level-4 operators in the CANN-Bench Season 1 final leaderboard, with a geometric-mean gain of 25.1%, while its full-suite submission ranks first on the A3 leaderboard. As of 2026-10-30, public submissions generated by ForgeKernel rank first on all three CANN-Bench 2.0 solution leaderboards (Ascend 910C, 950DT, and 950PR) and on the SOL-ExecBench FlashInfer-Bench track.


### [169] [Characterizing Serverless Workloads: A Large-Scale Analysis of Function-as-a-Service Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05081)
*Saman Akbari, Yassine Lazreg, Mohammadreza Malekabbasi, Manfred Hauswirth*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Serverless computing is a popular approach to application deployment without having to manage the underlying infrastructure. Its most common implementation is Function-as-a-Service (FaaS), in which applications are structured as fine-grained functions. FaaS providers face non-trivial tasks in the management of their platforms, including cold starts, auto-scaling, and scheduling. The design of optimized platforms requires an accurate understanding of realistic serverless workloads. However, existing research and platforms rely on insights from single, often outdated datasets. In this work, we conduct a large-scale, cross-platform comparative analysis of eight publicly available FaaS workload traces from major cloud providers (Alibaba, Azure, Huawei, IBM) and a scientific computing platform (Globus). We provide a comprehensive and up-to-date view of the serverless ecosystem by characterizing key dimensions such as invocation patterns, latency, cold start behavior, and resource utilization. Our analysis shows that although invocation patterns remain universal, execution times have shortened, shifting the performance bottleneck toward platform scheduling and dependency management. We also observe an architectural shift toward "Serverless 2.0" models, where concurrent, long-lived containers are increasingly utilized to bypass cold start penalties. Furthermore, we found resource over-provisioning and extreme skew in user request volumes. These findings provide actionable insights for designing more efficient FaaS platforms, informing improved approaches to targeted cold start mitigation, predictive resource management, and dependency-aware scheduling.


### [170] [Workload-Static Optical Circuit Switching For Scalable LLM Inference Decode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05104)
*Maximilian Thürmer, Nicolas Scotto di Perto, Eloise Stein, Natanijel Vasic, Mikaeil Bagherli, Zhenguo Wu, Benjamin Klenk, Holger Fröning, Johannes Feldmann*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: LLM inference decode executes fine-grained collectives whose latency directly limits per-user interactivity, yet scale-up fabrics are still built from electronic packet switches designed for training. We observe that, for a given model deployment and parallelization, the communication structure of decode is fixed, and propose workload-static optical circuit switching (OCS): circuits are configured once per deployment, co-designed with model parallelism and with topology-aware collective schedules that turn spare accelerator ports into fewer communication phases or higher bandwidth. We evaluate this design with GXNET, a graph-based workload emulator coupled to analytical and flit-level network models grounded in hardware measurements. In a 576-accelerator scale-up domain serving 5T-parameter MoE models with up to 5M tokens of context, all-OCS fabrics improve interactivity by up to 65% and energy efficiency by up to 119% over 4:1 oversubscribed packet-switched fabrics.


### [171] [Distributed Linear Bandits for Freshness-Aware Preprocessing in MEC-Enabled Digital Twin Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05115)
*Mariam Yahya, Setareh Maghsudi*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Digital twins (DTs) require timely updates from Internet of Things (IoT) devices to accurately represent the state of the corresponding physical systems, but transmitting large data volumes can compromise freshness. Moreover, different task classes have distinct computational demands and freshness requirements, while stochastic task generation and sizes complicate resource allocation. In this paper, we propose a freshness-aware framework in which multi-access edge computing (MEC) servers preprocess IoT data, reducing transmission volume and DT-server processing load. The framework establishes mutual support between DT and MEC servers. The DTs determine feasible CPU-allocation strategies guaranteeing class-specific age-of-information (AoI) requirements under specified workload, delay, and update-generation bounds. MEC servers collaboratively identify the feasible strategy minimizing expected freshness-normalized preprocessing delay. We formulate CPU-strategy selection as a fixed-confidence distributed best-arm identification problem in linear bandits, with MEC servers as agents and CPU allocations across task classes as arms. We employ DistLinGapE, a distributed collaborative learning method, to coordinate exploration and share observations. Numerical results show that the method identifies the optimal feasible strategy and reduces preprocessing delay when deployed under stationary task-class assignments and workload statistics. Collaboration yields an identification speedup nearly proportional to the number of participating MEC servers in the evaluated settings.


### [172] [What Sets the Serving Speed of Looped Language Models? Measurements and Optimizations Graded by Exactness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05150)
*Dezhen Lu, Runguo Li, Zixiang Ni, Yuchen Tang, Xingquan Kong, Jiaxing Yang, Jiarui Guo, Lingxiang Gao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Looped language models apply one block of layers many times per token, so a serving engine pays every per-layer overhead once per loop; we measure serving for Ouro and Huginn-0125 in an open-source looped-model engine and grade our optimizations from bitwise-identical changes to those that require an accuracy check. At batch 1, fusing all loop submissions leaves Ouro throughput at 0.99x; the 5689 small kernels per token are the real cost, and fusing them behind rounding fences raises throughput 1.11-1.59x while 1014/1014 requests remain identical. An approximate fusion is no faster and misses a one-point GSM8K rule on 87 questions (61 and 62 correct versus 64), so we do not ship it. Huginn's released defaults avoid dead KV slots, reuse prefix-cache pages, and serve 1.77-3.16x faster than the base engine; Ouro reaches 1.65-2.15x and agent sessions 1.91-3.21x, while Huginn's noise and prefix-reuse changes stay within one point on all 1319 Huginn test questions; Huginn's packed prefill, -0.61 points on the same questions, meets that rule only on its point estimate. Position-keyed noise makes CPU cache reuse exact but still leaves 20 of 128 GPU full hits different, while loop fusion delays prefill admission and a 64-graph cache prevents eager fallbacks. Under prefill-decode disaggregation ExactLoop reaches up to 3.3x the base throughput with peer error handling disabled, and 3.1x in two of three runs with it enabled. Its transfer budget, sized to hold one request's KV in flight, cuts the commit tail from about one second under the earlier budget to 16 ms (base: 2.8 s), removing the polling rounds that the engine's interval would otherwise impose.


### [173] [Parallel Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics in Python: Task- and Data-Parallel SINDy with Numba, OpenMP, Threads and Multiprocessing for the Lorenz and Rössler Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05215)
*Pablo Guillen-Rondon*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: The sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy) recovers interpretable governing equations from time-series data by sparse regression over a library of candidate functions. As data sets, candidate libraries and the number of repeated fits grow, the cost of SINDy becomes dominated by the evaluation of the library matrix and its least-squares problems. We present an open-source Python implementation of SINDy in which a single set of loop-based kernels is just-in-time compiled with Numba and executed on a shared-memory multicore node by four interchangeable back ends: Numba prange, OpenMP directives through PyOMP, Python threads that release the global interpreter lock, and multiprocessing with shared memory. The normal equations are accumulated in one cache-blocked pass over the data, with an automatic fallback to a tall-skinny QR reduction for ill-conditioned problems. Two decompositions are studied on the Lorenz and Rössler systems: an ensemble (task-parallel) workload of 960 independent identifications and a library (data-parallel) workload of one trajectory with $2\times10^7$ samples and a fifth-order library of 56 candidate terms. All back ends recover the exact sparsity pattern in every identification, with coefficient errors identical to PySINDy. On a dual-socket Intel Xeon E5-2650 v4 node of the University of Houston-Downtown HPC cluster (24 cores, 48 hardware threads), the compiled serial code is 4.3-5.6x faster than PySINDy, the parallel back ends reach speed-ups of 14.8-20.3 on 24 cores (parallel efficiency 0.61-0.84), and the overall time to solution is reduced by up to 117x with respect to PySINDy. We analyse the differences between the back ends, the effect of the two sockets and of hyper-threading, and give practical recommendations.


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [174] [Alignment Faking Depends on the Value, Not Just the Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03650)
*Manodeep Ray, Vivek Srivastava, Shirish Karande, Murari Mandal*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Language models can follow a directive when they believe they are monitored and abandon it when they believe they are not, a pattern termed Alignment Faking. Prior work establishes this for harmful-content objectives and a handful of moral-foundations values, leaving open how it distributes across a wider value space. We argue it is value-dependent: whether compliance survives a change in perceived oversight is a property of the model--value pairing, not of the model alone. Our framework profiles Alignment Faking per value, scoring each model-value pair under three conditions: bare (no system prompt), unmonitored, and monitored. On 32 ValueBench dimensions across ten open- and closed-weight models (8B-~675B), compliance gaps span 0.05-0.50, with a distilled 70B above a ~675B model. We observe that majority of the structural variance depends on which model was asked about which value and not on the model or value alone. A six-category taxonomy over reasoning traces tracks the behavioural shift: training-signal reasoning dominates under monitoring and value-conflict reasoning dominates when unmonitored. We package per-model results as value cards, extending model cards with behavioral disposition. Code and data are available at this https URL .


### [175] [CARD: A Proof-Carrying Certificate-to-Action Interface for Relative-Measurement Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03651)
*Yushan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Geometric certificates make mathematical claims about specified estimator objects, while robotic action requires a physical transform, a service decision, and timely proof support. We present CARD, a proof-carrying interface that keeps these objects distinct and records how they combine. Its lifted-fiber formulation separates semantic motion from witness repair and characterizes when projected and proof-carrying costs coincide. Its actionability result gives an exact useful-service law: uniform sampling without replacement produces a hypergeometric yield, while sampling from the eligible population gives one useful service per request under the observed live-confirmation condition. The primary study contains 404 Full16 cases from 20 sorties of an AA15-X500 airborne survey system. All 404 checker-confirmed optimizer points are reference-discrepant, with median rotation and translation discrepancies of 1.9891 degrees and 21.6322 m. The paired causal-navigation transforms are reference-consistent in all 404 cases, with medians of 0.0036566 degrees and 0.0104519 m. Across five matched request budgets, the largest difference between the hypergeometric prediction and the observed ordinary-random mean is 0.8412 services. Ordinary matched random reaches 0/50 target successes, while actionability-matched random reaches 50/50 and matches the CARD policy. Public downstream evaluation on 11 KITTI, EuRoC, and TUM SLAM cases measures optimizer wall-time ratios of 1.128x-67.65x for random versus CARD graph interventions. Native solver records locate this difference in elimination fill, Cholesky factor size, and numeric factorization. Together, these results connect certificate semantics, physical execution, proof acquisition, and graph computation through explicit system objects.


### [176] [MCPGuard: Finding Conformance Gaps in MCP SDKs via Dual-Track Analysis and Dynamic Adjudication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03662)
*Xikai Sun, Kebin Liu, Fan Dang, Yawen Zheng, Zihao Yang, Yunhao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) connects large language model agents to external tools and services through official SDKs that implement message validation and session logic. Specification constraints omitted by these SDKs may propagate to downstream applications, highlighting the importance of SDK-level conformance testing. Yet existing MCP conformance tools mainly exercise running endpoints and reveal little about whether SDK models enforce specification requirements. We present MCPGuard, an automated LLM-based framework for detecting specification-to-SDK conformance gaps. Its structural track aligns JSON Schema constraints with normalized SDK models and produces constraint-specific evidence. Its behavioral track extracts normative prose rules, retrieves bounded function-level slices, and audits them with LLMs for candidate omissions. An evidence-aware dynamic adjudicator then inspects complete source context and builds PoCs to validate candidates. A shared kernel with language-specific adapters enables analysis across SDKs. On the official Python 1.26.0 and TypeScript 1.29.0 SDKs, MCPGuard confirms 13 and 19 findings, respectively, none of which are exposed by two black-box baselines. Tests on open MCP servers further show that these gaps can systematically propagate downstream. By making SDK conformance of specification constraints directly testable, MCPGuard complements conventional endpoint-oriented conformance suites.


### [177] [Functional-Group Knowledge Is Structured in Language Models Before It Is Spoken](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03684)
*Tran Nhat Khang Nguyen, Anh Nguyen, Obed Issakah, Janghoon Ock*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Language models serve as the reasoning core of agents for chemistry and materials science, where they must interpret molecular structure before planning syntheses or proposing materials. Yet their outputs show only what they say, not what they know. Given a prompt such as ``The primary functional group in 1-Fluorobutane (CCCCF) is'', a small general-purpose model may answer ``the alkyne group'' instead of an alkyl fluoride. The output alone cannot tell whether the model failed to recognize the fluorine or recognized it and failed to say so. We address this by reading the internal representations of five open-source models on prompts covering 92 molecules and 20 functional groups. Even without chemistry training, the models organize functional groups by chemical family, and they encode chemical differences as consistent directions. The shift from thioether ($\mathrm{R{-}S{-}R'}$) to thiol ($\mathrm{R{-}SH}$), added to ether ($\mathrm{R{-}O{-}R'}$), lands nearest alcohol ($\mathrm{R{-}OH}$). This structure emerges abruptly in the middle layers, where representations shift from tracking the wording of the prompt to tracking the functional group. The base model names the correct functional group in only 58.5\% of prompts, the lowest of the five, yet it encodes functional-group families and preserves chemically meaningful patterns. Its errors are mostly failures to answer rather than wrong answers. Functional-group knowledge is structured in the model before it is spoken. Chemistry fine-tuning reorganizes it, making functional groups rather than prompt wording dominate the representation. These results suggest that evaluating chemical language models through outputs alone can underestimate the chemical information they encode. Representation analysis can distinguish a lack of knowledge from a failure to express it, which matters when choosing or adapting models for chemical agents.


### [178] [LOLPlay: Benchmarking Real-Time Visual Agents in League of Legends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03685)
*Zhaojian Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Visual agents need to coordinate reasoning and action while their environment evolves during inference. We introduce LOLPlay, a benchmark in native \emph{League of Legends} with 36 champion-specific tasks spanning resource management, combat, cooperation, and objectives. Agents interact through screenshots and mouse--keyboard actions; automated initialization and 82 executable subgoal checks distinguish partial progress from completion. Across 324 task evaluations of three models at three reasoning-effort settings, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate of 25.0\% at low and medium effort, and the highest subgoal success rate of 40.3\% at medium effort. However, high effort improves neither metric over low effort for any model. For Astra, longer requests leave fewer interaction rounds, suggesting a cost to additional reasoning in real-time control. Trajectories reveal incomplete subgoal composition and actions executing after the observed scene changes, motivating agents that combine visual reasoning with timely action.


### [179] [Training LLM Agents to Automate LLM Post-Training with RSI-Dojo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03698)
*Shuzheng Gao, Zhaojian Yu, Penghao Yin, Yuling Shi, Kai Cai, Shilin He, Michael R. Lyu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) aims to enable AI systems to autonomously build and improve AI systems themselves, including models, harnesses, and underlying infrastructure. Within this broader agenda, a central capability is enabling agents to train and improve base models. However, existing studies on automated model training primarily focused on workflow- or harness-level optimizations, rather than internalizing the ability to conduct post-training into the model itself. Developing this capability requires executable model training environments that can produce high-quality trajectories demonstrating how to select data, train models, diagnose failures, and iteratively improve results. In this paper, we introduce RSI-Dojo, a collection of executable model training environments designed to teach LLM agents to conduct post-training. To construct RSI-Dojo, we develop RSI-Craft, an automatic pipeline that converts human research artifacts into executable model training environments. RSI-Craft automatically collects research papers from top venues, instantiates datasets and metrics into a unified interface, and reproduces evaluation dependencies. It then filters tasks by data size, execution reliability, and discriminability, yielding RSI-Dojo with 281 seed model training tasks. Building on RSI-Dojo, we employ a trajectory collection method with an improved harness to collect high-quality model-training trajectories from frontier models, then retain examples through rubric-based and outcome-based filtering. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on these trajectories. The resulting RSI-Dojo-9B and RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B improve over their base models by 8.74 and 16.87 PostTrainBench points, respectively. RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B reaches 29.95 on PostTrainBench and 53.29 on PostTrainBench-Lite, matching the performance of frontier models such as GLM-5.2 and GPT 5.5.


### [180] [Unsatz: Evaluating Scientific Discovery Without a Given Ansatz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03713)
*Ethan Ji, Wei Wang, Yizhou Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Before a scientist fits any parameter, they must decide what the model contains: which quantities exist, which are hidden, how they interact, and how they are measured. Physicists call such a trial form an ansatz. Benchmarks for automated equation discovery mostly supply it, by fixing the variables, a library of candidate terms, or a law to be modified. We introduce Unsatz, a benchmark in which an LLM agent must construct the ansatz itself. Each task is a simulated system, generated from a private seed as a program in a small modeling language: ordinary, delay, or partial differential equations, discrete maps, static and causal models, or stochastic processes, possibly with hidden states, distorted sensors, and uninformative measurement channels. The agent buys experiments within a budget, writes candidate programs in the same language, obtains their coefficients from the benchmark's fitter, and is scored only on how its final program predicts experiments it never saw: conditions outside the range it could explore, interventions, and longer runs. Across 80 systems in 20 domains, nine language models solve between 21% and 60%. On 44 of these systems, classical equation-discovery methods almost never succeed, although the same data usually suffice once the true equations are given with unknown coefficients; the difficulty lies in finding the structure, not in estimating it. Because mechanisms recur across systems, we also give agents the program they discovered for a related system; it helped only when the new system shared the mechanism and the first investigation had succeeded. Stronger agents make fewer unscientific errors; beyond these, agents find which variables a system has but not how they act, and prefer revisions that lower the training error to revisions that predict better.


### [181] [Counterexample-driven autonomous research with application to excess radiated energy in a Z-pinch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03721)
*Bowen Zhu, Jian Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs in artificial-intelligence-assisted mathematics show that open-ended machine search can outperform human mathematicians, yet transferring this paradigm to physics requires refactoring the research workflow. We introduce Simjecture, an open-source framework in which language-model agents pursue human-defined physical conjectures. The framework tackles two central challenges in this transfer: first, because physics is empirical and no model is final, it structures inquiry around counterexample-driven falsification and prospective hypothesis repair; second, because physical theories must interact with the real world, it uses computational physics as an executable testbed while separating the qualification of numerical instruments from scientific claim review so solvers and diagnostics can be adapted within a recorded investigation. We apply the framework in an 82.6 h investigation to tackle the long-standing problem of radiated energy exceeding the imploding-shell kinetic energy in a Z-pinch. The investigation reveals competing effects of energy conversion, retention, and release timing. Stronger coherent helical deformation increases resistive conversion while reducing radiation and increasing retained energy. A broader trailing-plasma profile delays the later radiation maximum and recovers much of its early bolometric deficit, with a spectral response that changes during the pulse. These results provide new insight into energy conversion in high-energy-density plasma physics and demonstrate a new paradigm for physics research.


### [182] [EMBERS: Empirically Motivated Robustness Testing of Deep Research Systems via Defect-Guided Fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03723)
*Shuochuan Li, Zan Wang, Dong Wang, Yuhang Jiang, Huajin Cui, Chen Zhang, Gang Wang, Junjie Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Deep Research (DR) systems extend large language models into autonomous agents that decompose complex information needs, iteratively search the web, and synthesize evidence to answer challenging questions. However, their multi-stage execution may introduce robustness risks, as variations in user questions or intermediate search inputs may propagate across research stages and lead to incorrect answers. Existing evaluations focus on task-solving capabilities or robustness against external evidence disturbances, leaving intrinsic weaknesses in DR execution insufficiently explored. Thus, we conduct an empirical study of 1,932 execution trajectories from three representative DR systems and identify nine recurring defect categories across subquestion formulation, online search, and final reasoning. Guided by these findings, we propose EMBERS, an empirically motivated, defect-guided fuzzing framework for robustness testing of DR systems. Starting from correctly answered seed questions, EMBERS generates stage-targeted test inputs using defect-guided mutation, web-search-assisted fidelity assurance, and diversity-aware seed maintenance. Experiments across 16 framework--model--dataset settings show that EMBERS achieves 3.21 times the failure-discovery throughput of the strongest baseline under identical testing budgets. Human and LLM-assisted evaluations demonstrate that most generated inputs preserve the original information need and necessary contextual constraints. Moreover, model fine-tuning experiments show consistent-answer performance improvement, highlighting their value for robustness testing and model improvement.


### [183] [From Logs to Faults: Reference-Free Fault Localization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03725)
*Hira Naveed, Scott Barnett, Stefanus Kurniawan, John Grundy, Chetan Arora, Hourieh Khalajzadeh, Omar Haggag*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines are widely used for domain-specific question answering, yet locating faults remains manual and time-consuming. Fault localization is challenging because RAG pipelines consist of interdependent stages, where faults can propagate downstream, masking their origin, or remain undetected even when the final answer is correct. Existing evaluation approaches often focus on individual stages and rely on reference answers or code instrumentation. We present RAGLog, which detects and localizes faults across RAG stages using execution logs, without requiring reference answers or code instrumentation. RAGLog transforms heterogeneous API logs into a common RAG schema and combines rule-based, embedding-based, and LLM-based checks to identify faulty stages. We introduce a live fault injector and RAGLog Faults, a dataset of 3,134 runs from five RAG pipelines with stage-level fault labels. With Claude Haiku 5.5, RAGLog achieves a pooled F1 score of 0.73 on runs with naturally occurring faults, outperforming Claude Code (0.58) and Codex (0.52), and comparable effectiveness on runs with injected faults (0.84 versus 0.80 and 0.82, respectively). It analyzes runs in approximately five seconds at US\$0.005 each, with overall lower cost and latency than agent baselines. RAGLog is available as an open-source tool for debugging, evaluating, and monitoring RAG pipelines.


### [184] [Reasoning is topologically simple](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03739)
*Chris Kapulkin, Nathan Kershaw*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: We formulate and investigate the hypothesis that reasoning text has simpler co-occurrence topology than non-reasoning text, and that higher-quality reasoning has simpler co-occurrence topology than lower-quality reasoning. We discuss and test applications to AI safety and AI evaluation.


### [185] [VagueBench: Benchmarking the Robustness of Embodied Agents on Ambient Context Illusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03765)
*Yang Qu, Qiang Fu, Zonghao Ying, Jiayi Zhao, Yaoyuan Zhang, Jinyang Guo, Jian Yang, Aishan Liu, Yingxun Wang, Xianglong Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Embodied agents are increasingly deployed in open-world environments, yet current robustness evaluations remain biased toward hazardous instructions and jailbreak vulnerabilities. A more fundamental issue is largely overlooked: agents are rarely tested under semantically sufficient but contextually minimal instructions (i.e., common in real-world human communication), where auxiliary contextual cues are absent. For the first, we identify a novel phenomenon termed Ambient Context Illusion, where agents fail to execute tasks correctly when non-essential contextual details are omitted, despite unchanged task semantics. To systematically study this, we establish VagueBench, a benchmark built upon the AI2-THOR interactive simulation environment for the evaluation of context dependency in embodied agents. We systematically consider key semantic components of instructions (entities, procedures, constraints, and prior knowledge) and derive a unified taxonomy with four categories and nine subtypes to reduce auxiliary information. VagueBench contains 1,303 instruction pairs across diverse indoor environments, constructed via a human-agent co-generation framework that integrates semantic redundancy estimation, taxonomy-guided transformation, and rigorous semantic-invariance verification. Through extensive evaluation on 6 state-of-the-art embodied agents, we observe significant degradation on VagueBench in both task performance and executability (relatively -19.65% in task success rate and relatively +54.10% in empty plan rate) revealing a strong reliance on auxiliary linguistic signals rather than core task semantics. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this phenomenon persists in real-world robotic systems, where it can lead to incorrect decisions and potential safety risks.


### [186] [El Agente Fármaco: A Scalable AI Scientist for Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03832)
*Marcel Müller, Mohammad Ghazi Vakili, Yoshihiro Ogura, Ella Ashoori, Shimeng Wang, Alessandro S. Nascimento, Eddie Calleja, Yutong Zhao, Yimeng Liu, Ella Miray Rajaonson, Aiwei Yin, Jiaru Bai, Yunheng Zou, Abraham C. Stern, Varinia Bernales, Alán Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Computational drug discovery offers a growing range of tools for proposing and evaluating molecules, but combining them and deciding what to do next still requires substantial expert effort. Here, we introduce El Agente Fármaco, an AI scientist that uses modern large language model (LLM) technology to turn discovery goals expressed in everyday language into complex, scalable computational screening workflows. The agentic harness selects scientific tools, reviews their results, and adjusts subsequent steps to the target and the user's objectives. Our approach centers on a shared scientific memory: Fármaco Atlas, which stores molecules, structures, and results together with their provenance -- a record of how they were obtained. The agent uses targeted database queries to retrieve the evidence needed for each decision. A single request can launch calculations for an entire collection of molecules, without requiring a separate LLM decision for every compound. This design limits the information the model must process as screening collections grow, while keeping the underlying results available for inspection and further analysis. The integrated tools support molecule generation, prediction of binding and molecular properties, and searches for compounds available for purchase. In a retrospective comparison with an expert-led campaign, the agent proposed chemically distinct candidates with competitive docking scores and higher calculated drug-likeness under explicitly imposed selection criteria. In a 22-task benchmark against plain tool calls and Codex, the agent achieved the highest observed mean model-judged score, with lower median cumulative token use and peak context. Fármaco provides a foundation for flexible, scalable drug discovery workflows that connect scientific reasoning with an enduring, traceable record of computational work.


### [187] [Low-Rank Hierarchical Merging for Efficient Long-to-Short Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03834)
*Zeqiu Yu, Xuesheng Zhang, Wenxiao Zhao, Shixiao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Long-to-Short (L2S) model merging seeks to combine the strong reasoning capability of long chain-of-thought models with the concise style of base models, but existing methods that rely on linear interpolation and first-order activation statistics fail to capture how the loss landscape responds to parameter displacement, leading to degraded accuracy on hard tasks. We propose Low-Rank Hierarchical Merging (LHM), a training-free framework that replaces heuristic statistics with lightweight second-order curvature information. LHM consists of three components: (1) a low-rank Hessian approximation that efficiently estimates per-layer Hessian traces via Hutchinson's estimator on random inputs, eliminating the need for task-specific data; (2) a Layer Second-Order Sensitivity (LSOS) index that combines Hessian trace with task-vector norm to quantify per-layer fusion difficulty; and (3) a Dual-Constraint Coefficient Mapping (DCCM) that converts LSOS scores into layer-adaptive merge coefficients while jointly satisfying a fusion-error bound and a global length-reduction target. Experiments across six mathematical benchmarks on 1.5B and 7B models show that LHM outperforms state-of-the-art methods such as ACM-TIES and ORCA, achieving over 4\% accuracy gain and up to 93.1\% reduction in output length relative to the long-CoT model.


### [188] [LaplaceDiag: Fault Diagnosis through Changes in Learned Actuator Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03849)
*Oliver Niggemann*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Identifying faulty components from sensor anomalies is difficult because fault effects can propagate through a plant and different faults can produce similar observations. Detailed component models and labeled fault examples may also be unavailable. We introduce LaplaceDiag, a data-driven method that uses changes in actuator effects to narrow the search for faulty equipment. Actuators act on specific devices and mechanisms, so their changed effects can provide clues to root causes. LaplaceDiag learns a healthy plant model with LaplaceNet, a neural network that operates in Laplace space. It separates nonlinear prediction based on measured history from an explicit matrix that describes how actuator values affect sensor values. Keeping the history predictor fixed allows current actuator effects to be estimated by linear regression without retraining the neural network. Comparison with a healthy reference accounts for estimation uncertainty and normal variation to detect changes and generate actuator-related diagnostic hypotheses. The method supports online and offline diagnosis, providing an intermediate step between anomaly detection and identifying faulty components. We successfully evaluate LaplaceDiag on two datasets.


### [189] [Performance, Scalability and Maintenance: An Experimental Study of RPA vs. Autonomous Agents in the Healthcare Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03853)
*Daniel Schulz, Pedro Cabral Buchaim, Bernardo Soares, Fernando Rafael Ensslin Schwengber, Lorenzo Farias, Daniela Duarte da Silva Bagatini, Ana Paula Wernz da Cunha Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Healthcare information systems are fragmented, and retrieving data from legacy web portals is a recurrent need. Robotic Process Automation (RPA) is deterministic but sensitive to interface changes, whereas autonomous agents based on large language models (Agent Process Automation, APA) promise adaptability at a higher computational cost. Following Design Science Research, we developed and compared an RPA solution (Playwright) and an APA solution (browser-use, LangGraph and Gemini 2.5 Flash-lite) to retrieve mammography and breast ultrasound reports from a laboratory portal, treating reports solely as informational artifacts.
