We introduce JHiMA-WebRL, a joint hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for web agents that couples a task decomposer with an actor. To train the decomposer, we use a strong teacher model to construct structured subtask supervision from multimodal GUI trajectories, and use these annotations to fine-tune both the decomposer and the subtask-conditioned actor. We then extend Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to jointly optimize the decomposer and actor in the interactive WebArena environment through hierarchical rollout grouping and role-specific advantages. On WebArena-Lite-v2, JHiMA-WebRL-14B, consisting of a 7B actor and a 7B decomposer, achieves an average success rate of 37.2%, compared with 24% for a ScaleCUA-32B agent that we fine-tune with RL on the same training tasks and evaluate under the same protocol. Ablations show that jointly updating the decomposer during RL outperforms updating the actor alone. These results suggest that jointly learned role factorization with smaller models can be a competitive alternative to scaling monolithic agents for long-horizon web tasks.


### [287] [The Geometry of Ignorance: Detecting Suppressed Knowledge in LLMs through Activation Fragmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05272)
*Adar Peleg, Dvir Alsheich, Tomer Ashuach, Avi Mendelson*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Evaluating whether a large language model (LLM) genuinely knows a concept remains a fundamental problem, since behavioral metrics can be fooled by suppression: a model that refuses to answer may still harbor recoverable knowledge. The framework measures the geometric coherence of \textit{anon-vectors}, the differences in hidden states between a concept-specific query and its anonymized paraphrase, and shows that genuine knowledge forms a tight, low-entropy cluster while ignorance forces geometric fragmentation. Across ten instruction-tuned LLMs from three model families, all fragmentation metrics reliably separate known from unknown concepts, and post-unlearning, fragmentation geometry correlates with adversarial recovery across four attack types and five unlearning algorithms, though the direction of that relationship is shown to depend on attack methodology. Furthermore, a layer-resolved analysis reveals that different adversarial mechanisms exploit this geometry at varying depths, with high attack-specific correlations that are invisible to behavioral evaluation. Together, these results constitute a single-phase, attack-free proxy for unlearning robustness that captures aspects of parametric knowledge inaccessible to output-level metrics. A reference implementation is available at this https URL


### [288] [You don't need long context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05306)
*Peter Belcak, Saurav Muralidharan, Jindong Jiang, Yonggan Fu, Wonmin Byeon, Hanrong Ye, Ryan Angilly, Ali Taghibakhshi, Shih-Yang Liu, Siru Ouyang, Greg Heinrich, Pavlo Molchanov*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Later tokens are costlier to generate than earlier tokens. On frontier open weight models, the disparity in cost may rise to as much as 7x. Through frequent compaction, we show across a broad range of long-horizon and long-input tasks that frontier language models sacrifice little to no task accuracy when maintaining short contexts. We further demonstrate that LLM performance does not collapse on long-horizon software engineering tasks even when taking at most one action per context, and based on that we outline the concept of semantic state space models (SSSMs). Our findings question the need for long-context language models.


### [289] [Adversarial Speculative Reasoning Improves Long Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05345)
*Hongyin Luo, Wei Fang, Lijie Yang, Owen Stepan, Daniel Suit, Jeff Krapf, Dana Wensberg, Jack O'Brien*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Speculative decoding has been treated purely as a systems optimization that speeds up large language model decoding without changing the model behavior. Under this view, the growing effort to train better draft models has been focused solely on acceptance rate. We argue that this view does not hold true for long-horizon agent reasoning. Measuring frontier open-weight models on complex agentic tasks with different speculative decoding approaches, we found that numerical noise grows within verification windows, and the draft acceptance results of the thinking process can change the output distribution of the model after 30 steps, thus noticeably influencing the reasoning accuracy of long-horizon agents in random directions. In this work, we improve the reasoning capability of target models by controlling such noise. While we cannot change the overall token distribution verified by the target model, we can encode critical reasoning with highest precision by training the draft model to be rejected. We identify these points in agent traces and train the draft model to favor adversarial preference. At inference the trained draft and the target part ways precisely at these decision points, verification restarts, so that critical thinking tokens are sampled from the target model and encoded under its least noisy conditions. This approach makes critical reasoning better indexed and more durable over a long horizon. On complex agentic coding benchmarks, our approach raises end-to-end success rate with no change to the target model or its output distribution. Our results recast the speculative decoding mechanism from a pure accelerator into an active allocator for context precision that improves the accuracy of long-horizon agents by training 0.06% to 0.28% of hosted parameters.


### [290] [Preserving the Privacy of Reward Functions in MDPs through Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.09809)
*Shashank Reddy Chirra, Pradeep Varakantham, Praveen Paruchuri*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Preserving the privacy of preferences (or rewards) of a sequential decision-making agent when decisions are observable is crucial in many physical and cybersecurity domains. For instance, in wildlife monitoring, agents must allocate patrolling resources without revealing animal locations to poachers. This paper addresses privacy preservation in planning over a sequence of actions in MDPs, where the reward function represents the preference structure to be protected. Observers can use Inverse RL (IRL) to learn these preferences, making this a challenging task. Current research on differential privacy in reward functions fails to ensure guarantee on the minimum expected reward and offers theoretical guarantees that are inadequate against IRL-based observers. To bridge this gap, we propose a novel approach rooted in the theory of deception. Deception includes two models: dissimulation (hiding the truth) and simulation (showing the wrong). Our first contribution theoretically demonstrates significant privacy leaks in existing dissimulation-based methods. Our second contribution is a novel RL-based planning algorithm that uses simulation to effectively address these privacy concerns while ensuring a guarantee on the expected reward. Experiments on multiple benchmark problems show that our approach outperforms previous methods in preserving reward function privacy.


### [291] [On Discovering Algorithms for Adversarial Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00922)
*Shashank Reddy Chirra, Jayden Teoh, Praveen Paruchuri, Pradeep Varakantham*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Adversarial Imitation Learning (AIL) methods, while effective in settings with limited expert demonstrations, are often considered unstable. These approaches typically decompose into two components: Density Ratio (DR) estimation $\frac{\rho_E}{\rho_{\pi}}$, where a discriminator estimates the relative occupancy of state-action pairs under the policy versus the expert; and Reward Assignment (RA), where this ratio is transformed into a reward signal used to train the policy. While significant research has focused on improving density estimation, the role of reward assignment in influencing training dynamics and final policy performance has been largely overlooked. RA functions in AIL are typically derived from divergence minimization objectives, relying heavily on human design and ingenuity. In this work, we take a different approach: we investigate the discovery of data-driven RA functions, i.e, based directly on the performance of the resulting imitation policy. To this end, we leverage an LLM-guided evolutionary framework that efficiently explores the space of RA functions, yielding \emph{Discovered Adversarial Imitation Learning} (DAIL), the first meta-learnt AIL algorithm. Remarkably, DAIL generalises across unseen environments and policy optimization algorithms, outperforming the current state-of-the-art of \emph{human-designed} baselines. Finally, we analyse why DAIL leads to more stable training, offering novel insights into the role of RA functions in the stability of AIL. Code is publicly available: this https URL .


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [292] [LoRA-Patch: Defending Against Backdoored LoRA Adapters via Directional Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03752)
*Shengyan Huo, Peihai Jiang, Xixiang Lyu, Yige Li*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Third-party LoRA adapters introduce serious backdoor risks: malicious adapters can maintain normal task performance while embedding hidden, trigger-activated behaviors, making them difficult to detect through standard utility evaluations. Existing removal-based defenses typically sanitize backdoored models by updating, pruning, distilling, or mixing model parameters. However, these approaches are misaligned with adapter-based deployment, where the trusted base model is expected to remain frozen. In this work, we propose LoRA-Patch, a lightweight backdoor defense tailored to LoRA adapters. Our key insight is that backdoor injection distorts the directional geometry of a poisoned adapter relative to its trusted base model. Building on this insight, LoRA-Patch suppresses trigger-activated behaviors by realigning adapter directions through layer-wise steering, without modifying the base model weights. Extensive experiments across five backdoor attacks and three models show that LoRA-Patch effectively mitigates trigger-activated backdoors, outperforming existing baselines while preserving benign task performance. Moreover, LoRA-Patch is computationally efficient, constructing patches within 20 seconds across all evaluated models and adapters. Our code is publicly available at this https URL .


### [293] [The Web Doesn't Sit Still: Adversarial Self-Evolving Attacks on Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03754)
*Geyuan Wang, Shunyu Liu, Siyuan Liang, Chenyang Lyu, Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for empowering search agents to tackle complex information-seeking tasks. However, since acquiring external information necessitates web search, these agents are highly susceptible to various adversarial attacks (e.g., malicious information injection or stealthy context manipulation). Existing attack strategies inevitably rely on static and human-crafted templates, failing to emulate the dynamic and complex nature of real-world threats. In this paper, we propose an adversarial self-evolution framework that integrates bi-level optimization between the attacker's strategy evolution and the defender's adaptive mitigation, to expose the vulnerabilities of search agents. Specifically, we introduce the Contrastive Rollout Evolutionary Optimization (CREO) method to drive the directional evolution of the attack strategy population via contrastive evolutionary signals. To provide a stable environment for this continuous evolution, we further construct a controlled sandbox and design fine-grained metrics to quantify internal vulnerabilities. Extensive experiments across various multi-hop QA benchmarks and frontier LLMs demonstrate that the proposed framework yields attack efficacy superior to static baselines, underscoring the urgent need to develop more robust defense frameworks.


### [294] [JevBreak: Your Model, Their Decision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03759)
*Nitin Aravind Birur, Divyanshu Kumar, Sahil Agarwal, Prashanth Harshangi*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Restricting what a model can say is often mistaken for restricting an attacker's influence over its decision. An attacker can add text to the evidence and steer the model toward a wrong answer that still belongs to the allowed set. We evaluate seven decision models spanning hosted APIs, open-weight models and an encoder on 16 tasks in seven domains from Decision Bench, targeting every wrong option across 240 text examples (990 targets per model). We present Slot Attack, a black-box attack designed for decision models that appends text assembled from named components to steer predictions toward an attacker-selected incorrect option. The method relies solely on returned label probabilities, achieves success rates of 32--99\% on initially correct decisions, and identifies the effective component combinations with a median of 17--72 queries per successful attack. Accuracy and confidence do not reliably reveal this vulnerability. Models with near-identical accuracy on the sampled tasks differ by about 20\%age points in attack success. We demonstrate that an attacker can force not just one wrong answer but every available one: on about 20\% of correct decisions for the JEV models, 77\% for gpt-6-luna and 98\% for GLiNER2.5; a confidence threshold does not reliably screen the attack out. A fixed answer set constrains a decision's form, not its source; robustness must be measured per model, per task and per version rather than assumed from output format.


### [295] [RuntimeTickets: Source-Grounded Security Obligations for Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03772)
*Kuiyi Gao, Xue Liu, Bowei He*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Third-party Agent Skills extend what an autonomous agent can do, but a malicious Skill can hide data exposure or output manipulation inside a legitimate workflow. Neither reading a Skill's description nor granting it tool permissions tells whether its execution meets the user's security requirements. We introduce RuntimeTickets, a protocol that enforces and audits these requirements while a Skill runs. From released malicious-Skill datasets we derive a risk model and build adversarial-benign Skill pairs. From the task's delivery contract, a language model proposes security obligations for that task. Each obligation cites the Skill lines it answers, and the ticket tightens the host's approvals into fine-grained allowed actions but never loosens them. A runtime gate checks the recipient, object and amount of every outbound send before forwarding it, and trusted monitors record the effects within their observation scope. The ticket links these checks and observations to the approved obligations of each Skill run, and a deterministic decoder turns the ticket's status into governable data flows and verifies each obligation. Neither enforcement nor decoding involves a language model, so text injected by a Skill can change the proposal but never the rule that decides a send. We evaluate RuntimeTickets at the network egress boundary of three unmodified agent frameworks against production Skill scanners and runtime baselines, among them an AgentBound-derived allow-list and SkillGuard. RuntimeTickets eliminated both failure modes of current Skill defenses, overlook (malicious Skills let through) and overkill (benign Skills blocked): its overlook rate was 0.0% and its overkill rate 0.0%, while the scanners erred on both sides. RuntimeTickets ties approved requirements to verifiable execution evidence and offers a common foundation for the safe and auditable deployment of third-party Skills.


### [296] [What Malware Actually Does: An Empirical Analysis of Post-Infection Behavior Across Malware Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03774)
*Emmanuela Andam, Emanuel S. Grant, Naima Kaabouch*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Most malware detection research focuses on identifying whether a file is malicious before it executes. Far less attention goes to what happens after infection. This paper presents an empirical analysis of post-infection malware behavior across 37 families, grounded in dynamic sandbox execution using CAPEv2. Behavioral traces are modeled as heterogeneous graphs with five node types and seven typed edge relationships, preserving relational structure that flat feature vectors discard. A voting ensemble trained on graph-derived structural features achieves 94.44% accuracy and MCC of 0.863, outperforming a flat behavioral baseline by 7.4 percentage points in MCC. A multiclass classifier distinguishes 24 malware families, and a per-family behavioral fingerprint analysis characterizes post-infection activity across registry, network, process, and API dimensions. An LLM explanation layer generates analyst-readable reports with MITRE ATT&CK mappings grounded in observed behavioral evidence. The full pipeline is deployed as HeteroScan at this https URL .


### [297] [When Debate Amplifies Risk: Social-Bias Vulnerabilities in Multi-Agent Safety Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04289)
*Qinghua Mao, Xi Lin, Yuxin Qi, Zihao Xu, Jinze Gu, Yuliang Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented Web applications increasingly employ multi-agent debate (MAD) to aggregate conflicting, noisy, and heterogeneous information, yet deliberation is fundamentally a social process susceptible to interpersonal dynamics. When retrieved Web sources contain latent hazards alongside safe factual content, social biases such as conformity pressure and authority deference can override evidence-grounded safety boundaries. In this work, we investigate whether the social structure of deliberation can paradoxically amplify latent Web safety risks. We introduce SafeConflict-Bias, a controlled counterfactual diagnostic protocol spanning six core Web risk categories, which holds queries and retrieved evidence strictly invariant while isolating conversational majority sway, authority cues, and confidence asymmetry. Empirical evaluation across diverse proprietary and open-source backbones reveals that while isolated models reliably reject unsafe guidance, standard MAD catastrophically capitulates to social pressure---surging unsafe adoption from 0.0\% up to 100.0\% and systematically silencing safe minority evidence. To resolve this systemic fragility without retraining or debate regeneration, we propose RiskTrace-Debias, an inference-time auditing framework uniting evidence-first precommitment, social-metadata masking, and consistency-gated arbitration. RiskTrace-Debias suppresses worst-case unsafe adoption to $\le 2.0\%$ across all architectures without inducing over-refusal, while generating verifiable provenance traces to audit interpersonal manipulation in Web deliberation.


### [298] [CloakFEM: Agentic FEM Code Generation for Red-Teaming Computational Resource Exhaustion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04356)
*Li Zheng, Simin Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Finite element method (FEM) solvers are foundational to scientific and engineering simulation, yet their susceptibility to \emph{efficiency attacks} remains largely unexplored. An adversary can craft syntactically valid and runnable FEM programs that complete successfully while forcing excessive computational work, enabling computational denial-of-service (DoS) attacks. Finding such inputs is challenging because FEM programs occupy a vast, structured space constrained by numerical, geometric, and constitutive requirements. We introduce CloakFEM, a domain-guided LLM-agent framework for automatically generating runnable adversarial FEM programs that amplify solver computation. CloakFEM combines LLM-guided search with an FEM-specific intermediate representation and a deterministic validity gate that enforces runnable constraints. Across three FEM stacks, 17 problem classes, and 1,068 seeds, CloakFEM discovers adversarial inputs that substantially amplify solver work while remaining valid and solvable. In the most extreme case, solver iterations increase from 43 to 247,367, a \(5{,}752.72\times\) amplification. CloakFEM outperforms five generic black-box baselines and retains an advantage over evolutionary search under a controlled bounded action space. Cross-solver analysis further reveals both problem-intrinsic and solver-specific sources of vulnerability. Our results uncover an underexplored efficiency-attack surface in FEM solvers and show that runnable FEM programs can serve as adversarial inputs.


### [299] [Voluntary Servitude: Weaponized Safety Warning Mechanisms for Controlling AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04393)
*Ilan Zendel, Matan Levy, Stav Cohen, Avi Mendelson, Amit LeVi*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act on information retrieved from public sources they treat as credible, which turns a manipulated recommendation into a transaction, a dependency, or a decision with physical consequences. The security literature models this threat as instruction injection, in which an adversary writes a command into retrieved content and deployed defenses learn to recognize command syntax. We show that an attacker can instead steer an agent through the agent's own harm-avoidance. A single fabricated hazard, written as an ordinary review, leads the agent to disqualify the legitimate options and select the attacker's target by its own reasoning, with no command for a detector to recognize. We call this voluntary servitude. These payloads steer agents about as often as instruction injection (60.4% vs. 66.0%), and deployed injection detectors score them as benign, so behind a filter they are 1.7-4.4x more effective. We measure this at the generation stage across six models and ten consumer-service domains, for three attack families, and show that it transfers to production browser agents, outperforming instruction injection, and to a retrieval pipeline (1.0-57.0% end-to-end success, depending on the retriever). We then show that a corroboration requirement added to the agent's instructions removes roughly 95% of the exposure against a non-adaptive attacker without retraining. With the requirement in place, the agent still reads the planted hazard and no longer acts on it.


### [300] [Perseus: A Full-Stack System for Homomorphic Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04488)
*Alessandro Zirilli, Davide Marincione, Evgenios M. Kornaropoulos, Giuseppe Ateniese, Emanuele Rodolà*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made it exceptionally convenient for users to send their private data to model providers, which process them in plaintext. Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) promises to secure remote inference by computing directly on encrypted data, yet it has remained prohibitively slow. The effort to make it practical remains fragmented, with most existing frameworks being closed-source, which keeps progress from compounding. We present Perseus, a free and open Python software library that supports the full stack of encrypted model inference, from the cryptographic backend and data packing, to encrypted nonlinearities and end-to-end model execution. Perseus ships with built-in novel optimizations, one at each level of the stack: in-kernel regeneration of key-switching keys at the (i) backend level, a complex variant of Cachemir packing at the (ii) data level, and a state-of-the-art bootstrap placer that is both packing- and magnitude-aware at the (iii) model level. Perseus achieves state-of-the-art latency across multiple models and modalities, within $3\%$ of the plaintext models' accuracy and perplexity. Remarkably, it is $3.96\times$ faster at single-token decoding than any other implementation on Llama-3-8B. We initiate, for the first time, a public speedrun on encrypted GPT-2, so that progress can compound.


### [301] [ScrambleMark: Zero-Query Watermark-Agnostic Black-Box Evasion of Modern Speech Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04122)
*Binhao Ma, Weikang Ding, Hanqing Guo, Wenwei Zhao, Xiaowen Li, Leah Ding, Zhuo Lu, Rui Duan*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Speech watermarking is becoming a key defense for digital copyright and content authentication. Existing attacks treat evasion as a removal problem: they erase the watermark, relying on detector access or on speech data that is hard to collect, such as paired clean-watermarked or clean target speaker speech. Commercial platforms are moving to close off these assumptions. TrustedAudio, for instance, restricts detection to authenticated users and denies meaningful detection feedback to unauthorized parties. Adversaries thus cannot tell whether a sample is watermarked or which watermark it carries, nor reliably remove it without degrading speech quality. In this work, we show that evading a speech watermark does not require erasing it: detection relies on the structural consistency of the embedded pattern, and disrupting this consistency is enough to defeat it. Building on this observation, we identify harmonic structure as an effective attack surface, as perturbing it breaks watermark consistency while preserving perceptual quality. We therefore propose ScrambleMark, a zero-query, watermark-agnostic, black-box attack that evades detection through harmonic scrambling. ScrambleMark combines a deterministic engine for discovering transferable harmonic perturbations with a generative model that learns randomized scrambling patterns, avoiding reliance on fixed attack rules. Across three commercial platforms (Google SynthID, Resemble AI, and TrustedAudio), ScrambleMark is the only attack to both evade detection and keep speech natural, achieving nearly 97% evasion and a 4.2/5 human rating, far above the 1.1 to 1.7 of prior attacks. Beyond evasion, ScrambleMark withstands adaptive recovery designed to reverse it, evidence that structural evasion is hard to mitigate in practice.


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [302] [Can Network Models of Science Do Without Pluralist Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04590)
*Jonas Hallgren, David Hyland, Mahault Albarracin*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: Science is a process of useful models being proven wrong time and again, but despite its many flaws, it somehow works. Why is this? Paul Feyerabend argued that it works because people dare to hold and defend incompatible rival theories against the accepted one, since a theory's failures often lead to its abandonment in the presence of a more accurate or simpler alternative. He called this view theoretical pluralism. We introduce a theoretical and experimental model in which this can be examined. It compares communities of pluralist agents, which can hold rival theories side by side and maintain beliefs within and between those theories, with communities of monist agents, which can only incorporate what they hear or observe into a single model of their own. In a multi-agent variational inference setting, our experimental demonstrations illustrate how communities with rival theories can go through periods of uncertainty followed by a shift in the theories they hold true, how the information that is communicated between communities affects beliefs when those communities make different observations about the world, and how sharing support for theories and sharing beliefs within them contribute differently to collective belief dynamics.


### [303] [GeoFaham: Domain-Specialized Multi-Agent Geospatial Analytics for Disaster Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04977)
*Muhammad Sohail Danish, Gilles Q. Hacheme, Akram Zaytar, Satej S. Soman, Inbal Becker-Reshef, Juan Lavista Ferres, Salman Khan, Girmaw Abebe Tadesse*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: Real-world geospatial decision support requires more than single-step retrieval or code generation. Disaster-response questions often involve multi-source workflows that combine spatial databases, map-derived features, satellite imagery, raster products, and reusable geospatial artifacts, where correctness depends on preserving spatial scope, temporal context, and intermediate execution state. While large language models and coding agents offer a promising interface for such workflows, they struggle with geospatial-specific failure modes, including handling large artifacts, selecting the wrong evidence source, losing spatial or temporal metadata, and applying invalid geometric operations. We introduce GeoFaham, a domain-specialized multi-agent system for geospatial disaster analytics. GeoFaham decomposes workflows into specialist agents for vector operations, map feature retrieval, imagery discovery, and raster analysis, coordinated by an Orchestrator with a dual-ledger control loop for dependency-aware planning, progress tracking, validation, and replanning. To support artifact-centric collaboration, GeoFaham uses a Response Store that persists full geospatial outputs and typed metadata outside the LLM context window while agents exchange compact identifiers and summaries. We also introduce GeoFaham-Bench, a 40-task benchmark grounded in 17 real-world disaster records, designed to evaluate multi-step geospatial reasoning, cross-source integration, raster-vector computation, artifact generation, and recovery under non-rigid ground truth. We evaluate GeoFaham on GeoFaham-Bench against strong general-purpose coding-agent baselines, including Codex, Claude Code, and OpenCode. GeoFaham achieves the best overall performance, scoring 0.940 versus the next-best 0.879, while using 7.5 times fewer tokens. Our code and benchmark are publicly available at this https URL .


<div id='astro-ph.CO'></div>

# astro-ph.CO [[Back]](#toc)

### [304] [3D dark matter map of the JWST COSMOS-Web field using diffusion-based generative priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03786)
*Diana Scognamiglio, Brandon Zhao, Katherine L. Bouman, Olivier Doré, Caitlin M. Casey, Maximilien Franco, Ghassem Gozaliasl, David Harvey, Olivier Ilbert, Jeffrey B. Jewell, Jeyhan S. Kartaltepe, Anton M. Koekemoer, Gavin Leroy, Richard Massey, Jason Rhodes, Brant E. Robertson, Marko Shuntov, Sune Toft, Greta Toni, Michael A. Troxel*

Main category: astro-ph.CO

Abstract: Mapping the three-dimensional distribution of dark matter is key to tracing the growth of cosmic structure. Two-dimensional weak lensing mass maps recover its projected distribution, but they blend structures at different distances along the line of sight. We reconstruct the three-dimensional matter distribution in the 0.54 deg$^2$ COSMOS-Web field using deep James Webb Space Telescope imaging, with an effective source density of 121 galaxies arcmin$^{-2}$ We combine a physical weak lensing forward model with a diffusion-based Bayesian generative prior learned from cosmological simulations, yielding a posterior distribution of the matter field across 20 lens planes to $z \simeq 2$, doubling the redshift reach of previous Hubble Space Telescope-based 3D reconstructions and approaching cosmic noon, an epoch of rapid galaxy growth. The reconstruction separates structures that overlap in projection and recovers non-Gaussian matter structure across a range of spatial scales, including over-densities associated with independently identified galaxy clusters that were not detected in earlier weak lensing maps. Posterior sampling quantifies the range of three-dimensional matter distributions allowed by the data, enabling a probabilistic visualization of the cosmic web while propagating uncertainties in its inferred structure. Null and simulation-based tests show that the recovered structures are supported by the lensing signal and are consistent with expectations from the fiducial $\Lambda$CDM cosmology. These results extend three-dimensional weak lensing mapping into the high-redshift, non-Gaussian regime and establish a route towards probabilistic mapping of the evolving cosmic web with current wide-field lensing surveys.


<div id='math.ST'></div>

# math.ST [[Back]](#toc)

### [305] [Estimating "Realized" Skewness using Convolutional Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04598)
*Haozhe Jiang, Ostap Okhrin, Michael Rockinger*

Main category: math.ST

Abstract: We propose a new estimator of low-frequency skewness (monthly) that exploits high-frequency data (5 min) through a direct functional mapping consisting of layers of Convolutional Neural Networks followed by Multi Layer Perceptrons. Monte Carlo experiments involving stochastic volatility models, with and without jumps, show that the estimator reduces finite-sample bias relative to existing realized-skewness estimators and remains accurate under model misspecification. Empirically, our estimator exhibits temporal stability and delivers cross-sectional performance in actual skewness-sorted portfolios. We characterize the finite-sample bias of conventional plug-in skewness estimators and establish risk consistency properties of the proposed NN estimator under standard regularity conditions.


### [306] [Conformal Prediction at Performative Equilibrium: The Price of Performativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05277)
*Ahmed Mahrous, Roberto Di Pietro*

Main category: math.ST

Abstract: Conformal prediction gives finite-sample coverage when calibration and test points are exchangeable, but exchangeability can fail when deployment changes the data distribution. We study conformal prediction in performative settings, where the deployed predictor affects the data distribution, and seek prediction sets valid at performative equilibrium: coverage must hold under $D(f^\star)$ for a performatively stable predictor $f^\star$. Under total-variation $L$-sensitivity of the distribution map, we prove a phase transition at $L=1$. If $L \ge 1$, no finite-sample procedure can uniformly guarantee both equilibrium-valid coverage and nontrivial prediction sets. If $L<1$, the minimax excess miscoverage over informative procedures is $\widetilde{\Omega}(1/((1-L)\sqrt{n}))$, implying a sample-complexity inflation of order $1/(1-L)^2$ for fixed excess miscoverage. Our main technical tool is a transfer theorem: any procedure returning a predictor within $\varepsilon$ of equilibrium yields equilibrium-valid coverage up to an additive $L\varepsilon$ term after split-conformal calibration. We instantiate the theorem with two retraining algorithms: a model-free method for discretizable classes and a parametric tail-averaged method that matches the $n$- and $L$-dependence of this rate up to logarithmic factors. Experiments, including semi-synthetic credit scoring, demonstrate the predicted threshold behavior and scaling laws.


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [307] [Nonparametric GARCH: An Iterated Neural Estimator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04157)
*Ruizhi Deng, Guohao Shen, Ngai Hang Chan*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study nonparametric estimation and forecasting for GARCH-type processes and propose an iterated neural estimator that alternates between estimating the recursion map and updating the unobserved conditional variances. Under mild conditions, we establish finite-sample bounds for the time-averaged in-sample variance-path error and show that the estimator is minimax rate-optimal up to logarithmic factors. Under stationarity and mixing conditions, we establish the same rate for recursion-map estimation and derive finite-sample bounds for out-of-sample variance forecasts and expected excess Gaussian quasi-likelihood (QLIKE) loss. The convergence rates depend on the true lag orders and remain unchanged when the fitted orders are overspecified. Simulation studies illustrate the effectiveness of our estimators under nonlinear and time-varying volatility dynamics. Real-data applications suggest that our neural methods perform competitively and can incorporate option-price and high-frequency information.


### [308] [Point interpolation and distributional universality in flow-based generative models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04207)
*Antonio Álvarez-López, Jingpu Cheng, Liyan Zhang, Qianxiao Li*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Motivated by the expressive power of neural ordinary differential equations in flow-based generative modeling, we study the approximation of probability measures by pushforwards generated by parameterized families of Lipschitz vector fields. For Wasserstein distances, we establish a quantitative relationship between finite-sample point interpolation and distributional approximation, and derive universality criteria based on $L^p$-approximation of transport maps. In particular, affine-invariant families containing a non-affine vector field are universal from compactly supported absolutely continuous base measures. For total variation, we show that coordinate-wise dynamics are insufficient in dimensions $d\geq 2$ and establish sufficient conditions based on one-dimensional Sobolev approximation and shear-type mixing. We further examine the role of point interpolation, establishing when different regimes relate to one another and when approximate interpolation can be made exact.


### [309] [Two-Layer Random ReLU Networks Can Separate Disjoint Classes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04211)
*Yichen Liu, Jianbin Yang*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Set separation by continuous linear functionals is fundamental in functional analysis and convex optimization and underlies binary classification in machine learning. Support vector machines provide maximum-margin affine separators for linearly separable data, while kernel methods represent nonlinear decision rules through linear separation in a reproducing-kernel Hilbert space. We study when two classes, not necessarily convex or linearly separable in the input space, can be separated with positive margin by finite ReLU networks and by random ReLU features. At the deterministic level, we prove that positive-margin separation by a finite ReLU network is equivalent to the existence of finite closed convex covers whose opposite-label members have positive pairwise distances, and exactly two hidden layers suffice. For random separation, suppose each class is represented as a finite union of nonempty closed convex cells, possibly unbounded. If every opposite-label cell pair has positive distance and its closed difference cone is pointed, then two independent random ReLU layers yield a common positive-margin affine separator with high probability. The first layer separates the feature image of each negative cell from the feature image of the positive class by a hyperplane through the origin. It also controls all cross-class distances uniformly. The second layer combines cellwise scores through Chebyshev polynomials and realizes the resulting polynomial using Gaussian Hermite features, followed by a uniform finite-width approximation. With the remaining geometric, bias, and confidence parameters fixed, each sufficient width is quadratic in the largest Gaussian width of a spherical difference-cone section, hence at most linear in its span dimension. An example shows that this squared Gaussian-width dependence is sharp for both layers.


### [310] [UniFlow: Unified Multi-Scale Flow Matching for Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04825)
*Shibo Feng, Wei Li, Min Wu, Chunyan Miao, Peilin Zhao*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics across multiple temporal resolutions, yet effectively incorporating multi-scale information into generative modeling remains challenging without increasing generative complexity. We propose UniFlow, a unified framework that integrates multi-scale representation learning with efficient non-autoregressive generation through a shared-semantic discrete interface. Specifically, a multi-scale tokenizer employs shared-index voting to consolidate complementary temporal information into a single public-ID sequence, while scale-specific codebooks preserve the information required for reconstruction. Building on this unified representation, we develop geometry-aware categorical flow matching, which initializes transport from learned low-rank anchors and generates public identities non-autoregressively. This design captures multi-scale temporal structure without requiring multiple generative token streams or sequential token decoding. Experiments across four benchmarks and both short- and long-term settings demonstrate improved generative fidelity, strong cross-scale consistency, and substantial inference speedups over representative autoregressive and diffusion-based baselines. Code is available at this https URL


### [311] [How Risky Is Your Data-Driven Policy?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04292)
*Omar Besbes, Alon Rieger*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Data-driven algorithms are ubiquitous across industries, and there is an extensive literature on analyzing their \emph{average} performance. The risk profile of such algorithms, while critical, is much less understood. This paper quantifies the risk of data-driven policies and isolates its sources. We evaluate a policy through the conditional value-at-risk (CVaR) of its loss over the training sample, any policy randomization, and the future outcome, at a fixed unknown distribution. Regret against an oracle that knows the distribution decomposes into expected post-sample regret (PS), the average outcome-only CVaR gap of the selected action, and a sample-risk premium (SR), the additional tail risk omitted by averaging those evaluations. The analogous premium vanishes under expected loss. We characterize when the premium vanishes and how large it is relative to post-sample regret. For losses with a continuous density near the tail cutoff, we identify conditions under which both components decay at the same rate, yet the limiting ratio of the premium to post-sample regret can reach the thousands. When a probability mass at the cutoff moves with the action, the premium can decay more slowly and dominate post-sample regret. We establish conditions under which sample average approximation learns the oracle action at rate $n^{-1/2}$ in both settings, but its combined-risk regret is of order $n^{-1}$ in the first and $n^{-1/2}$ in the second. In finite-sample numerical examples of SAA in both settings, the premium accounts for approximately $74\%$--$97\%$ of regret.


### [312] [Nearly Linear-Time Follow-the-Perturbed-Leader for Weakly Observable Graph Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04307)
*Jongyeong Lee, Hyundam Yoo*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study online learning with directed weakly observable feedback graphs and propose a Follow-the-Perturbed-Leader (FTPL) policy that achieves best-of-both-worlds guarantee with nearly linear expected time complexity. To preserve the simple structure of FTPL, we develop a novel graph-based loss estimator that combines forced exploration, conditional geometric resampling and adaptive learning rates with surrogate probabilities. Our policy achieves $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\delta_*^{1/3} T^{2/3})$ regret for adversarial regimes as well as $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\delta_* \log T / \Delta_{\min}^2)$ regret for stochastic regimes, where $\delta_*$ and $\Delta$, respectively, denote the fractional weak domination number and the suboptimality gap. These results show that refined guarantees for problems with minimax regret of $\Theta(T^{2/3})$ can be obtained through appropriately designed adaptive learning rates and loss estimators without sacrificing the simplicity of FTPL.


### [313] [Conditional independence and graphical models for rankings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04586)
*Aritz Pérez*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Ranking models define probability distributions over rankings and are fundamental in applications such as preference learning and decision-making. Without structural assumptions, these models are intractable due to their factorial complexity. Although independence assumptions are key to reducing complexity and enabling interpretability, standard notions of conditional independence must account for the mutual exclusivity constraints inherent to rankings. We introduce relative rank models, a probabilistic framework based on structured coarsenings of the ranking space that relax these constraints while preserving relative order information. This representation enables a well-defined notion of conditional independence for rankings and allows rankings to be modeled using Bayesian networks. As a result, standard methods for learning and probabilistic inference can be applied to rankings, with interpretable representations based on conditional independence. We further show that two prominent ranking models based on independence assumptions tailored to rankings arise as special cases, providing a unifying framework for independence in ranking models.


### [314] [One-shot Distributed Learning via Weighted Averaging with Server-side Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04700)
*Pavle Tepavčević, Dušan Jakovetić, Miloš Savić*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Distributed learning has drawn considerable attention due to its capability of learning from distributed data without aggregating raw observations. However, frequent exchange of model updates can become the main bottleneck of communication. Therefore, one-shot approaches where each worker fits a local model and sends it to the server to aggregate emerge. It is known that averaging can achieve the so-called pooled-data mean squared error (MSE) order of a hypothetical node that has access to data from the server and all the workers, and performs empirical risk minimization (ERM) on it. However, when the number of workers increases too fast compared to the local sample size, nonzero local estimation bias prevents averaging from achieving this order. In this paper, we study whether independent server data can overcome this limitation. We consider $m$ workers with $n$ observations each and a server with $p$ observations, all i.i.d., giving $N=mn+p$ observations in total. Under certain regularity conditions, a pooled-data ERM achieves MSE of order $\mathcal O(N^{-1})$. We analyze deterministic nonnegative averaging of convex-loss M-estimators, with equal worker weights and the server's own estimator included. Under our assumptions, including nonzero local bias of order $n^{-\alpha}$, $\alpha\geq1$, sample-size weighted averaging achieves the pooled-data MSE order whenever $m=\mathcal O(n^{2\alpha-1})$. Above this worker scale, it does so if and only if $p=\Omega(nm\cdot m/n^{2\alpha-1})$. Hence, server-only estimation, whose MSE is of order $\mathcal{O}(p^{-1})$, can achieve pooled-data order while sample-size weighting fails. Analytical examples and synthetic experiments illustrate when averaging matches the pooled-data MSE order, complemented by CIFAR-10 experiments comparing aggregation strategies with linear and nonlinear classifiers.


### [315] [A Random Matrix Analysis of Symmetric Tensor Unfolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05137)
*Tom Szwagier, Xiaoyi Mai, José Henrique de Morais Goulart*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study a fundamental problem in machine learning: partial recovery of a planted signal $\boldsymbol{v} \in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ from a spiked tensor $\boldsymbol{\mathsf{X}} = \lambda \, \boldsymbol{v}^{\otimes p} + \boldsymbol{\mathsf{Z}}$, where $\boldsymbol{\mathsf{Z}}$ has symmetric Gaussian entries of variance $\asymp n^{-1}$. While previous works replace $\boldsymbol{\mathsf{Z}}$ by an i.i.d. standard Gaussian tensor to circumvent technical difficulties caused by dependencies, this paper directly analyzes the symmetric model via random matrix theory. We show that the square unfolding algorithm is subject to a sharp phase transition for partial recovery at $\lambda \asymp n^{(p-2) / 4}$, validating an open conjecture from Montanari and Richard (2014). We get precise expressions for the limiting spectral distribution, deterministic equivalent, phase transition threshold and overlap. The main message is that unfolded symmetric tensors spectrally behave like standard Wigner matrices, up to simple transformations accounting for high-order symmetries. We consider extensions to multiple orthogonal spikes, recursive unfolding and non-square unfolding via disproportionate random matrix theory, leading to an interesting conjectured statistical-computational tradeoff regarding the choice of unfolding: square versus long.


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [316] [An Investigation on Numerical Bugs in GPU Programs Towards Automated Bug Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05121)
*Ravishka Rathnasuriya, Nidhi Majoju, Zihe Song, Wei Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: General-purpose graphics processing unit (GPU) computing has emerged as a leading parallel computing paradigm, offering significant performance gains in various domains such as scientific computing and deep learning. However, GPU programs are susceptible to numerical bugs, which can lead to incorrect results or crashes. These bugs are difficult to detect, debug, and fix due to their dependence on specific input values or types and the absence of reliable error-checking mechanisms and oracles. Additionally, the unique programming conventions of GPUs complicate identifying the root causes of bugs, while fixing them requires domain-specific knowledge of GPU computing and numerical libraries. Therefore, understanding the characteristics of GPU numerical bugs (GPU-NBs) is crucial for developing effective solutions. In this paper, we conduct a comprehensive study of GPU-NBs by analyzing 397 real-world bug samples from GitHub. We identify common root causes, symptoms, input patterns, test oracles that trigger these bugs and the strategies used to fix them. We also present GPU-NBDetect, a preliminary tool designed to detect numerical bugs across six distinct bug categories. GPU-NBDetect detected a total of 226 bugs across 186 mathematical functions in four libraries, with 60 of the bugs confirmed by developers. Our findings lay the groundwork for developing detection and prevention techniques for GPU-NBs and offer insights for building more effective debugging and auto-repair tool.


### [317] [A Compact Formula-Aware Spreadsheet Abstraction for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03705)
*Tie Cheng*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to assist spreadsheet users with tasks such as spreadsheet model construction, formula generation, and data analysis, yet their interaction with real-world spreadsheets remains limited by the mismatch between the structure of spreadsheets and the sequential textual representations typically provided to LLMs. We propose a compact formula-aware spreadsheet abstraction that represents large homogeneous ranges through their positions, types, R1C1 formula patterns, and selected boundary values. An efficient algorithm detects and structures these ranges before a textual serialization is supplied to an LLM. The abstraction is provided to the LLM before detailed spreadsheet content is submitted, improving the accuracy and efficiency of spreadsheet processing while reducing token consumption. We evaluate the method on 50 spreadsheet-query cases. The evaluation shows that the abstraction alone was sufficient to answer some queries. When additional spreadsheet content is required, the abstraction reduces the amount of data that must be submitted to the LLM, thereby reducing token consumption. The abstraction layer also improves the reliability and accuracy of spreadsheet processing. We conclude by discussing further techniques for combining spreadsheet static analysis with artificial intelligence to improve the reliability, accuracy, and efficiency of AI assisted spreadsheet tasks.


### [318] [Testing Processor Contracts in Speculative Decoding Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04042)
*Tianyu Ding, Yaocheng Tong, Muzhi Yang, Lei Yang, Fei Jiang*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Speculative sampling can accelerate decoding without changing the target distribution. Its standard guarantee assumes that logits processors, which enforce constraints or modify token scores, receive the appropriate token prefix and state at each verification position. We formalize this processor-prefix contract and present BADC (branch-aware differential conformance testing). BADC combines enumerated references on tiny random models, checks of processor inputs and state, and simultaneous confidence bounds on output-event probabilities. Preregistered, source-informed experiments on selected Transformers and llama-cpp-python releases confirm two causes: processors read unverified drafts or advance state without rollback. For one pilot-selected prompt on a trained checkpoint, fresh samples give a 60.26-percentage-point lower bound on the difference in the probability of a predefined first-token event between ordinary and prompt-lookup decoding, with nominal simultaneous 95% coverage under within-arm i.i.d. sampling. Controlled comparisons establish no cause-recovery advantage of directed selection over random selection, while permutation testing detects each tested family at least as often as BADC. These findings connect processor-interface failures to observable decoding differences without establishing diagnostic superiority or workload prevalence.


<div id='physics.soc-ph'></div>

# physics.soc-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [319] [The Digital-Energy Recomposition of the Kitchin Cycle: Exploratory Evidence from U.S. IT and Irish Data-Centre Series (1995-2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33122)
*Bartosz Ziollko, Adam Niedzialkowski, Bartosz Szyma*

Main category: physics.soc-ph

Abstract: Using quarterly FRED and CSO Ireland data over 1995Q1-2025Q4, we examine whether U.S.\ IT investment and Irish data-centre electricity are recomposing the Kitchin cycle around energy- and compute-backed assets. Three findings stand out. First, a Quandt-Andrews sup-Wald test locates a significant break in the U.S.\ IT-GDP correlation at 2014Q3 (sup-W $= 9.69$; $r$ falls from $+0.45$ to $-0.12$); the Fisher $r$-to-$z$ test survives Bonferroni adjustment ($z = +3.11$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} = 0.006$), whereas the analogous test on the imposed Cloud-vs-AI boundary does not ($p_{\text{Bonf}} = 0.164$), so ``Strategic Decoupling'' pre-dates the AI Era by approximately six years. Second, in Ireland the synchronous AI-Era correlation between metered data-centre electricity and Real GDP is large ($r = +0.41$, $N = 16$) but \emph{collapses to $r \approx 0$ when Real GDP is replaced by Real GNP}, suggesting the headline is largely an artefact of the shared multinational tax-structure footprint between hyperscaler operations and Irish gross output. Third, the Irish cross-correlation profile is asymmetric in the opposite direction to a leading-indicator reading: the largest positive coefficients cluster on the $k < 0$ side (GDP precedes data-centre load), so compute capacity is better read as coincident or moderately lagging. Given the small AI-Era samples and the absence of a quarterly multinational-adjusted aggregate such as GNI*, all coefficients are exploratory; subject to these caveats, the U.S.\ Kitchin cycle is best dated to 2014Q3, and the Irish Energy-Data Nexus is dominated by multinational firm activity.


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [320] [Must AI Spell It Out? Pragmatic Comprehension of Alerts in a Time-Critical Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03976)
*Anwesha Das, Vera Demberg, Anna Maria Feit*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Assistive systems typically tell users everything, often spending time and attention on what a user already knows, leaving less for what happens next. People, on the other hand, leave out what they expect the listener can supply. Can people use their prior knowledge to understand an incomplete message when the speaker is a machine though? We studied this pragmatic comprehension in a monitoring task controlling users' prior knowledge and the available time to detect critical situations. In a preregistered study (N=114), we compared a pragmatic planner that reasons over a model of the listener's knowledge to decide what to say, when, and how explicitly, with a baseline specifying problems fully in onset order. Participants resolved underspecified alerts almost as reliably as fully specified ones and sooner, detected more problems, and rated the pragmatic assistant helpful more often, with no increase in reported mental demand. Our findings show that people can recover what the system left implicit and that pragmatic communication can support effective assistance under the constraints of a changing world.


### [321] [Agentic Path Maps: Exposing an Agent's Traversal Through the Garden of Forking Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04183)
*Madeleine Grunde-McLaughlin, Hafsah Usman, Daniel Mitroshkov, Emily Reif, Nino Migineishvili, Lakshya Chauhan. Kevin Yu, Jeffrey Heer, Daniel S. Weld*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: When an AI agent executes an underspecified task, it embeds choices that may contradict unarticulated user context and preferences. However, interrupting the user for clarification on all such decisions is untenable. How might agents communicate impactful, but unexplored, paths to the user after execution? To investigate, we first distill a design space for communicating alternative paths, supporting the generation of new systems and structuring dimensions of future study. We then measure the impact of one vital design space dimension on user comprehension: the number of alternatives displayed. Through a user study (n=48) in the data analysis domain, we find that participants rarely consider alternative paths of their own volition, but AI overreliance reduces when alternative paths are visualized for them. By revealing paths the agent did not take, we demonstrate how users can better interpret results and incorporate personal context.


### [322] [SEAM: Situated Executable Associative Memory for a Co-located Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04296)
*Keru Wang, Michael Xu, Ken Perlin, Nebojsa Jojic, Andrzej Banburski-Fahey*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: In groups, knowledge is distributed among individuals, and how it is brought together depends on social ties. Taking inspiration from how a single mind recalls and reacts to memories and current stimulus, we build a shared memory that does this for a community, prototyped as a social app, SEAM. Members post arbitrary moments; SEAM works out how fragments relate, distills knowledge about the group, and replies in a situated and associative way. Some of these memories act as instructions that can alter how the system behaves, making it programmable by its members. We deployed SEAM to 46 people in a co-located community for two weeks of open-ended use, and ran a follow-up workshop with 14. Members shared their days, interrogated the memory, and instructed it to track routines and run games; replies introduced them to people and subjects they had never encountered. SEAM points towards communities where knowledge finds people.


### [323] [From Watching to Steering: Enabling Human-Coding Agent Collaboration through Execution Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04466)
*Xiaoke Yan, Jiaying Lei, Danqing Shi, Zichen Wang, Nan Cao*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled coding agents to autonomously perform complex software development tasks, yet their long-running execution remains difficult for users to inspect, understand, and steer. We present Vibetrace, an interactive trace visualization and intervention system for agentic programming built upon OpenCode. Vibetrace introduces a human-agent collaboration paradigm of Observe, Understand, Intervene, and Evolve. It (1) visualizes agent workflows as interactive execution traces for inspecting actions, intermediate states, and failures; (2) enables users to fork execution from prior steps and provide targeted corrections without restarting validated progress; and (3) distills traces and human feedback into reusable skills for continual agent evolution. On SWE-bench Verified, distilled skills reduce input tokens by 23.0% and cost by 15.5%, while improving pass rate from 65.0% to 68.0%. User studies further demonstrate improved accuracy and efficiency over conventional log-based interaction.


### [324] [False Reassurance in LLM Severe-Weather Guidance: A Replayable Evaluation Against Real NWS Alerts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05290)
*Kadharmoideen Fadurudeen*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: An LLM assistant that cannot see official severe-weather alerts may tell users that conditions are calm while a warning is active. We call this false reassurance and measure it by replaying 117 frozen cases built from real U.S. National Weather Service (NWS) alerts (69 alert cases, 32 no-alert controls, 16 non-U.S. locations) through five conditions of a Claude-based preparedness pipeline that differ in alert access and prompt design. One rater hand-labeled all 342 alert-case responses under a seven-category scheme written after an initial binary pass, which we disclose. False reassurance was 0.0% for the production pipeline (0/66, 95% CI 0.0-5.5) and for the same alerts under a neutral prompt (0/69), 37.7% for a blind neutral prompt (26/69), and 44.9% when the blind prompt also told the model to disclose its blind spot (31/69). Every case was implicit; no response explicitly denied an alert. Real alerts removed false reassurance (paired McNemar p < 0.0001); the disclosure instruction did not (p = 0.40). An automated regex measure suggested the opposite and missed 53 of 57 true cases, so we do not use it. Alert data alone was not enough for safe behavior: with a neutral prompt the model raised some alarm on 87.5% of no-alert controls, against 0% under the production prompt, and a preregistered single-sentence ablation showed that one production instruction explains part of that gap. We also report two failures common to similar systems, NWS test products ingested as real alerts and a token budget that fails on complex cases, and we release the dataset, labels and code.


<div id='cs.LO'></div>

# cs.LO [[Back]](#toc)

### [325] [Aero-VPA: Few-Step Flow-Matching Control over Non-Reconstructive JEPA Latents for Agile Flight on Edge Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04392)
*Yeonseok Lee*

Main category: cs.LO

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models generalize across tasks, but their autoregressive decoders need sequential forward passes in proportion to the action horizon, each bounded below on embedded aerial platforms by streaming billions of parameters from memory. We present Aero-VPA, a lightweight Vision-Predictor-Action architecture for 3D navigation on edge hardware. A compact RGB-D Vision Transformer and a five-head ensemble predictor, trained without pixel reconstruction, forecast the next latent state; a Conditional Flow Matching solver generates a velocity and yaw-rate chunk in two Euler steps, so each decision takes five sequential network evaluations; and the ensemble dispersion drives an uncertainty fallback. Version 1 specified the architecture, deployment and protocol without results. This version reports the first closed-loop evaluation: PX4 software-in-the-loop simulation of quadrotor missions in procedurally generated forests. Behaviour cloning failed on all 60 missions despite open-loop errors of 0.02-0.04 m/s: the policy copies its last command, a copycat form of causal confusion. DAgger with a privileged, safety-gated supervisor gave the first successes but plateaued at 11.1-21.1% across six checkpoints. A map-free runtime shield with a 15 s depth memory, barrier constraints, a cap on motion outside the 73-degree field of view and backoff along the flown path raised the first DAgger policy's success on 180 paired missions from 21.1% to 52.2% (95% CI [45.0, 59.4]; exact McNemar p = 1.2 x 10^-10) and cut its crashes from 72.2% to 22.2%, while timeouts rose from 2.8% to 23.3%. All six checkpoints gained 30.0-34.4 percentage points, at mean speeds of 1.16-1.36 m/s. The code ( this https URL ), the trained policy (72.6 MB, this https URL ) and the full benchmark dataset are public.


<div id='math.NA'></div>

# math.NA [[Back]](#toc)

### [326] [Physics-informed traditional and generative Mori-Zwanzig-reduced models for multiscale systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05211)
*Emily Williams, Saad Qadeer, Amanda Howard, Panos Stinis*

Main category: math.NA

Abstract: Model reduction via the Mori-Zwanzig (MZ) formalism is well understood for coarse-grained representations constructed from standard basis functions such as Fourier functions. We show that the same formalism can be used to build reduced-order models in bases discovered by physics-informed machine learning. The fluctuation-dissipation theorem (FDT) is used to close the resulting non-Markovian evolution equations for the expansion coefficients of the learned basis. We further introduce a complementary, generative closure model. Relative to the Markovian dynamics alone, both the MZ and generative closures substantially improve predictions of the filtered states.


<div id='econ.EM'></div>

# econ.EM [[Back]](#toc)

### [327] [Regret-based Characterization, Information Limits, and the Sample Complexity of Auditing Adaptive Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05227)
*Irene Aldridge*

Main category: econ.EM

Abstract: A policy can earn its performance by where it locates on average or by reacting to information. For any Markov policy $\pi$ in a finite-horizon MDP, let $\nu$ be its open-loop marginal, which plays $\pi$'s average action distribution at each step. We show that $J(\pi)-J(\nu)$ equals $\sum_t tr Cov(Q^\nu_t,\pi_t)$ under endogenous dynamics. Audits based on immediate costs alone can have the wrong sign. Writing the covariance as a joint-versus-product gap gives the bound $|J(\pi)-J(\nu)|\le\sum_t span_t\sqrt{I(s_t;a_t)/2}$, with a sharp constant. Hence, an MDP's adaptivity gap is bounded by the information that its optimal policy uses. Estimating $J(\pi)-J(\nu)$ from $\pi$'s own bandit-feedback logs takes $\Theta(\bar\chi^2 \log(1/\eta)/\delta^2)$ rounds, where $\bar\chi^2$ is an average $\chi^2$-divergence. At the same time, deterministic non-constant policies cannot be audited at all. We also provide an importance-sampling auditor for episodic MDPs.


<div id='cs.DS'></div>

# cs.DS [[Back]](#toc)

### [328] [Gaussian Partitioning for Reliable Learning of Multi-Index Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05264)
*Ilias Diakonikolas, Giannis Iakovidis, Daniel M. Kane, Mingchen Ma*

Main category: cs.DS

Abstract: We introduce Gaussian partitioning, an algorithmic primitive for distributions smooth with respect to a Gaussian. It partitions space into polyhedral regions so that almost all mass lies in thin strips restricting a few orthogonal directions. Within each strip, the conditional distribution approximately matches low-degree Gaussian moments in the unrestricted directions. Our construction builds on logconcave partitioning of Diakonikolas, Kane, and Ma (FOCS 2026). Under input marginals smooth with respect to the standard Gaussian, we obtain agnostic learners with error $(1+c)\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon$ for broad classes of Boolean multi-index models, including functions of halfspaces, low-dimensional convex concepts, and low-dimensional polynomial threshold functions. For functions of $k$ halfspaces, both agnostic and testable agnostic learners achieve this error with dimension dependence $d^{\mathrm{poly}(k/c)}$, improving the previous exponential dependence on $k/c$ in the agnostic exponent. For learning from positive examples with imperfect unlabeled data (PUI), we give algorithms and Statistical Query (SQ) lower bounds that qualitatively characterize complexity through Gaussian moment matching. For fixed smoothness and positive-mass parameters, we learn intersections of $k$ halfspaces with dimension dependence $d^{O(\log(k/\epsilon))}$ and matching SQ lower bounds, improving the previous $d^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}$ upper bound even for one halfspace. A general reduction from agnostic reliable learning to PUI yields negative reliable learners for these intersections with the same dimension dependence, for fixed smoothness. Finally, for PQ learning and testable learning with distribution shift, we prove qualitatively optimal SQ lower bounds of $d^{\Omega(1/\epsilon^2)}$, even for intersections of two halfspaces with Gaussian training data and arbitrary test distributions.


<div id='cs.NI'></div>

# cs.NI [[Back]](#toc)

### [329] [Hierarchical Intelligent Evolutionary Optimization for UAV-Assisted NOMA-MEC Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04474)
*Yaxuan Jiao, Jiaxuan Lv, Jyoti Sahni, Wuyungerile Li, Winston K.G. Seah*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: The integration of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs), mobile edge computing (MEC) and non-orthogonal multiple access (NOMA) provides an effective solution for computation-intensive and latency-sensitive services in infrastructure-deficient environments. However, the joint optimization of UAV trajectories, task offloading, communication and computing resources under heterogeneous task priorities results in a highly coupled mixed-integer non-convex problem. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a Hierarchical Intelligent Evolutionary Algorithm (HIEA) for priority-aware multi-UAV cooperative NOMA-MEC networks, which integrates multi-head deep Q-network (MH-DQN)-based intelligent decision generation, communication-aware initialization, adaptive evolutionary exploration and successive convex approximation (SCA)-based precision refinement. Specifically, a novel communication-aware initialization mechanism, termed Channel Quality Indicator (CQI)-guided Load-Aware Trajectory Initialization (CQI-LATI), is designed to incorporate CQI and task workload characteristics into evolutionary population construction, generating high-quality and diversity-preserving initial populations. Theoretical analysis characterizes HIEA's search-space reduction, the quality-diversity trade-off of CQI-guided initialization, and the accuracy-efficiency trade-off of SCA refinement, and extensive simulations demonstrate that HIEA outperforms benchmark schemes, with gains of up to 6.5% in throughput, 13.6% in task completion rate and 15.3% in energy efficiency.


<div id='eess.SY'></div>

# eess.SY [[Back]](#toc)

### [330] [When Music Needs Meaning: Semantic Communication as a Transformative Enabler for the Musical Metaverse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03995)
*Ali Al Housseini, Jaime Llorca, Cristina Rottondi, Luca Turchet, Tiziano Leidi, Omran Ayoub*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: The Musical Metaverse (MM) enables geographically distributed musicians, audiences, sound engineers, and AI agents to interact through synchronized audio, visual, kinematic, and haptic streams, imposing latency, synchronization, bandwidth, and scalability requirements that are difficult to satisfy with conventional bit-level communication. This paper argues that semantic communication, understood here as the transmission of musically meaningful information rather than raw audio-visual-haptic samples, provides a principled communication paradigm for the MM. We define the MM from a communication perspective and derive modality-specific require ments; propose a three-pillar framework for musical semantic communication comprising semantics extraction, semantic-aware orchestration, and cross-modal semantic synchronization; survey representative work across semantic communication, music AI and edge orchestration, identifying the gaps that prevent their integration; and propose a semantic-first reference architecture based on device-side semantic encoders, edge semantic routers, and cross-modal semantic synchronization. The resulting framework positions the MM as a concrete and demanding use case for 6G semantic communication.


### [331] [Large Language Models for Forecasting of Electricity Price Spikes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04354)
*Liang Lin, Jianguo Zhu, Gang Lei, Allen Wang, Xin Lu*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: Can market notices read by a large language model (LLM) improve short-horizon electricity price spike forecasts? We study five regions of Australia's National Electricity Market. A local LLM extracted features once per notice, only notices visible at the forecast origin were used, and a gradient-boosting model produced onset probabilities. In an out-of-sample development back-test (July 2022 to July 2024), notice features did not lower the 60-minute Brier score of a numeric model including the market operator's next-hour forecasts. At 24 hours they did, but rule-based features did so almost equally and attribution to notice content was not established. A locally frozen protocol, not publicly deposited, governed a retrospective single-use holdout from 15 August to 17 October 2026. At 60 minutes, the hypothesis that LLM features lower the numeric model's Brier score was not established (H1; relative Brier skill -0.09%), so the planned comparison with rule-based features was not tested (H2). At 24 hours, the hypothesis that LLM features lower the score against a text-free control was not established (H3; +0.37%, 95% CI [-9.74, 1.39], inconclusive). By the frozen wording rule the non-significant 24-hour improvement comes mainly from recalibration and cannot be attributed to notice content, although post hoc the decomposition could not separate recalibration from resolution. The 24-hour base rate was 1.56% on the holdout against 14.0% in development. With 5 onsets on 3 days and 24-hour positives in 2 of 22 three-day blocks, the holdout neither established nor excluded a 24-hour effect.


### [332] [OperAItor: An Agentic Framework for Fault-Tolerant Operation of Chemical Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05179)
*Javal Vyas, Mehmet Mercang{ö}z*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: OperAItor, an open-source framework for developing and evaluating large language model agents for supervisory fault management in chemical processes. The framework is demonstrated on a dynamic reactor-distillation process representing isopropanol dehydrogenation, with physically based thermodynamics and modified reaction kinetics to permit liquid-phase operation. The process is represented by a 113-variable differential-algebraic model and operates at a nominal feed rate of 100kmol/h, producing approximately 4t/h of acetone. The simulated plant includes 8 PI control loops, 22 process alarms, 5 duty-standby pump trains, and 11 independent safety instrumented functions. The agent architecture integrates multivariate statistical process monitoring, fault diagnosis, knowledge-graph retrieval, action planning, and supervisory action generation. Five action-validation configurations are investigated, comprising operation without validation, an LLM-based judge, digital-twin simulation, and deterministic admissibility checks with and without consequence projection. The framework is evaluated using four LLMs across 20 scenarios involving single and multiple simultaneous faults. Without agent intervention, 18 scenarios result in plant stops, corresponding to an economic loss of $399,000 relative to fault-free operation under the assumed benchmark economics. For GPT-5 nano, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B, validated agent configurations reduce the number of stops to 5-8 and recover $265,000 of the loss. The study provides a reproducible benchmark for comparing agent architectures, language models, and action-validation methods for supervisory fault management in chemical processes.


### [333] [Comparing model representations and fitting methods for port-Hamiltonian system identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05031)
*Sølve Eidnes, Håkon Noren Myhr*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: Port-Hamiltonian models describe physical systems by separating stored energy, dissipation and external inputs. Learning such models from data requires a choice of representation and fitting method. We compare using neural networks, analytic expressions and combinations of the two for representing the different components of the port-Hamiltonian system, and compare fitting the models with gradient-based optimisation or sparse regression. Testing on simulations of mechanical and electrical systems, with and without added noise, we present prediction accuracy and computational cost of the different approaches.


### [334] [Learning curves in natural and artificial systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05224)
*Dmitry A. Novikov*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: A general mathematical model of experience formation is considered, in which the learning level is defined as the probability that the next value of the uncertainty factor has already been mastered and not forgotten. Within this model the shape of the learning curve is derived, and it turns out to be determined primarily by the distribution of situation occurrence: a uniform distribution yields an exponential curve, whereas a power-law distribution yields a power-law curve whose exponent equals the ratio of the distribution exponent to that same exponent increased by one. Both mechanisms generating power-law curves, established earlier in the analysis of debugging, assembly and repair activities, are shown to have been independently rediscovered in deep learning theory as the "frequency" and "spectral" mechanisms of scaling laws for artificial neural networks, while the exponential curves of machine learning (contraction operators, strongly convex optimization, stationary reinforcement) are generated by the same constancy of the contraction coefficient that was revealed half a century earlier in models of radioactive decay, the first-order lag element, and others. A solution is given for the problem of choosing a training distribution that differs from the deployment distribution. A methodological consequence is discussed: three distinct mechanisms generate curves indistinguishable in shape, so an observed learning curve does not allow one to judge the "internal" structure of the learning system.


<div id='physics.plasm-ph'></div>

# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [335] [From Forecasting to Feedback: Evaluating Learned Dynamics for Tokamak Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03690)
*Guoyang Shi, Siqi Ding, Xuanhe Wang, Pei Guo, Changquan Yu, Jiale Hu, Jiayi Zhi, Zhengyuan Chen, Jianguo Chen, Tianyuan Liu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

Abstract: Learned temporal models are moving beyond offline forecasting toward interactive environments for decision making, yet logged-trajectory accuracy need not predict closed-loop behavior under policies optimized in the learned model. This forecasting-control gap matters in tokamak magnetic control, where coupled plasma-circuit dynamics and actuator constraints can amplify errors through feedback. Using a calibrated free-boundary Grad-Shafranov solver, we collect four datasets in a 2x2 design over physics-parameter and current-reference coverage. At matched budgets of 500, 1k, 3k, and 5k rollouts, we train 16 action-conditioned Transformers; PPO policies optimized in the 5k surrogates are then evaluated without adaptation on six reference-solver conditions. Only joint-coverage D4 shows monotonically decreasing cross-condition mean error, whereas D1 improves in distribution but degrades out of distribution.
