Binary completion masks substantial closed-loop differences: D2-D4 each complete 83.3% of conditions, but reach on average 99.0%, 94.7%, and 90.5% of the evaluation horizon, while their worst conditions reach 94.2%, 68.2%, and 43.2%. Policies trained with action noise complete 5/24 matched conditions, compared with 19/24 under nominal training, and have a lower mean horizon-capped episode length. Based on one trained policy per configuration and one evaluation realization per condition, the results support combining prediction error, episode completion, condition-level duration, and tracking quality.


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [336] [Reasoning about Shared-Qubit Concurrency in Quantum Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04274)
*Mingsheng Ying*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: In this paper, we develop a semantic model for the parallel execution of quantum circuits, providing a formal foundation for studying shared-qubit concurrency in quantum computing. As in classical shared-memory concurrency, parallel quantum circuits may interfere with one another through operations on shared qubits. However, quantum entanglement and the noncommutativity of quantum operations introduce additional challenges for reasoning about their correctness. To address these challenges, we generalise the classical Owicki-Gries method to quantum computing at the level of quantum circuits. Our approach enables compositional reasoning about parallel quantum circuits by separating the verification of individual components from the analysis of interference between them. We formulate proof rules for establishing the correctness of parallel quantum circuits and prove their soundness with respect to the proposed semantic model.


### [337] [Spectral Quantum Memory for Implicit Neural Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04574)
*Daniel A. Moroz-Broitman, Eliya Nachmani*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Implicit neural representations (INRs) model signals as continuous functions from coordinates to values, but their performance depends strongly on how spectral capacity is allocated. Yet most Quantum INR (QINR) architectures treat spectral components as independent outputs of separate quantum blocks. We introduce the Spectral Quantum Memory Model (SQMM), a memory coupled quantum INR in which sequential hybrid data reuploading layers (bands) communicate through a shared coherent quantum memory register. Each band reads the memory, computes on its own coordinate upload, and writes part of its state back, so later bands can build on the computation of earlier ones. When memory read and write layers are input independent, we prove that the memory state forms an operator valued Fourier series whose support is contained in the Minkowski sum of the initial memory spectrum and the local band spectra. Downstream bands then condition their Fourier coefficients on the spectral content stored by earlier bands, and the memory coupling creates no new primitive frequencies. This theory motivates a spectral memory alignment loss that encourages memory to encode the spectrum expressed by the deployed prefix of the model. Across audio and image reconstruction benchmarks, SQMM improves over compact QINR and INR baselines.


### [338] [Hardware-Aware Design and Layout Selection in Quantum Reservoir Computing for Insurance Claims Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03891)
*Renáta Rusnáková, Eugen Šlapak, Gaurav K. Pandey, Juraj Gazda*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Quantum reservoir computing (QRC) processes temporal information through quantum dynamics, but it remains unclear whether small QRC models can forecast real data competitively with classical models and whether their predictions survive the transfer from ideal simulation to noisy hardware. We address both questions for a six-qubit gate-based reservoir that forecasts next-day motor-insurance claim counts. Five reservoir candidates were selected and frozen using hardware-native interaction graphs, Trotterized Ising dynamics, memory and measurement-robustness criteria, circuit cost, and calibrated-noise screening. On 232 held-out test days, the ideal candidates reached root-mean-square errors (RMSEs) of 5.66-6.12 claims/day, compared with 5.36 for ridge regression. For four of the five, the difference from ridge was not significant under a 95% block bootstrap, and their accuracy was comparable with classical recurrent and reservoir baselines. On the 156-qubit IBM Kingston processor, we ran 63 evaluations in 21 paired rounds, comparing hardware-aware, random-native, and Qiskit optimization level 1 layouts. Hardware-aware placement gave lower RMSE than Qiskit in every round, with a maximum transfer distortion of 7.59 claims/day (11.84 for Qiskit), and avoided the severe transfer failures seen for some random layouts. Most of the hardware distortion appeared as a systematic forecast shift, which a short hardware probe on training inputs detected before the test runs. Longer replay windows raised circuit cost without a consistent gain in forecasting accuracy or hardware transfer. Small QRC models can thus reach competitive accuracy on this task, and hardware-aware layout selection makes their transfer to IBM Kingston more consistent.


### [339] [Data informed Ansatz Selection for Variational Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05079)
*Manav Seksaria, Advith Desu, Anil Prabhakar*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Choosing a variational quantum circuit (VQC) usually means searching over ansatze and depths, and training each candidate. We give three rules that fix these choices from the data and the gate set alone, before any training. First, a circuit without entangling gates has a rank-one Fourier coefficient tensor on every subset of qubits. A singular value decomposition of a dataset's Fourier coefficients therefore indicates whether entanglement is needed, which qubits it connects, and a lower bound on the error of omitting it. Second, the rank of the quantum Fisher information matrix grows by $p_0$ per layer, after a first-layer deficit $d_0$, until it reaches a ceiling set by the dynamical Lie algebra. This law fixes the depth. Third, reading out all $2^N - 1$ Pauli-Z products from one batch of shots resolves half of the state's directions, the most any single-basis readout can. A single-qubit readout resolves an exponentially small fraction. For the Madelon dataset, the rules prescribe 7 blocks of 3 qubits at depth 3: a 239-parameter classifier with 78.5% test accuracy, found without a grid search. Ablations confirm three predicted effects: shallower circuits, smaller blocks and dropping reuploading all lower accuracy.


### [340] [Reading Why: Born-Measured Attention with Exact Word Evidence on Light and Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05355)
*Teerawat Tongloy, Jiraporn Pongsopa, Rangsan Jomtarak*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: When a language model answers, we can usually only estimate why. We build a transformer in which every step is a Born measurement - attention $|\langle q|k\rangle|^4$ normalised over each row, a measured product in place of the feed-forward nonlinearity, and a Born readout. With its denominators held at their values for the input, each class probability is a polynomial in the word amplitudes, so its integrated-gradient evidence is computed by a finite quadrature with no quadrature error: the words' contributions sum to the class probability above its no-information share, to $10^{-11}$ or better. On e-SNLI natural language inference, with about 98k parameters outside the embedding, the model reaches $76.98 \pm 0.17$% test accuracy, level with a softmax transformer of the same size ($76.62 \pm 0.36$%). Deleting the word its evidence ranks first changes the answer in 45% of test pairs, out of the 53% any single deletion can change, against 32% for the softmax model explained the same way and 40% by its attention x gradient. Attention can look more like the words annotators highlighted, yet accounts for the answer less well: deleting the word it ranks first changes the Born Transformer's answer in 32% of pairs. The same weights compile exactly to optoelectronic optics and to qubits, and a coherent attention layer becomes one circuit equal to the model (852 CNOTs on 23 qubits). On IBM's ibm_kingston, reading only the attention overlaps by Hadamard tests, the device gave the model's answers on two short test pairs and kept or changed them under deletion as the model does; the evidence itself did not yet survive the device, and we work out the shots and calibration that measuring it on hardware would need.


<div id='stat.AP'></div>

# stat.AP [[Back]](#toc)

### [341] [Behavioral Prior Elicitation via LLM for Heterogeneous-Agent Bitcoin Flash-Crash Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03660)
*Tran Phuong Linh Pham, Thi Minh Phuong Ngo, Phuong Thao Nguyen, Quang Minh Nguyen, Thai Hoa Ngo, Trong Nghia Nguyen*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: Bitcoin perpetual futures trade continuously with shallow liquidity and high price impact, making them prone to millisecond-scale flash crashes. Agent-based limit order book simulators are a natural tool for studying such events, but their behavioral parameters are usually hand-crafted or require full order book histories that are rarely available at scale. We propose a four-stage pipeline that constructs a reproducible 100 ms BTCUSDT flash-crash event panel from 66 detected events, elicits behavioral priors for four agent archetypes using an offline large language model conditioned on empirical microstructure anchors, instantiates a heterogeneous limit order book simulator with the validated priors, and applies causal discovery plus counterfactual analysis to the resulting synthetic panel. Across 500 simulation runs, LLM-elicited priors produce a crash rate of 0.176 versus 0.008 under uninformative uniform priors and generate heav- ier return tails. In the frozen counterfactual analysis, reducing leverage amplification by one within-sample standard deviation lowers the predicted crash rate from 0.176 to 0.136, whereas reducing directional pressure yields only a marginal change. These find- ings suggest that empirically anchored LLM elicitation can improve simulator fidelity and help identify plausible crash-amplification mechanisms in a controlled synthetic environment


### [342] [Development of tools for all-cause mortality prediction in heart failure patients: a systematic review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03665)
*Erik Aerts, Annika Rosengren, Martin Lindgren, Martin Adiels, Yinan Yu, Helen Söland*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: Heart failure is a serious medical condition associated with risk of acute deterioration, recurrent hospitalizations and high mortality making accurate mortality prediction potentially valuable for patient care and healthcare planning. Numerous prognostic models have been developed using conventional statistics (CS), machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL), but variation in study populations, heart failure definitions, prediction horizons and development practices may limit interpretation of applicability. We conducted a systematic review of mortality prediction models published between 2009 to the start of 2026 searching three databases (PubMed, IEEE and Scopus) for studies predicting all-cause mortality over a 1-60 months prediction horizon among patients with heart failure. Data extracted were cohort characteristics, heart failure definitions, model development, cohort size, validation practices, mortality, computational model design and reported performance. The risk of bias and applicability were assessed using the Prediction Model Risk of Bias Assessment Tool (PROBAST). Of 1516 publications identified, 56 met the inclusion criteria. Publication numbers increased over the observation period, alongside increasing use of ML/DL models and decreasing use of CS approaches. Most development cohorts (64%) were classified as Unknown/Undefined heart failure, reflecting inconsistent reporting and terminology that did not correspond with established heart failure guidelines. External validation was reported in 23% of studies. Overall, research in modeling heart failure mortality has grown alongside increasing adoption of ML/DL approaches. Greater standardization of study design and reporting is needed for meaningful comparison, reproducibility and evaluation of models for clinical application.


### [343] [Mapping urban heat from citizen-collected data: a scalable Gaussian process approach to correcting for preferential sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03659)
*Ellie K. Kim, Zachary D. Calhoun, Michael H. Bergin, David E. Carlson*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: The urban heat island (UHI) effect amplifies heat stress in cities, increasing heat-related illness, mortality, sleep disruption, and energy demand. Because UHI intensity varies at neighborhood scales, intra-city temperature mapping is essential for assessing public health impacts, yet existing monitoring networks are sparse. Crowdsourced weather stations provide dense, hourly observations but suffer from noise and systematic biases, including preferential sampling driven by where residents install sensors. We introduce two scalable Gaussian process frameworks which adjust for preferential sampling and enable statewide, high-resolution temperature prediction. The first integrates sampling correction directly within a sparse variational Gaussian process (SVGPR+PS). The second uses a two-stage estimator involving a log-Gaussian Cox process (LGCP+GP) that estimates the sampling intensity field and incorporates it into temperature prediction. Both methods rely on inducing-point approximations and variational inference to scale beyond traditional Gaussian processes. Applied to approximately 10,000 citizen stations across North Carolina, the models generate hourly temperature fields within minutes. Validation against independent urban heat campaign data shows that the preferential sampling correction improves predictive performance, increasing average afternoon correlations across four cities from 0.26 to 0.43. This scalable framework enables neighborhood-level UHI assessment and supports studies of heat exposure disparities statewide.


### [344] [Tail reliability in probabilistic electricity price forecasts: comparing model combination and conformal prediction in the GB day-ahead market](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03661)
*Anirudh Agrawal, Aidan O'Sullivan*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: Probabilistic forecasts of day-ahead electricity prices are increasingly used to manage price risk, yet competing models are usually ranked on aggregate scores that average over the price regimes in which uncertainty matters most. This study evaluates probabilistic forecasts of hourly EPEX SPOT GB day-ahead prices over a full held-out calendar year, applying a common pre-auction information boundary and rolling-origin design to linear quantile regression, a quantile regression forest (QRF), a LEAR-QRA benchmark and an encoder-decoder quantile long short-term memory (LSTM) network. Two routes to more reliable intervals are then compared: conformal recalibration and Quantile Regression Averaging (QRA). Aggregate scores separate the LSTM and QRF only weakly, with a non-significant 4.4% difference in continuous ranked probability score (CRPS), but their conditional behaviour differs sharply. Above 200 GBP/MWh the LSTM covered 0.12 of outcomes and QRF 0.71, consistent with the forest retaining realised spikes in its leaf distributions while the network learned a smooth upper tail. Conformal recalibration corrected marginal coverage but did not improve the interval score or materially repair spike coverage. QRA combining the LSTM, QRF and LEAR-QRA forecasts reduced CRPS to 5.14 GBP/MWh and raised coverage above 200 GBP/MWh to 0.65 at unchanged interval width. Recalibration and combination therefore address different failures and are not substitutes.


### [345] [Predicting Factor Decay: ML Models for Cross-Factor Predictability Erosion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03668)
*Chorok Lee*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: We develop ML models that predict when cross-factor predictability will erode, enabling prospective risk management. A decay prediction model achieves C-index $= 0.93$ on held-out factor pairs, providing early warning of signal erosion---the key predictor is initial signal strength: stronger signals decay faster (``regression to the mean''). An online regime detector enables real-time classification with 6.6-day average detection delay. Prior work explains why factors decay (arbitrage, publication effects); we predict when decay will occur. Limitation: The primary HML$\to$SMB signal does not replicate in post-break OOS (2013--2024); this null result is consistent with genuine decay but limits confirmatory claims. Motivation: Among 30 directed Fama-French factor pairs (1990--2024), predictability targeting the Size factor (SMB) decays fastest. Of 7 pairs showing exponential decay ($R^2 > 0.3$), 4 target SMB (Mkt-RF$\to$SMB, CMA$\to$SMB, HML$\to$SMB, RMW$\to$SMB; half-lives 2.4--4.1 years). This SMB-concentration pattern motivates the predictive models. Methodology: Using a regime-conditional Granger framework with Student-$t$ HMMs, we document structural breaks clustering around June 1998 ($p < 10^{-10}$). A pre-break OOS test (HMM trained 1990--1995, tested 1996--2007) confirms signals existed: HML$\to$SMB shows $p = 0.013$ (Elevated) and $p < 0.001$ (Crisis). The primary HML$\to$SMB signal does not replicate in post-break OOS (2013--2024), consistent with genuine decay rather than overfitting---after ${\sim}6$ half-lives, the expected signal is $<$1% of its 1990s peak. International analysis finds 2/4 non-US markets with Bonferroni-surviving OOS effects; correction for full specification search was not applied, so results remain exploratory.


### [346] [Integrating Machine Learning and Uncertainty Quantification for Real-Time Decision-Making in Digital Twins: A System-Centric Review and Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04269)
*Yi-Ping Chen, Ying-Kuan Tsai, Seul Lee, Vispi Karkaria, Dex Doksoo Lee, Wei Chen*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: Digital Twins are evolving from passive virtual representations into closed-loop systems that continuously synchronize with physical assets and drive real-time decisions. Yet predictive modeling, machine learning (ML), uncertainty quantification (UQ), and decision-making have been studied as separate capabilities, leaving limited understanding of their integration and maintenance during online operation. This narrative review, scoped by the functional requirements of real-time decision-making rather than by Digital Twin terminology alone, examines how such decisions are generated and sustained in engineering Digital Twins. We first examine foundational decision-making architectures and representative engineering applications, followed by methods that address their limitations across the decision loop. We identify optimization-based, policy-based, and knowledge-based approaches as the major pathways from virtual representation to action. Across them, ML plays three roles in decision-making: enabling efficient surrogate modeling, transforming heterogeneous physical feedback into decision-relevant information, and accelerating or directly generating decisions. UQ supports uncertainty-aware decisions and model adaptation, while continual validation, drift detection, and adaptation help maintain reliable operation as the physical system evolves. Together, these findings underscore the need for a shift from a model-centric to a system-centric perspective on Digital Twins, where its performance depends on the interactions with sensing, state estimation, computation, decision-making, and adaptation rather than on their standalone performance. Therefore, future research should shift from isolated algorithm development toward system-level co-design, scalable decision-making, and approaches to design, and data-efficient continual adaptation and validation for AI-enabled autonomous systems.


<div id='stat.ME'></div>

# stat.ME [[Back]](#toc)

### [347] [Post-stratified importance sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05238)
*Yuling Yao, Sergei Tikhonov*

Main category: stat.ME

Abstract: Importance sampling is a building block of modern statistical computing, but the weighted estimate is prone to high or infinite variance. Moreover, its empirical standard error is often unreliable, leaving importance sampling without practical uncertainty quantification. Motivated by multilevel regression and poststratification (MRP) in survey sampling, we propose post-stratified importance sampling (PIS) for estimating target expectations from proposal draws. PIS reformulates arbitrarily high-dimensional weighting into two one-dimensional probabilistic modeling problems: regression for the conditional mean given the importance ratio and marginal density estimation for the importance ratio. Different modeling choices yield a family of PIS estimators, encompassing ordinary importance sampling as a special case. We develop a model-based implementation that combines one-dimensional Gaussian process regression for the conditional mean and score-informed heavy-tailed density estimation for the marginal law, using both the observed weights and the available density evaluations. We study the oracle gains of PIS and establish consistency under general and model-specific conditions. Simulations and applications to cross-validation demonstrate improvements in estimation accuracy and uncertainty quantification.


<div id='physics.med-ph'></div>

# physics.med-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [348] [MedPhysBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Language Models on Medical Physics Calculations and Error Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05340)
*Omar Nusrat*

Main category: physics.med-ph

Abstract: Medical physicists turn a prescription into a delivered dose and check one another's calculations, because one error in a beam calibration or a monitor-unit (MU) calculation can affect every patient treated with it. MedPhysBench has ten seeded generators of calculation items in radiation therapy, diagnostic imaging, nuclear medicine and radiation protection, up to clinical tasks such as a TG-51 calibration worksheet. Reference answers come from code implementing the governing protocols, and a grader converts units and applies tolerance bands. From the same inputs, separate checking items test error detection, showing a colleague's worksheet or only the colleague's result, half correct and half with one planted error. On a held-out test split of 1,200 items (half for two models), 12 models scored 33.3 to 100.0% on calculation. Of the nine answers graded incorrect for the four best (99.3-100.0%), five contained model errors and four followed NCRP Report 151 on points our items misstate or leave open. Below them, most models did worst on level-3 clinical tasks, and Claude Sonnet 5.5 omitted the inverse-square correction in 19 of 20 isocentric MU items. Five models detected at least 96.7% of mostly large planted errors with at most 4% false alarms; for four of the other seven, checking only the result raised false alarms significantly. In an exploratory targeted test with 20 public cases of an omitted inverse-square correction, the three best models tested caught it in every worksheet, and three weaker models accepted 16 to 18 of 20 planted worksheets, one of them while applying the correction in most of its own calculations. Claude Opus 5.5 accepted 6 of 20 wrong results, although it applied the correction in all 20 of its own. We report the effect of two problems in our shielding items, found after the runs, and release the code, per-item outcomes and targeted-test responses.


### [349] [Weight-space programming for auditing clinical decision networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05341)
*Humza Nusrat, Omar Nusrat*

Main category: physics.med-ph

Abstract: Machine learning models in radiotherapy are commissioned by testing them on cases with known answers, which may not reveal a shortcut that the test cases share with the training data. Frontier language models have recently been reported to read and write the raw weights of small neural networks, an ability called weight-space programming. The purpose of this study was to determine whether such a model, given only a clinical protocol and a network's weights, can audit and repair the network. Networks with 433 to 4,801 parameters were trained to screen head-and-neck planning requests against four rules. Flaws were planted through the training data: confounds that hid each rule, a 3 Gy shift in the cord threshold, and a hidden trigger. Claude Opus 5.5 audited each network blind, without tools, data or code execution, and every claim and repair was tested on about 420,000 requests. The language model named the broken rule in 16 of 18 flawed networks (95% CI 67% to 97%), including all 15 with a confound or a threshold shift. It repaired each of the 16 by changing a median of 3 parameters. Fine-tuning on protocol-labeled requests also repaired every network, but changed nearly every parameter. Each confound and shift it found was visible in single units, and a simple statistic chosen for each flaw type found the same flaws. It cleared 2 of 5 healthy networks without being forced and found 1 of 3 triggers. Testing against the protocol detected every flaw, and black-box testing found the confounds and the shift faster. The results indicate that, in small networks, weight-space programming can locate and repair flaws that silence an input or shift a threshold, but cannot yet certify that a network is correct.


<div id='q-bio.BM'></div>

# q-bio.BM [[Back]](#toc)

### [350] [Predicting protein structures at any folding stage from sequence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04287)
*Lisheng Zhang, Qingsong Li, Zinan Xie, Yanting Yang, Qikui Yang, Peng Lu, Zhican Fu, Wentao Cui, Runtong Hou, Zhengwei Xie*

Main category: q-bio.BM

Abstract: Protein structure predictors reliably identify native conformations, but do not describe the continuum connecting unfolded ensembles to folded states. Here we present FoldScape, a sequence-conditioned deep learning framework that generates C$\alpha$ conformational ensembles at a specified folding fraction $Q$. FoldScape reconstructs three-dimensional chains through hierarchical, sliding-window regressions of one-dimensional inter-residue distances, followed by chain assembly and sampling with a rectified-flow decoder. It is trained on multi-scale coarse-grained folding trajectories and all-atom mechanical-unfolding trajectories. Across 20 human proteins (36-857 residues), at matched sample size, FoldScape covers about an order of magnitude more of the folding coordinate than seed-varied AlphaFold 3 ensembles, robustly across proteins, order parameters and binning choices. AlphaFold 3 better anchors the immediate native vicinity, which FoldScape does not reach, making the approaches complementary. FoldScape reconstruction accuracy remains approximately constant along $Q$, from collapsed coil to near-native globule, rather than deteriorating away from the native state. A stride-one crop-and-superpose scheme extends inference to chains several times longer than a single chain-level forward pass. We also show that training trajectories resolve mechanical unfolding at residue resolution, that elongation-conditioned ensembles exhibit reproducible continuous observables across pulling velocities, and that partially unfolded chains form geometrically coherent segments. FoldScape thus combines broad coverage of folding stages with stable reconstruction accuracy, without per-protein simulation.


### [351] [Ablating Design Choices for Protein Ensemble Generators Across Structural Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05055)
*Eoin Quinn, Oliver Bent*

Main category: q-bio.BM

Abstract: Generative models of protein conformational ensembles inherit much of their design from structure prediction for folded proteins. Ordered and disordered proteins, however, occupy distinct structural regimes, and we investigate how design choices hold up across them. Using a minimal C$\alpha$ flow-matching model, we ablate choices of architecture, sequence conditioning, rigid-body alignment and training source, and evaluate each variant against experimental SAXS and NMR chemical-shift data partitioned into ordered, disordered, multi-domain and transitional proteins. Mixture-of-experts routing, a static pair representation and a disorder probe have no detectable effect, a smaller protein language model performs worse on ordered proteins but not on disordered ones, and alignment in the loss degrades agreement with SAXS. Replacing coarse-grained simulations of disordered proteins with fragment-assembled ensembles improves agreement with chemical shifts at a cost to SAXS, while an equal mixture of the two keeps most of the gain at a smaller cost. This points to the training data, rather than the architecture, as the main constraint.


<div id='cs.NE'></div>

# cs.NE [[Back]](#toc)

### [352] [HyVox: Rethinking Low-Power Voice Intelligence towards Neuromorphic-von Neumann Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03735)
*Snehasis Addy, Eduardo Calle Ortiz, Prashant Shenoy, Phuc Nguyen*

Main category: cs.NE

Abstract: We present HyVox, a hybrid neuromorphic--von Neumann system for low-energy open-set speaker identification under tight energy budgets. HyVox splits the workload at the speaker embedding. A neuromorphic processor embeds the speech with sparse, event-driven computation, and a von Neumann microcontroller holds the speaker gallery and makes every decision: it scores queries by cosine similarity, rejects unknown speakers, and enrolls new users by writing a prototype, with no weight update. On the neuromorphic processor, a hierarchical temporal abstraction (HTA) SNN backbone learns a synaptic time constant per leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neuron, allocating 832 neurons across fast, medium, and slow timescales. A 32-neuron spiking embedding extractor (SEE), pretrained on episodes that each enroll new speakers and reject outsiders, turns the backbone output into an embedding for speakers unseen in training; its 32 spike counts are the only interface between the two processors. To fit the chip's synapse and integer budgets, we compress the backbone 6.5x to 29.0k synapses, train it for integer bit-shift dynamics, and fine-tune it with chip-accurate spiking training (CAST). CAST trains through a differentiable replica of the chip's integer neuron update, derived from its cycle-accurate simulator, so that gradient descent optimizes the exact weights and thresholds the chip runs; it doubles the top-1 accuracy of the mapped backbone on unseen speakers. On 20 Common Voice households of unseen speakers, HyVox reaches 99.6\% top-1 and 5.5\% rejection EER with 2 members and 94.9\% and 13.2\% with 20, and the network deployed on the neuromorphic chip matches its trained model. Each inference on a 3-s window takes 0.96 s and 1.63 mJ of dynamic energy, 42\% lower latency than the fastest embedded ANN baseline and 13.5x less dynamic and 5.8x less total energy than the most energy-efficient one.


<div id='cs.DB'></div>

# cs.DB [[Back]](#toc)

### [353] [Navigating Runtime Dynamism in Semantic Query Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04248)
*Hengrui Zhang, Xupeng Miao*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: LLM-powered semantic queries increasingly combine relational processing with multiple LLM-based operators over unstructured data. Efficiently serving such queries is challenging due to runtime dynamism at two coupled levels. Across queries, dynamic arrivals and heterogeneous resource demands continuously change scheduling priorities. Within a query, heterogeneous characteristics across fine-grained input batches cause operator costs and selectivities to vary throughout execution, making a statically optimized plan unreliable. These two forms of dynamism create a circular dependency: inter-query scheduling requires estimates of remaining LLM work, while the remaining work depends on runtime plan choices whose costs become observable only during execution. We present SeqoFlow, a runtime framework that jointly adapts query plans and schedules concurrent semantic queries. SeqoFlow enumerates equivalence-preserving plan alternatives under operator dependencies and decomposes queries into fine-grained units, enabling safe plan adaptation and query preemption at batch boundaries. To support both decisions without exhaustively profiling candidate plans on the target LLM, SeqoFlow uses lightweight speculative estimation to capture runtime costs. These estimates jointly guide plan adaptation and inter-query scheduling, closing the loop between query optimization and LLM inference. Experiments across diverse query structures, data distributions, and arrival rates show that SeqoFlow substantially reduces end-to-end latency compared to existing semantic query systems, adaptive query processing techniques, and conventional scheduling policies.


<div id='eess.IV'></div>

# eess.IV [[Back]](#toc)

### [354] [Learning to read residual images for single-pixel photography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03729)
*Hongjun An, Shuaizhe An, Tong Tian, Zhe Sun*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: Continuous video scales in cost with camera count, resolution and recording duration, yet many monitoring tasks seek events rather than complete images and can operate on a compact single-pixel measurement stream. Recovering a chosen interval as video is then desirable, but at 0.24\% of the pixel count the scene correlation image looks like noise and per-clip optimization takes minutes. Correlating the residual of an estimate with the coding patterns nevertheless gives a residual image, the gradient of a centered measurement loss. Lifted through a pretrained video decoder, its agreement with the true error exceeds that of control decoders. Video-latent inversion (VLI) then outperforms frame-wise image-latent reconstruction at 1{,}000 bucket values. Measurement-Gradient-Conditioned Video Flow (MGV-Flow), trained once to read the lifted residual image with preceding frames, reconstructs five frames in 1.55\,s at 22.9\,dB median PSNR, against 398\,s and 18.1\,dB for VLI, with the largest gains in high-motion scenes. Directional fidelity ranks 30 coding configurations and selects one needing a third fewer bucket values. On a physical system, MGV-Flow outperforms per-frame reconstruction without retraining. A sliding window over the bucket-value stream yields 3.2 frames per second on one RTX~4090 and, in theory, 20 frames per second on a data-centre accelerator. Demonstrations are available at this https URL .


### [355] [Measure Before You Repair: An Explainable Agent for Blind Restoration of Multi-Artifact 3-D MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03996)
*Necati Sefercioglu, Mehmet Ozan Unal, Metin Ertas, Altan Cakir, Isa Yildirim*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: A brain MRI scan can carry an unknown mix of artifacts, so restoration has to be blind, yet most deep-learning methods are built to correct one known artifact. We present Dx-Rx, an agent in which a multimodal large language model (MLLM) measures suspected artifacts before it repairs them. To our knowledge, it is the first agent to restore whole 3-D MRI volumes, whereas earlier MRI agents restore 2-D slices. In a loop, it looks at slices of the 3-D volume, calls five diagnostic tools (noise, spike, ghosting, bias field, motion) and four restoration tools in any order it chooses, checks each repair, can return to any earlier version to change the order, and ends with a radiology-style report of its diagnostic reasons, the repairs it applied and their results. We simulated up to five artifacts on 148 whole 3-D T1-weighted volumes, at levels calibrated to radiologists' grades of real scans. An MLLM that only looked at the image, as earlier MRI agents do, removed at most 19.7 % of the artifact error. With the best of five MLLMs, Dx-Rx removed a mean 64.5 %, nearly matching the 64.6 % of the same tools given the ground-truth artifacts, which are never known for a clinical scan. With all five models, it made no volume worse and was on par with or better than an expert rule-based pipeline built from the same tools (62.3 %). Code and demo are public at this https URL .


<div id='physics.optics'></div>

# physics.optics [[Back]](#toc)

### [356] [LIPS-PC: Learning Illumination Patterns for Single-Pixel Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04780)
*Tomer Porat, Talya Eden, Eliahu Cohen*

Main category: physics.optics

Abstract: In single-pixel (ghost) imaging, known illumination patterns probe an object and a detector with no spatial resolution returns one number per pattern. Each measurement costs acquisition time and photons and, for ionizing probes, contributes to delivered dose. Motivated by automated radiographic defect screening, where a class label suffices, we introduce Learning Illumination Patterns for Single-Pixel Classification (LIPS-PC). The patterns are learned end-to-end while the measurement operator remains one fixed linear matrix, identical for every input. We parameterize it as a residual correction to a two-dimensional discrete cosine transform (2D-DCT), $D=D_{\mathrm{base}}+\Delta$, and jointly train the correction and a compact classifier using cross-entropy, without a reconstruction objective. This yields a task-driven linear embedding through optimization. We evaluate LIPS-PC numerically on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST under a controlled protocol, then on RIAWELC, a real industrial X-ray weld-radiograph dataset. Across five budgets, $M\in{8,16,32,64,128}$, corresponding to sampling ratios from below 1% to 12.5%, the learned operator outperforms the best tested fixed transform by 9-15 percentage points at $M=8$ on all three datasets. Its eight-measurement accuracy requires roughly 16-32 measurements with the best fixed transform, indicating a $2\times$ to $4\times$ saving at comparable accuracy. The advantage generally shrinks as $M$ increases, with a small reversal on RIAWELC at higher budgets. These results show that learning what to measure is especially valuable when measurements are scarce.


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [357] [A Simulation-Driven Digital Workflow Integrating Bayesian Optimization for Early-Stage Process Design: A Methyl Acetate Production Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03673)
*Trung Ngoc Trinh, Trung Thanh Nguyen, Nikolaos Tsakoumis, Roman Tschentscher*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

Abstract: Methyl acetate production via direct carbonylation of dimethyl ether and carbon monoxide involves strong nonlinear interactions among reactor operation, recycle structure, and energy consumption, making conventional simulation-based optimization computationally demanding. In this work, a data-driven framework integrating steady-state process simulation, surrogate modeling, and Bayesian optimization was developed for process intensification. A dataset of 2730 steady-state simulations was generated using a Python-based automated process simulation workflow and a structured design of experiments across the operating domain. Surrogate models were trained to predict carbon conversion yield (C-yield) and cost of specific energy consumption (C-SEC), with neural network-based models showing the best predictive performance and robustness. These models were integrated into a multi-objective Bayesian optimization framework to identify optimal operating regions. Results indicate that high C-yield (up to ~95%) can be achieved with reduced energy cost through lower recycle flow rates and compression demand. Pareto analysis revealed a nonlinear trade-off between C-yield and C-SEC, where further improvements near the conversion limit require disproportionately higher energy input. Validation against rigorous simulations showed deviations below 3% for C-yield and 10% for C-SEC.


### [358] [A local orchestration harness for molecular design with foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03676)
*Sakib Mostafa, Md Atik Ahamed, James Zou, Lei Xing, Md Tauhidul Islam*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

Abstract: Molecular design requires coordinated generation, property prediction and iterative editing, with each decision linked to the correct chemical structure and supporting evidence. Integrating these capabilities in practice presents three major challenges: retaining local control of molecular data, connecting specialized models across tasks and verifying that proposed changes satisfy chemical constraints. Here we present MolCrew, a locally deployable system that coordinates four separately trained molecular specialists with a language-model orchestrator and RDKit calculations. The specialists generate structures from descriptions, describe molecular structures, predict biological endpoints and propose edits towards specified goals. Separate model weights allow each capability to be evaluated and updated independently within the workflow. RDKit calculations check structural validity, quantify descriptor changes and assess specified structural constraints, while returned observations guide further tool calls. We evaluated low-rank adaptation of the orchestrator for tool-use decisions and failure recovery while leaving specialist weights unchanged. On 3,300 ChEBI-20 test examples, MolCrew achieved 96.4% exact structure recovery, compared with 38.7% for published Atomas-large results. Across eight MoleculeNet datasets evaluated using scaffold splits, mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was 0.851, compared with 0.770 for published Atomas results. In a recorded evaluation using the unadapted orchestrator, 1,554 of 1,699 requests (91.5%) passed every predefined workflow check. By preserving inspectable links between molecular proposals, model outputs and chemical calculations, MolCrew provides a modular framework for computational candidate assessment and refinement under local data control.


### [359] [All Paths All at Once: A Quantum Monte Carlo and Neural Committor Approach to Reaction Pathway Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03699)
*Chien-Chai Chang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

Abstract: Locating competing reaction pathways is difficult when potential-energy gradients are unavailable. Here we present tReax\_expedition, an energy-only approach combining coupled Monte Carlo exploration, stochastic quench labelling and restrained continuation. Exploration identifies connected states, while continuation resolves candidate channels and records sampled chain maxima. Single-search benchmarks on three two-dimensional potentials recovered an intermediate and competing routes without transition-state guesses. For eight of nine gas-phase reactions on GFN2-xTB, sampled chain maxima differed from independently refined saddle energies by less than 0.76~kJ\,mol$^{-1}$. Alanine dipeptide instead revealed incomplete conformational relaxation, showing that route connectivity can persist despite structural and energetic errors. Neural committor learning and local straddling-pair sampling connect the resulting paths to transition-region sampling, although molecular shooting diagnostics indicate limited committor calibration. These results show how energy-only exploration and continuation recover candidate pathways, while distinguishing route connectivity from geometric localization, barrier resolution and finite-temperature transition statistics.


### [360] [Complete Donor-Set Guidance for Frozen Diffusion Docking Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03731)
*Hyunwoo Kim, Insoo Park, Ziseok Lee, Wooyeol Lee, Yoorim Gang, Kyungsu Kim*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

Abstract: Metalloprotein docking requires a ligand pose to satisfy both global pocket fit and the identity and geometry of metal-coordinating atoms. General-purpose diffusion docking models do not explicitly condition generation on externally specified complete ligand donor sets, and their predicted poses can exhibit incorrect metal-donor relationships. Metal-aware models address coordination during training and generation; here we ask whether external donor information can correct already-trained general-purpose models without retraining. We propose Complete-Identity Contract Guidance (CICG), which applies a supplied complete donor set through translation, rotation, and torsion updates, and Historical Contract Transfer (HCT), which supplies donor identities from earlier complexes at compatible receptor sites. We compare HCT with trajectory-local controls retaining the historical donor count with or without elemental composition. On 86 assessable HCT-covered queries, HCT-guided CICG increases frozen-confidence-selected joint success from 21.5% to 36.6% on SurfDock and from 25.0% to 34.9% on DiffDock-L. The joint criterion requires correct global ligand placement, complete native donor recovery, coordination geometry, preserved stereochemistry, and absence of fatal receptor clashes. Composition-matched guidance reaches 33.14% and 34.30%, leaving the additional benefit of fixed historical membership unresolved. Our results therefore support donor-set guidance as a conditional correction for frozen docking models, while identifying donor-source reliability and physical-quality preservation as the main barriers to broader use.


<div id='math.AP'></div>

# math.AP [[Back]](#toc)

### [361] [Solving the Nonlinear Westervelt Equation in Ultrasound Propagation Using Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05130)
*Luke Oluwaseye Joel*

Main category: math.AP

Abstract: Modeling the nonlinear acoustic wave propagation in biological tissues needs accuracy, especially in ultrasound imaging and therapeutic applications. The 1D Westervelt equation, \( p_{xx} - p_{tt} + \gamma\, p_{ttt} + \eta\,(p^{2})_{tt}=0, \) could be used to describe a finite-amplitude ultrasound propagation in these tissues. This equation accounts for nonlinearity, \(\eta\), and attenuation, \(\gamma\). To solve this equation, we observe that traditional solvers may struggle to do so correctly. This is due to the difficulties arising from the nonlinear nature of the equation. We solve the equation by defining three synthetic cases - linear (\(\eta=0.001\), \(\gamma=10^{-6}\)), shock-dominated (\(\eta=0.02\), \(\gamma=10^{-6}\)), and dissipation-dominated (\(\eta=0.01\), \(\gamma=5\times10^{-4}\)) - and using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) to showcase the solution. A sinusoidal activation function is used to capture the oscillatory behavior of the problem. The results of our study, with PDE residual converging to at least \(10^{-6}\), boundary condition loss to \(10^{-5}\), and the PINN loss converging to \(10^{-5}\) for each case at \(30,000\) epochs, show that \(\gamma\) scales the rate of amplitude decay, while \(\eta\) governs frequency-dependent phase speeds.


<div id='eess.AS'></div>

# eess.AS [[Back]](#toc)

### [362] [NepTTS-Bench: Native-Speaker Benchmarking and NepaliMOS for Nepali Text-to-Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03718)
*Ashish Thapa, Rupak Ghimire, Lekhnath Sharma Pathak*

Main category: eess.AS

Abstract: Nepali TTS has reached a point where multiple systems are available, but there is no shared evaluation to compare them. NepTTS-Bench was created to address what existing automated and manual evaluation systems lack for Nepali TTS. A controlled benchmark with phonological grounding which contains 205 sentences (77 minimal pairs targeting aspiration, retroflex/dental contrasts, nasalization, gemination, and schwa deletion) which is rated by 180 speakers. There are 6,962 ratings for 10 TTS configurations and two human references. Along with this 7 ASR systems were evaluated for round-trip CER. SCOREQ was also run as an automated baseline. The key finding is that automated evaluation like SCOREQ prefers proprietary systems like ElevenLabs but native listeners prefer Piper. This is contributed by the fact that most of the systems not properly tuned for Nepali tend to show Hindi phonological behaviour and our minimal pairs are designed to surface such contrasts. We also present NepaliMOS predictor which is fine tuned on collected ratings. NepaliMOS achieves system-level rho = 0.90 on the dense panel but drops to utterance-level rho = 0.43 under leave-one-system-out evaluation. This is statistically significant and confirms that NepaliMOS captures genuine signal but cross-provider transfer is limited. A human calibration is recommended for new systems for which we release a Python evaluation package for practitioners to run the benchmark against new systems.


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [363] [AIRoA-0: Long-Horizon, Precise, Cross-Embodiment Manipulation from Handheld Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03815)
*Yueh-Hua Wu, Kei Ota, Hanlong Li, Hayata Yuasa, Jason Wang, Kengo Ikeuchi, Kensuke Chiyoma, Makoto Sugiura, Masafumi Takahashi, Masatoshi Tateno, Naoaki Kanazawa, Nicola Scianca, Petr Khrapchenkov, Satoshi Otsubo, Takehiko Ohkawa, Takuya Okubo, Tatsuya Matsushima, Yuki Noguchi, Yuma Shin, Zixuan Zhong*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: AIRoA-0 (A-0) is a bimanual manipulation system learned from 16,151 hours of handheld demonstrations, with no robot data collected at any stage. The demonstrations are recorded with YUBI, our open-source handheld gripper, which lets people record manipulation data anywhere with no robot present. From a single instruction, A-0 sorts more than a dozen small parts into their numbered cells and changes the mop pads and brush of a robot vacuum; among other tasks, it unzips a pencil case and folds a towel. It performs these tasks using only a camera on each wrist, with no head or scene camera, at a site where none of its training data was recorded. Across the ten tasks of our evaluation, it averages a success rate of 78%, against 27% for the strongest of four other vision-language-action models post-trained on the same task demonstrations. We collect no teleoperation data and use no reinforcement learning or on-robot corrections, and the robot appears only at test time. The system learns which regions of each wrist view matter for the task, predicts the stage the task has reached, and decodes actions as a continuous stream. A-0 is embodiment-agnostic: it runs on robots that mount the YUBI gripper, such as a bimanual Franka, TX-G2, OpenArm and UR. We are releasing the dataset, to our knowledge the largest open dataset of handheld-gripper demonstrations with task and subtask instructions, together with the pre-trained weights and the post-training recipe. Videos of A-0 on every task are on the project page, this https URL . Anyone can build the gripper, record a task of their own and post-train the model on it in under four hours on a single node of eight H200 GPUs.


### [364] [RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850)
*Deng-Ke Wu, Yifan Jin, Yida Yang, Lei Qian, Zhaoye Zhou, Hongru Zhang, Kai Ye, Shuyuan Hu, Yiran Mao, Zewei Ye, Bo Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Simulation benchmarks are the standard way to compare robot policies, but each benchmark brings its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention, and evaluation is slow: 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes with one simulator and one model on one GPU take 359.7 minutes. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluator shows that simulation occupies CPU cores while the GPU averages 14.3% utilization. RoboScope Kit wraps each model once in a Python class called a Policy, and each benchmark once in an adapter around the benchmark's own evaluator, so the benchmark's rules and scoring stay unchanged. Scheduling runs simulator workers and Policy replicas as separate pools, sizes both from measured probes, and shards work deterministically across GPU instances; runtime optimization renders only the observations an action-chunk Policy reads. Adapters cover six benchmark families. On LIBERO-Long with a StarVLA checkpoint on one 48 GiB RTX 4090, the concurrency search selects twelve simulator workers sharing three Policy replicas, which evaluate 500 episodes 4.45$\times$ faster than the official StarVLA evaluation with equivalent success rates, 2.78$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 1.52$\times$ faster than vla-eval at its tuned configuration. With CUDA MPS, which lets the replicas run their kernels concurrently, the search selects sixteen workers sharing four replicas, 6.16$\times$ faster than StarVLA, 3.85$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 2.10$\times$ faster than vla-eval. On RoboTwin Easy, four GPUs with chunk-boundary observation complete the 500 episodes in 14.4 minutes, 25.04$\times$ faster than the single-GPU baseline, with identical success outcomes and terminal steps.


### [365] [A Practical Causal Framework for Autonomous Mobile Robots: From Simulation to Real-World Human Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04027)
*Luca Castri, Gloria Beraldo, Nicola Bellotto*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Causal inference has emerged as a powerful paradigm enabling robots to make safe, efficient decisions by reasoning about the ``why'' behind events. However, its real-world application is hindered by the difficulty of collecting causal data, especially in human-populated environments where training takes time, while users expect immediate operation. To this end, we tackle the challenge of transferring simulation-learnt causal relations to the real world with minimal effort. While retaining the invariant high-level causal structure, we empirically demonstrate the system adapts efficiently to real-world situations by updating only the causal model parameters with a limited set of new observations. Our results show that the parameter update enables rapid sim-to-real transfer. The framework runs efficiently onboard of a mobile robot and significantly outperforms a non-causal baselines navigating successfully and safely in complex human-shared scenarios.


### [366] [DiMuRe: Diagnosis-Guided Multilevel Recovery for Robotic Task Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04226)
*Renkai Yi, Yuwei Wu, Haoyu Zhang, Penghao Dai, Zhi Gao, Jin Chen, Che Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Disturbances can invalidate the conditions required by a robot's next action even when much of its task program remains useful. A recovery system must therefore choose a response suited to the current state. We present DiMuRe (diagnosis-guided multilevel recovery), a framework for robot-use agents that uses phase-specific evidence and method preconditions to select program reuse or predefined recovery skills, including bounded vision-language-action (VLA) grasping. After the original program exits, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) can generate corrective code from images and execution feedback. Local verification checks the outcome of each recovery attempt. We also introduce LIBERO-Disturb, a benchmark for robustness and recovery under mid-execution disturbances, supporting program-based agents and VLA policies. On 801 scheduled disturbed Encore development cases, Local DiMuRe (program reuse and recovery skills) achieves 77.65% complete-task success, compared with 58.68% for the original program under the same physical-step allowance. Full DiMuRe achieves 81.90% with terminal replanning, an additional step allowance, and early handoff. Local DiMuRe also uses fewer physical steps per scheduled episode than the original program.


### [367] [Latent Policy Barrier: Learning Robust Visuomotor Policies by Staying In-Distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05941)
*Zhanyi Sun, Shuran Song*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Visuomotor policies trained via behavior cloning are vulnerable to covariate shift, where small deviations from expert trajectories can compound into failure. Common strategies to mitigate this issue involve expanding the training distribution through human-in-the-loop corrections or synthetic data augmentation. However, these approaches are often labor-intensive, rely on strong task assumptions, or compromise the quality of imitation. We introduce Latent Policy Barrier, a framework for robust visuomotor policy learning. Inspired by Control Barrier Functions, LPB treats the latent embeddings of expert demonstrations as an implicit barrier separating safe, in-distribution states from unsafe, out-of-distribution (OOD) ones. Our approach decouples the role of precise expert imitation and OOD recovery into two separate modules: a base diffusion policy solely on expert data, and a dynamics model trained on both expert and suboptimal policy rollout data. At inference time, the dynamics model predicts future latent states and optimizes them to stay within the expert distribution. Both simulated and real-world experiments show that LPB improves both policy robustness and data efficiency, enabling reliable manipulation from limited expert data and without additional human correction or annotation.


### [368] [CLARA: A Cognitively-Driven Clarification Framework for Grounding Ambiguous Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04708)
*Dimitrios Bekris, Marios Glytsos, Panagiotis P. Filntisis, Niki Efthymiou, George Retsinas, Petros Maragos*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Robotics and AI systems are increasingly deploying large vision-language models (LVLMs) to follow high-level instructions, from routine household chores to more complex organizational tasks. Yet, broad directives like "clean the room" or "organize the desk" frequently omit crucial details about user preferences and specific arrangement of objects that influence the desired outcome. Without any further guidance, such models tend to overestimate their grasp of ambiguous instructions and frequently assume they have sufficient understanding despite missing important details. In this paper, we propose CLARA (Conversational cLArification for Robotic Arrangement), a cognitively-driven clarification framework that proactively elicits key information from users, in a similar fashion as humans' clarification seeking behavior. Through targeted questioning, CLARA refines ambiguous requests into structured, detailed instructions describing precisely how objects should be organized or rearranged, according to the user's preferences. These clarifications then guide a diffusion-based image generation pipeline, producing realistic visual renderings of the final, desired scene. This final rendering also serves as a visual confirmation of the system's understanding from the user. Experimental results and user studies demonstrate that CLARA leads to more accurate and consistent outcomes compared to another State-of-the-Art LVLM system, highlighting the importance of interactive clarification for bridging the gap between vague directives and fully grounded task specifications.


### [369] [LLMs as MDP Designers: A Dependency-Aware Agentic Framework for Automated Robot Policy Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04974)
*Hong Je-Gal, Jinwoo Kim, Chanbin Yi, Hyun-Suk Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: We investigate the automated generation of executable robot reinforcement learning (RL) policies from natural-language task descriptions. Rather than using large language models (LLMs) as execution-time decision makers, we use them as designers that construct RL pipelines before deployment, including Markov decision process (MDP) formulation and simulator implementation. We identify a phase-wise structure in this problem: MDP formulation is dependency-structured and sequential, whereas simulator implementation becomes modular once the formulation is fixed. Based on this structure, we propose ARPG, a dependency-aware agentic framework that performs sequential MDP modeling, localized verification and correction, and simulator-structured implementation while externalizing intermediate artifacts. ARPG targets a key failure mode of automated policy generation: executable code may still yield non-learnable policies when state, action, reward, and termination artifacts are not semantically aligned. Experiments in Isaac Sim show that ARPG improves modeling correctness, environment executability, and policy-generation success compared with monolithic LLM baselines and off-the-shelf agentic systems. Additional demonstrations on custom robotics, standard RL, and engineering-domain MDPs further suggest that the same modeling pipeline can be reused beyond the main benchmark suite.


### [370] [Do Agentic Policies Need to Look at Every Step? Geometric Abstraction for Reasoning and Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05058)
*Yihang Liu, Yiming Chen, Mingzhe Zheng*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Recent multimodal language models can reason directly about robot scenes, but it is unclear whether they need to repeatedly reinterpret raw visual observations at every step of an interaction. We propose shifting routine spatial reasoning from images to text and symbols through a 3D geometric abstraction that is refreshed locally from observations and read directly by the language models. Specifically, our interface represents scene geometry via typed, evidence-aware symbolic references to object parts, grounds them from calibrated RGB-D, and returns compact textual checkpoints with metric residuals after each action. Symbolic task structure can thus be reused across decisions while metric values are rebound to the current scene, with direct visual reasoning invoked only on demand. Across offline policy synthesis and continuous online execution, reasoning over grounded text and symbols improves multi-step manipulation completion while substantially reducing repeated multimodal queries, positioning explicit geometric state as an effective substrate for agentic robot policies.


### [371] [Trajectory Evaluation via Rollout-Free World Model for End-to-end Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05072)
*Junzhe Wang, Jinqing Zhang, Qingjie Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Although existing candidate trajectory evaluators have substantially improved end-to-end planning accuracy, their underlying mechanism remains suboptimal. Model-based evaluators typically make scoring future-aware by explicitly predicting candidate-conditioned future states over the planning horizon, which we term rollout. However, rollout incurs high inference cost and accumulates prediction errors. We argue that planning does not require reconstructing a full future scene for each candidate, but only future-relevant information for reliable comparison. We therefore propose RFWorld, a Rollout-Free World model for end-to-end trajectory evaluation. RFWorld constructs a shared scene memory and shapes it into a future-readable representation via lightweight training-time temporal readout with semantic supervision, keeping training overhead low. At inference time, candidate trajectories directly query the shared memory via sparse spatial readout for scoring, reducing computation by roughly the planning horizon times the number of candidate trajectories, while avoiding recursive rollout errors. Experiments on NAVSIM and Bench2Drive show that RFWorld learns a more planning-aligned world representation and achieves state-of-the-art performance.


### [372] [Asynchronous Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05155)
*Sombit Dey, Luc Van Gool, Danda Pani Paudel*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Scaling vision-language foundation models has significantly advanced generalist robot manipulation, but the increased model scale introduces computational latency that degrades real-time physical reactivity. Existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) architectures execute high-level semantic reasoning and action ex- ecution sequentially within the primary control loop, creating a fundamental tension between semantic-reasoning capability and robot agility. To address this, we propose Asynchronous Robot Policy (ARP), an architectural framework that decouples semantic reasoning from low-level action generation using asynchronous processes operating at independent frequencies. In ARP, a high-capacity Vision-Language Model (VLM) asynchronously updates a semantic memory at low fre- quency, while a lightweight Vision-Action Expert, conditioned on this semantic memory, generates robot actions at a much higher control frequency. Such a formulation, enables having a fast perception-action loop without compromising on the semantic capability. To overcome asynchronous memory staleness, ARP trains the vision-action expert on the semantic staleness and execution delay, with temporally mismatched observation across processes. On Franka manipulation tasks, ARP achieves an 18% higher success rate and 20% less time need for execution in quasi-static setups, while 3-5 times baseline performance in dynamic simulation environments. Furthermore, ARP enables operating the VLM at 10 times lower than its nominal frequency with no significant performance loss making it much more efficient than the baseline. These results highlight the architectural advantages of modeling robot policies as asynchronous processes, enabling real-world reactive deployment without sacrificing the scale of foundation models.


### [373] [RoboTryOn: Synthesizing Dexterous Robot Demonstrations from Human Videos via Virtual Try-On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05163)
*Seungyong Lee, Suhyeok Jang, Minyoung Kim, Jinwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Robot foundation models have shown remarkable performance in robot control, yet adapting them to a new task still relies heavily on teleoperated demonstrations. Egocentric human videos offer a scalable substitute, but overlaying rendered robots on human videos or retargeting human poses into robot actions leaves significant visual or action gaps for high-DoF robot embodiments. To address this limitation, we propose RoboTryOn, which treats human-to-robot translation as virtual try-on: try-on keeps the person and swaps the garment, RoboTryOn keeps the demonstrated motion and swaps the human for the robot. Specifically, RoboTryOn converts a human video into a video of the target robot specified by a reference image, while preserving the original task, objects, and background. To maintain visual consistency, we translate selected keyframes autoregressively and use them to guide video synthesis with motion conditioned on the human hand skeleton. Additionally, we use an inverse dynamics model (IDM) that incorporates depth features to infer action labels from synthesized videos for policy learning. Our experiments across multiple humanoid embodiments show that policies trained with our synthetic data improve the average success rate on novel tasks, including challenging bimanual manipulation, from 2% to 60%, without any real robot demonstration of those tasks. Moreover, training with our synthetic data matches the performance of policies trained solely on teleoperated demonstrations at approximately a third of the collection cost, demonstrating the cost efficiency of our approach.


### [374] [PyRUA-DataFarm: LLM Agent as a Cost-Efficient Teleoperator for Real-Ready Robot Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05320)
*Kewei Zhang, Juncheng Ma, Guodong Lv, Duomin Wang, Feixiang Ruan, Ruiyang Si, Chuanrui Zhang, Yuxuan Chen, Hao Chen, Yichen Liu, Yintianrun Zhang, Yufan Deng, Enze Xie, Daquan Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Teleoperation remains the default way to collect robot demonstrations, but every episode costs robot and operator time, and every new task starts from scratch. We ask whether a coding agent can take over the teleoperator's job. In our DataFarm, the agent receives a data request and one calibration episode of the robot in the target scene and rebuilds the task in simulation: it writes the scene, an expert controller, and a success judge. The simulator supplies exact actions, and a video model conditioned on real images renders each rollout as photographic video, generating all camera views together consistently. The output is real-ready data: synchronized camera views labelled with the robot's actions, on which a policy can be trained directly and deployed on the real robot. Independent judges certify the expert and every rendered episode before admission. On five real-robot tasks, policies trained only on farm data match policies trained on as many teleoperated episodes (93 vs. 84 successes in 100 trials), and under an unseen tablecloth and coloured light they keep 87 successes where the teleoperation-trained policies fall to 46. Adapting a task is fully automatic and costs \$21 of API usage on average, paid once per task, plus \$5 for a render contract; every further episode costs \$0.80 on average for simulation, rendering, and review, with no robot or operator. One hour of three-view data for a task costs \$107 on average, these costs included, slightly below a quote of \$117 for teleoperation in China and below \$290 in labour alone in North America.


### [375] [Hybrid Particle-Voxel Dynamics for Multimodal 3D World Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04492)
*Jimmy Wang, Yifan Chou, Kaifeng Zhang, Yixuan Wang, Binghao Huang, Tao Yu, Saleh Nabi, Yunzhu Li*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Predicting how the world evolves in response to robot actions is central to policy learning, evaluation, and planning. However, many action-conditioned world models predict future observations in 2D image or video space without explicitly modeling the underlying 3D scene, making spatial consistency across viewpoints and over long-horizon interactions challenging. We introduce 3D World Simulator, an action-conditioned multimodal world modeling framework that jointly predicts appearance, geometry, and tactile observations from a unified 3D representation. Our approach combines a hybrid particle-voxel autoencoder that compresses point clouds into compact 3D voxel latents with an action-conditioned latent dynamics model that predicts their evolution. The shared 3D representation supports geometrically consistent rendering from arbitrary viewpoints and flexible integration of spatially aligned tactile signals. Our experiments demonstrate interactive, long-horizon, multimodal prediction at 50 Hz. We then show how our model enables scalable data generation for policy training and evaluation using tactile and multi-view visual observations, including wrist-mounted and novel camera views. Furthermore, we scale our method to large datasets containing diverse objects and demonstrate model-based planning with unseen objects and robot configurations. Together, these results highlight the potential of hybrid particle-voxel dynamics as a foundation for multimodal 3D world simulation.


### [376] [CRONOS: Benchmarking Non-Episodic Reinforcement Learning for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05080)
*Po-Yi Wu, Djengo Cyun-Jyun Fang, Dian Cheng, Tsung-Wei Ke*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is promising for adapting robot policies to unstructured real-world environments. However, standard RL pipelines rely on episodic training with frequent scene resets, which is impractical for real-world deployment due to the need for substantial human intervention. While prior work has studied non-episodic RL in relatively small policies trained from scratch, its behavior when fine-tuning modern large-scale pre-trained vision-language-action (VLA) models remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce CRONOS, a simulation benchmark for RL fine-tuning of VLAs under non-episodic scene resets. CRONOS leverages high-fidelity physics simulators and adopts shared-scene multi-task settings. Using CRONOS, we show that naively fine-tuning pre-trained policies fails in non-episodic settings due to shifts in object configurations and end-effector poses; however, these challenges can be mitigated through targeted reset allocation and by addressing biases in pre-trained models. Moreover, we find that non-episodic training enhances long-horizon manipulation and improves generalization to previously unseen object configurations and task sequences. Project page: this https URL


### [377] [GSBR: Geometric Support Behavior Regularization for Online RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05295)
*Charles Xu, Jacob Berg, Liyiming Ke, Sergey Levine*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Regularizing online reinforcement learning (RL) toward a pretrained base policy can improve sample efficiency and learning stability, but regularization that is too restrictive limits how much the policy can improve. Base policies -- typically diffusion or flow-matching models trained by imitation learning on large, diverse datasets -- capture many behaviors that are reasonable but suboptimal. Existing regularizers using such base policies are too restrictive in one of two ways: behavioral cloning regularizers pull the actor toward the full base-policy distribution, penalizing it for discarding suboptimal modes, while hard support constraints forbid improvements that lie just outside the base policy's support. We propose Geometric Support Behavior Regularization (GSBR), which penalizes actions by their distance from the base policy's support. Actions within the support incur no penalty, so the actor can concentrate on high-value modes; outside the support, where value estimates are less reliable, the penalty grows smoothly with distance while still permitting improvement when the value gained outweighs the penalty. GSBR estimates this distance from a finite set of base-policy samples, so it requires only sample access to the base policy. Across 19 tasks on four robotic benchmarks, GSBR raises the average success rate from 33% for the base policy to 95%, achieving the highest average success rate and throughput among all compared methods, with about 23% higher normalized throughput than the next-best RL method. Project website and open-source code at this https URL .


### [378] [GUILD: Guided Iterative Data Collection for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05368)
*Shivin Dass, Kevin Nguyen, Rohan Yelandur, Junhong Xu, Roberto Martín-Martín*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Imitation learning policies in robotics are trained on datasets collected through costly real-world teleoperation. Often, the data-collection process is unguided, relying on the teleoperator's intuition, resulting in data that contributes little to overall policy performance. Recent work has shown that curating such unguided datasets can substantially improve policy performance, but these approaches operate by filtering existing datasets after collection and therefore do not prevent the acquisition of uninformative data. In this work, we propose GUILD, a novel Guided Data Collection (GDC) method that actively reasons about which data should be collected to maximize downstream policy performance. We introduce an iterative framework that alternates between 1) task proposal and data collection and 2) data evaluation to close the loop. For task proposal, we leverage semantic priors from vision-language models to identify relevant environments, object configurations, and goals for data collection. However, since semantic relevance does not imply policy improvement, we perform data evaluation. By leveraging predictive data attribution we approximate how the collected trajectories influence policy performance on a target deployment setting. This signal is then used as feedback to guide subsequent data collection, closing the data collection loop. We evaluate our approach in simulation and real robot settings, demonstrating that guided data collection substantially improves sample efficiency and deployment performance compared to passive data collection strategies, multiple baselines and experienced roboticists. More information at this https URL


### [379] [PolicyIK: High-Precision Inverse Kinematics for Robot Arms with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05372)
*Yuhan Liu, Xinqi Liu, Zixuan Dong, Keith W. Ross, Shenji Wan*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Inverse kinematics (IK) converts end-effector pose commands into joint motion, and many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on it to act on a given arm and to transfer across embodiments. Precise IK remains laborious: redundancy and singularities complicate the inverse mapping, execution depends on hand-tuned controllers, and training data on physical robots are costly to collect. We present PolicyIK, a goal-conditioned reinforcement learning policy for high-precision IK. It updates joint-position references from pose feedback and observes its accumulated reference relative to the measured joints, so it can retain the offset that the position controller needs at rest. Two-scale pose errors with an error-dependent action gain resolve millimeter residuals while preserving large motions. Progress and dwell rewards teach it to both reach the tolerance and stay within it. In simulation, a single attempt by the trained PPO policy keeps the mean errors over the final second within 1 mm and 0.2° on 93.5% of held-out tasks, and a deployment protocol raises success to 98.9%. When trained directly on a physical arm with an off-policy RL learner, PolicyIK achieves higher reaching and holding success than all four classical IK controllers under the same time budgets. In simulated pick-and-place, PolicyIK executes the end-effector commands of the $\pi_{0.5}$ VLA policy with 95.7% success. These results show that reinforcement learning can provide high-precision inverse kinematics for robot arms. Code is available at this https URL .

# math.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [380] [Learning Almost Complex and Complex Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03872)
*Edward Hirst, Tancredi Schettini Gherardini, Tomás S. R. Silva, Jakob Stein*

Main category: math.CV

Abstract: We study the numerical constructions of almost complex and complex structures on spheres and products of spheres up to real dimension six. Our general set-up parametrises almost complex structures via a global matrix field in an ambient Euclidean space, compressed by the tangent projector. The projector's algebraic residual measures the almost complex condition, while its Nijenhuis tensor is evaluated in the intrinsic norm of the round product metric. Polynomial features and neural networks are trained without prescribed targets to perform general searches for these structures. The almost complex search passes numerical admissibility checks on all nine examples that admit such structures and rejects $S^4$, where a topological obstruction is known. A matrix correction then enforces the almost complex identity, subject to a novel uniform admissibility condition. For integrability, a free polynomial representation with Levenberg-Marquardt optimisation learns the complex structures on $S^2\times S^2$, $S^3\times S^1$, $S^3\times S^3$ and $S^5\times S^1$, with the largest Nijenhuis norms found by adversarial search all below $10^{-9}$. These general searches were unsuccessful on $S^6$, $S^4\times S^2$ and $S^4\times T^2$. Therefore, for these three cases direct numerical reconstructions were built in torus-fibred coordinates. They are integrable by construction, satisfy their compatibility conditions to better than $10^{-11}$, and corroborate the recent constructions on $S^6$ and $S^4\times S^2$.


<div id='cs.OS'></div>

# cs.OS [[Back]](#toc)

### [381] [GHOST Driver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03649)
*Azhar Shaikh*

Main category: cs.OS
